Rexel · RXL-FR · the new Memo conveyor's card of 25 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Rexel

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 47,515 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

C6513th of 38

Memo topic 1, Tailwinds in the operating environment: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Rexel's external environment has moved from a headwind (2023-24) to a moderate net tailwind (2025-26). The tailwind is narrower and more price-dependent than headline growth suggests: about half of H1 2026 organic growth was price, about a quarter data centres, and core construction volume was roughly flat. The model should treat 2026 as largely secured, with H2 boosted by copper above $14,000/t and data centres, and should treat 2027 organic growth as the key uncertainty, with a central case of about 3-5% and a wide cone (about 0-7%). That depends on whether European construction (indicators still contracting) replaces fading copper and data-centre comparisons. Company-level macro (France, dollar translation, rates) is the main reason the external picture helps earnings more than it helps the share price.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0248C-0566C-0735C-0958C-3564C-3955
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

B488th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: broadly stable and rational in the core, slightly rising in the high-growth pockets (US data-centre tenders, European digital capability), and benign on pricing while product inflation lasts. No sign of a price war; the risk sits in large-project bidding and in Sonepar's digital catch-up.

  • Stable core. Rexel, Sonepar and Wesco run "identical consolidation strategies". Scale players show constructive pricing discipline. Expert calls describe Rexel as "highly rigid and disciplined" on margin while rivals discount to win project volume. Non-cable pricing returned to +1% to +2% in Europe after two years of deflation. Legrand's CEO says Rexel has been "massively gaining market share in France"; Rexel's CEO calls shares "relatively stable" and stresses selectivity. Read: Rexel prices for margin, not share.
  • Rising intensity in the growth pockets. US data-centre tenders are "highly competitive" (Jefferies). Wesco's data-centre business is about four times Rexel's ($4-5bn run-rate, 23% of Wesco revenue) and it leads in white space. Hyperscale projects dilute gross margin (-5bp in H1). GCG is Rexel's answer, and Bernstein's counter-hypothesis is that bidding dilutes GCG's 11-13% margin.
  • Digital catch-up. Sonepar's Spark platform and AutoStore roll-out, and an expert saying "rivals rapidly closing gaps on B2B e-procurement", suggest Rexel's digital lead is narrowing. The Morgan Stanley survey shows customers rank availability (63%) and delivery (61%) far above digital ordering (14%), so the digital gap matters more for cost than for share.
  • Local aggression. Fragmented independents price aggressively to defend municipal relationships. Graybar's gross margin fell 20.1% to 19.3% in 2025 on "competitive pricing pressure". Nordic consolidation (Solar Norge buying Sonepar Norge) intensifies Rexel's residual Nordic market (Sweden, after the Finland exit).
  • Direction for the next 6-12 months. In a nominal-growth environment (copper, tariffs and supplier price rises: 54% of manufacturers expect higher prices) competitors share the inflation and intensity stays low. The danger is a volume downturn combined with deflation, as in 2024 (solar and PVC collapses of up to 70%). Then local independents and Sonepar would compete harder, as the 2024 margin fall of about 90bp shows.

For the model: no deterioration in core gross margin from competition in the base case. Apply 5-10bp a year of gross-margin dilution from large-project mix in North America while data-centre share rises, partly offset by GCG's higher-margin mix.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

A661st of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: a net tailwind now, and an unusually strong one, because a distributor's "input cost" is its product cost and product inflation lifts nominal gross profit on a fixed cost base. But the tailwind is concentrated in copper and tariffs, which can reverse, and the operating-cost side (wages 2.5%, fuel and freight with Brent at $111) is a steady headwind that has nearly matched pricing (spread only +6bp).

  • Product prices (tailwind). Copper was $13,408/t in Q2 2026 (+42% year on year) and above $14,000 in Q3, against guidance set at $11,000. Jefferies: each $1,000/t adds about 100bp of organic growth and 10-15bp of EBITA margin. Cable price contributed +1.7% (Q1) and +2.2% (Q2). Non-cable prices are back at +1% to +2% in Europe; supplier price rises continue (Morgan Stanley: 54% of manufacturers expect higher prices; Schneider pricing 4.2% in Q2; Legrand never cuts average prices and carries a 3-6 month lag). Rexel passes supplier rises of 3-5% through.
  • Operating costs (headwind). Opex inflation is 2.5% (wages, benefits, incentives in North America), and transport and energy are about 1% of sales (fuel surcharges imposed). Price minus opex inflation was +6bp in H1; it was negative in 2024 and 2025 (-19bp cited). The thinness of this spread is the key fragility: with copper flat, the spread rests on non-cable pricing of about 1.5% against 2.5% wage inflation on a base where personnel and delivery are 68% of opex. That gap must be closed by productivity (4% in H1).
  • Availability. Mixed but net positive for scale distributors. Transformer and medium-voltage switchgear lead times run 60-200 weeks; utilities sign framework agreements with "trusted incumbents"; buyers pre-book and consolidate vendors (a steel maker raised Rexel-related spend up to 16%). The negatives were Rexel's own early-2026 availability gaps (Legrand software migration, halogen-free cables in France), which pushed customers to Wesco and independents temporarily.
  • Supplier concentration is stable and moderate: top five suppliers 25% of purchases, 50% of purchases from 28 suppliers. Schneider and Legrand hold specification power, and Rexel is about 5% of Legrand's sales. Supplier margins of 18-25% EBIT against Rexel's 6% show where power sits.
  • Working-capital side effect. Inflation raises inventory and receivables in euros; trade working capital went from 15.0% to 16.3-16.7% of sales. The tailwind is paid for partly in cash.

For the model: treat copper as an explicit input. Cable price contribution about +2% for H2 2026, fading to about +1% in H1 2027 and about 0% in H2 2027 at flat copper. Non-cable price +1.5%. Opex inflation 2.5-3%. Productivity offset of 2-3% a year.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

F3528th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: macro is a net headwind, and it bites hardest at company level through three Rexel-specific channels: (1) French exposure, through sovereign spread, tax and pre-election investment deferral; (2) unhedged dollar translation; (3) the rate-sensitivity of residential and new-build construction. Energy-price inflation is the one macro force that helps, by lifting nominal prices and European energy-independence demand.

  • Rates (sector and company). US 10-year near 5.10%, Fed still hiking in December, ECB deposit rate projected at 2.75% by end-2026 after further hikes, mortgage rates around 7.1-7.3%. This keeps residential (24% of sales) and new-build depressed and defers European building recovery into 2027 or later. It also compresses the multiple: the discount rate is "every asset's fair-value denominator".
  • Energy and inflation (helps). Brent at $111 and the Strait of Hormuz disruption accelerate European demand for solar, heat pumps and EV charging (energy-transition categories +15%) and feed product price inflation. The cost is fuel and freight surcharges and slower rate cuts.
  • Currency (company level, material). 61% of revenue is outside the euro; the US dollar is 38% of sales. A 5% currency move changes sales by €507.4m and EBITA by €32.2m (about 2.5% of EBITA, about 4% of EPS). Translation cost -1.6% of sales in 2025 (-€26.7m EBITA) and -2.6% in H1 2026 (-4.3% in Q1). Translation is structurally unhedged. With GCG, US dollar exposure rises above half of sales. Consensus has the dollar strengthening into mid-2027 (Morgan Stanley; range 1.10-1.19), which would turn translation into a modest tailwind, but this is a forecast, not a hedge.
  • France (company level). The FfF digest carries a Double Underweight on France; the French corporate tax surcharge lifts the group tax rate to 30%; the French services and construction indices are near lows; election risk is in April 2027. France is about 20% of Rexel's revenue and a disproportionate share of European profit (core countries above 30% share and "historically high-margin").
  • Risk-off regime. Defensives lead cyclicals on every window; the Momentum tilt moved to Underweight; electrical and climate wholesalers fell 6% over three months, the only falling cohort in the sector. A geared, cyclical, French-listed distributor sits on the wrong side of the Fragility (Double Underweight) and Country tilts.
  • Which level bites hardest. Industry level (capital goods) is neutral, with the data-centre wave a positive. Sector level (distributors) is mixed. Company level is negative, because Rexel uniquely combines France, unhedged dollar translation, high construction exposure and recently raised leverage.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–58

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: three further dynamics matter. (1) Takeover interest sets a soft floor and an option on the share price (positive). (2) Labour scarcity at installers caps the conversion of backlogs into sales (negative). (3) Channel consolidation, with large customers concentrating spend on national tier-one distributors, favours Rexel against independents but strengthens buyers' bargaining (mixed, net positive).

  • Takeover floor and option. QXO's unsolicited €28-28.40 a share (€8.5bn) bid was rejected unanimously, with Cevian (23%) supporting the board. CVC was reportedly in talks with Cevian's support, and advisers were retained. Options imply a 16.8% probability of the €25 bear price, down from 23.7%. The price of €32.63 is 15% above the QXO bid. The floor is soft (a bid can set a ceiling as well as a floor), but private-market interest limits downside below about €28 in a non-recessionary setting.
  • Installer labour bottleneck. Electricians are concentrated on hyperscale projects, delaying traditional commercial and residential jobs (Morgan Stanley). If contractor capacity, rather than demand, is the binding constraint, record backlogs convert slowly and the core volume recovery is delayed even when demand returns. This argues for gradual, not V-shaped, European volume recovery.
  • Customer consolidation and vendor rationalisation. National accounts consolidate onto Rexel, Sonepar and Wesco (the only three able to serve Fortune 500 multi-site contracts), and supply reliability matters more than price. The flip side is PE roll-ups of contractors negotiating harder and sometimes direct with OEMs (a 2-5 year medium-credibility threat).
  • Equity market dynamic. Rexel fell 11.8% over three months after the €500m placing and trades 6% below the €34.75 issue price, now a technical resistance. The FfF Momentum tilt moved to Underweight. These are flows, not fundamentals, but they set the near-term path.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

A643rd of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: low threat overall. Adjacent entrants (home-improvement and building-products distributors such as Home Depot and QXO) are buying into specialty distribution in the US, a medium-term structural threat mainly in residential and HVAC. Standard entrants are blocked by scale, availability and technical prequalification. Orthogonal entrants (Amazon Business, AI procurement platforms) threaten only simple consumables.

  • Adjacent: Home Depot (SRS at 16.1x EBITDA, Mingledorff's) and QXO (TopBuild at $17bn, and its bid for Rexel) show capital moving into specialty distribution. More a valuation support (acquirer interest) than an operating threat to electrical distribution for 2026-28.
  • Standard entrants: high barriers (branch density, inventory availability, supplier rights, prequalification for grid and data-centre projects).
  • Orthogonal: Amazon Business wins where customers already hold exact product codes. The AI "democratisation" risk (smaller rivals buying inventory and pricing tools cheaply) is the most plausible longer-term erosion of Rexel's digital edge.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

C+5512th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: currently a support. Suppliers are raising prices (supporting nominal gross profit), allocating scarce product to trusted scale distributors, and relying on distributors for most of their flow. The risk is direct selling to large accounts and the supplier-distributor pricing-coordination issue that produced the antitrust fines (Schneider €207m, Rexel €124m, Legrand €43m, Sonepar), which constrains how pricing can be coordinated in future.

  • Concentration: top five suppliers 25% of purchases; 50% of purchases from 28 suppliers; 221 key suppliers make up 75% of spend; 7,092 suppliers in all. Rexel is one of Legrand's two largest distributors; Schneider named Nedco an "Alliance Master" automation distributor.
  • Behaviour: supplier pricing intentions "robust but recently stabilised"; the supplier-distributor pricing skew has narrowed by 9bp (Morgan Stanley); Legrand's 2026 software migration disrupted availability; Section 232/301 tariffs push suppliers to restructure supply chains, which complicates restocking.
  • Payables: payable days fell from 50.5 to 47.9, a small cash headwind (suppliers tightening terms).
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0158C-0360C-0445C-0865C-3255C-3747C-4356
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

D5819th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: demand has turned from contraction to moderate growth over the last six months, but the improvement is narrow. About one-third of it is structural (US data centres, European electrification, grid), about half is price (much of it copper), and core construction volume, two-thirds of sales, is still flat to down. Visibility is short for the core (3-4 months) and genuinely multi-quarter only for the data-centre and project pockets.

  • What changed. Same-day sales went from -2.4% (2024) to +2.5% (2025), +3.4% (Q1 2026) and +6.7% (Q2 2026). Six consecutive quarters of sequential improvement, and Q2 volume of +3.1% was the first all-region volume growth since Q2 2023. The Q3 headlines ("significant acceleration") say the trend continued.
  • Decomposition: the critical insight. US sales are about 38% of the group (46% × 83%). Data centres were about 4.5-5% of US sales a year earlier and grew 80% in H1 and more than 100% in Q2. That adds roughly 1.4 percentage points to H1 group organic growth and about 1.8 points in Q2. H1 organic growth of 5.1% minus 3.3% price minus about 1.4 points of data centres leaves underlying non-data-centre volume of roughly zero to +0.5%. Morgan Stanley and the CEO say the same in words: excluding data centres and industrial automation, US construction is "slightly negative".
  • Structural pieces. US data centres are 9% of US sales (Q2) and about 13% with broadband (Q3 release), with a 25% sequential backlog increase and 100 active projects with none delayed (Q1). European energy-transition categories (HVAC, solar, EV charging) are 22% of European sales, growing about 15% in Q2, helped by heat waves and energy-independence buying after the Middle East conflict and Brent at $111. US industrial automation is recovering: +3% in Q1, +15% in Q2, about +20% in Q3, helped by reshoring and the Revere acquisition. These are real but not yet two-plus years visible, apart from grid and data-centre backlog with 18-24 months of hyperscaler order-book visibility (per Schneider).
  • Cyclical pieces. Eurozone construction PMI was 43.4 in September 2026, the seventh month of contraction. France's construction PMI was 39.8, with residential at 37.5 and permits down 12% in July. Germany's PMI was 43.5. UK starts were down 29% (Glenigan) and Rexel UK & Ireland -3.8%. The US 30-year mortgage rate is 7.1-7.3%. European building recovery is deferred into 2027 by every broker. With the US 10-year at 5.10% and the Fed still hiking (FfF digest), a near-term rate-led housing recovery is unlikely.
  • Visibility. North American backlog is 2.6-2.8 months of sales. The risk report's re-run trigger is backlog below 3 months, which it already is, so that trigger is badly specified. Lead times are 3-4 months. Visibility for 60-70% of sales is one quarter.
  • Skew for the next 12 months. Price is the most fragile leg: copper above $14,000/t against guidance built on $11,000 can reverse. The data-centre leg faces a financing wobble (the FfF digest says the AI theme is "cracking at its financing, not its demand"), and comparisons double in 2027. The European leg has upside optionality. The cone is wide: plausible 2027 organic outcomes run from about 0% to +7%.

For the model: forecast organic growth as four legs: (i) core construction volume at about -1% to +1% in 2027; (ii) data centres decelerating from about 80-100% to 20-40% growth on a base of about 2.5-3% of group sales; (iii) non-cable price +1% to +2%; (iv) cable price that fades to zero, or turns negative, as copper comparisons lap from Q3 2027.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

B606th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: three disruption forces act on Rexel. Electrification is strengthening and is a net tailwind. Digital and AI is being turned by Rexel into a cost advantage rather than a threat. Direct sourcing and modular prefabrication by hyperscalers and OEMs is strengthening at the top end but is contained to the large-project layer. The net effect is mildly positive for the next 18 months and an open question for 2028 onwards.

  • Electrification (tailwind, strengthening). 22% of European sales in energy-transition categories growing about 15%. The US data-centre vertical moved from 5% (early 2025) to 9% (Q2 2026) of US sales. Grid end-of-life replacement covers more than 40% of grids. Electricity's share of final energy is forecast to rise from 23% to 30% by 2050. GCG raises exposure to grid, defence and white space. This disruption acts for a distributor of the physical bill of materials.
  • Digital/AI (turned to advantage). Digital penetration was 33.7% (H1 2025), 35.3% (H1 2026) and 36% (Q3 2026). North America moved from 27% to 31% in a quarter (+604bp year on year). Revalgo/RexelGPT AI order intake cut processing "from hours to seconds"; 65% of US inside sales and 70% of French service-centre staff use it; AI quoting gives 10-15% inside-sales productivity. Amazon Business threatens only simple consumables. The sector digest's arithmetic: removing costs worth 1% of sales lifts operating profit about 16% at a 6.2% margin, so thin-margin distributors gain most. The Wisdom Library warns "AI claims are operating basics, not moats": rivals will buy the same tools within two to three years, so this is a 2026-2028 margin window, not a permanent moat.
  • Direct sourcing and modularity (strengthening, contained). Hyperscalers buy transformers and switchgear direct from OEMs and limit distributor spend to 35-40% of the electrical bill of materials. Prefabricated modular builds are projected to go from 20% to 60% of builds by 2030. Lennox-style direct models and PE roll-ups of contractors also act here. The disconfirming evidence is strong: standard wire, cable and conduit "flow almost exclusively through scale players", 100 data-centre projects showed no delays, and procurement experts say execution certainty favours scaled distributors. 800V DC is a supplier-level issue (Legrand, Schneider), not a distributor one; it changes what Rexel sells, not whether.
  • Insight. Rexel's data-centre position is grey-space and cable-heavy (wires, cables and bus ducts 70% of US data-centre scope, cables 50%). That is the layer least exposed to direct sourcing but most exposed to copper and to competitive tendering. GCG moves Rexel into kitted, engineered white-space products (prefabricated whips giving "ten times faster power-up"), where the distributor adds labour-saving value that is harder to disintermediate.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

F4529th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: on demand, regulation is a modest, durable positive. On profit and valuation, French politics is a clear and rising negative through tax, sovereign risk premium and investment deferral. The two roughly offset for the next 12 months, with French risk the swing factor around the April 2027 election.

  • Positives. France plans to double electrification support to about €10bn a year (heat pumps, EV charging), anchoring Rexel's most profitable country. The EU Energy Performance of Buildings Directive requires national renovation plans by December 2026. Germany's Building Modernisation Act (29 July 2026) doubles eligible insulation costs to €60,000 with a 5% multi-family bonus from 2027. The EU AFIR requires 600kW corridor charging by 2027. US: IRA funding and NEVI ($7.5bn). US tariffs (Section 232 on steel and aluminium cores at 50%; Section 301 duties of 10-12.5% on 60 trading partners) add nominal price inflation that Rexel passes through ("extensive local stock allows effective pass-through" — Morgan Stanley). US executive order EO 14420, with implementing rules due 24 December 2026, restricts Chinese grid equipment and favours Western supply chains through distributors.
  • Negatives. (i) The French corporate tax surcharge lifts Rexel's group rate to about 30% from 27% (FY2025 reported 30.2%). (ii) French sovereign stress: OAT-Bund spread past 120bp, "decade highs", spilling into Italy. The FfF digest carries a Double Underweight on France. The Wisdom Library entry "sovereign stress hits a group through its risk premium before its earnings" applies: French-listed Rexel gets de-rated even though France is only about 20% of revenue. (iii) Clients defer industrial investment ahead of the 2027 presidential election (Geography digest). (iv) Antitrust: the €124m fine was paid April 2025 and is under appeal; a parallel magistrate investigation holds a €48m cash guarantee, so a second cash outflow is possible but bounded.
  • Subsidy volatility is a recurring risk: the 2023-24 solar subsidy cuts and price deflation drove the 2024 guidance downgrade. The UK postponing boiler bans to 2035 weakens heat-pump demand.
  • Tax as a model input. If the surcharge is a 2025-2026 exceptional measure, the tax rate falls back to about 27% in 2027 and adds about 4% to EPS. Consensus appears to assume part of this: consensus adjusted net income over pre-tax profit rises from 72.7% in FY2026 to 76.9% in FY2027. That could instead be amortisation of GCG acquired intangibles added back. The second-set Analyst must establish which.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

D6522nd of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: yes, the data-centre power build-out and European electrification are accelerating Rexel's demand now and are its main growth sources for 2026. But Rexel captures a smaller share of the wave than Wesco, Schneider or Legrand, and the contribution will roughly halve in 2027 as comparisons rise, unless GCG and grid and infrastructure take over.

  • Size of the wave. Data-centre power demand goes from 70GW (2024) to 180-220GW by 2030; T&D captures a $1.45tn capex cycle to 2030; content is $2.5-3.3m per MW. Peers show the force: Wesco data-centre revenue +45% to $1.5bn a quarter; Legrand data-centre organic above 30% and 32% of sales; Schneider data-centre orders up triple digits; Sonepar $1.7bn of data-centre sales.
  • Rexel's capture. US data-centre sales +50% in 2025, +80% in H1 2026 and above 100% in Q2; 9% of US sales (Q2), about 5% of group sales. Guidance for 2026 data-centre growth was raised from 20% to above 50%. Canada data centres are 10% of regional sales. GCG lifts US data-centre exposure from 8% to 10% and adds white space (21% of GCG sales), utilities (18%) and defence (11%).
  • Relative capture is lower. Rexel's 2026 organic guidance of about 5% compares with Wesco's 10-12% revenue growth, Schneider's 10-13% and Legrand's 8-10%. Rexel's group exposure (about 5%) is far below Wesco's 23% or Legrand's 32%. The sector digest's framing applies: the channel gains volume and mix, not scarcity price, and is re-rated in the multiple if at all.
  • Electrification in Europe. It is accelerating with heat waves and energy-security buying, but subsidy-dependent and volatile (2024 solar deflation). The French €10bn plan is the anchor.
  • Arithmetic for 2027. If US data centres grow 30% in 2027 on about 3.5% of group sales, the contribution falls from about 1.5-1.8 points (2026) to about 1 point. GCG, organically growing about 11% a year since 2019, adds about 0.5 points of organic growth once in the base, from 2028.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

D5524th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: customer demand supports the "self-help plus secular pockets" thesis but not a "broad cyclical recovery" thesis. Data-centre, utility, industrial automation and energy-transition customers are buying more and earlier. General contractors and residential customers are flat to down and price-sensitive. The support is moderately fragile because it rests on a few concentrated growth verticals and on customers' willingness to pre-buy amid supply tightness.

  • Positive behaviour. Utilities: European spending up 5-6% a year; German utilities raised Rexel spend by 6% organically. Vendor consolidation and framework agreements. Pre-booking power equipment. Steel and industrial buyers concentrating with Rexel (up to +16%). Hyperscale customers: 100 active projects, none delayed. Customers in energy transition hedge energy costs rather than chase subsidies.
  • Negative behaviour. General commercial installers face labour constraints and rising project costs (US ex-data centres "slightly negative"). Residential buyers are frozen by 7%+ mortgages and NAHB confidence at its lowest since 2012. UK and German new-build has stalled. Large buyers shift 5-10% of spend on commodity lines through benchmarking. Hyperscalers buy big equipment direct.
  • Fragility. If AI capex financing cracks (FfF: neocloud credit widening) or copper prices fall, the two most visible supports fade together.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

D4726th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: they help demand (electrification subsidies, building codes, EV mandates, grid security rules) and hurt profit and valuation (French tax, antitrust, sovereign risk). Fragility is moderate: subsidies are changeable (solar 2023-24, UK boiler ban delay, US incentive rollbacks) and French politics is volatile into April 2027. (Detail in C-04.)

  • Watch: the French budget for 2027 (surcharge renewal); the antitrust appeal ruling; the magistrate's investigation; US implementing rules under EO 14420 (24 December 2026); the size of the French €10bn plan in the 2027 budget.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F5629th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the market is very large relative to Rexel (global electrical distribution about €484bn in 2024, so Rexel is about 4%). The realistic growth it allows is a nominal 3-5% organic a year mid-term (structural mid-single-digit market growth in the electrification and data-centre-heavy US, low single digit in European construction), plus 2-3% from acquisitions. That is consistent with Axelerate's 5-8% total sales growth, and the TAM is not the constraint.

  • Sizing and growth signals: €484bn (2024) to €511bn (2029), an implied 1.1% CAGR (the source's figure, which looks like a real or volume figure, against other sources calling the sector "structural mid-single-digit growth"). US utility capex grew 7% a year over 2015-25. Data-centre power demand grows 15-22% a year to 2030. European electricity's share of the energy mix rises from 23% to 32% by 2030 (Sonepar and Legrand).
  • Inconsistency to resolve: the US share data (Rexel 7%, Sonepar USA 25% at $15.2bn) imply a US market of about $60bn, but Rexel's US sales (about $8.5bn) at 7% would imply about $120bn. The second-set Analyst should not build bottom-up US share models on these figures.
  • What it allows: in the 17 countries where Rexel operates, organic growth of 3-5% nominal a year with a construction cycle that is neither boom nor bust; 5-7% in a European recovery; 0-2% in a downturn with deflation. Acquisitions add a further 2-3% a year, but only once leverage returns to about 2x (2028).

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0670C-1058
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

B+704th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Rexel's own cycle has clearly turned up, from a trough in mid-2024 to an early upswing in 2026, about two to three quarters ahead of its European end markets. The turn is led by price and North America, and is now broadening into European volume. Rexel is about 12-18 months into a recovery whose next leg needs European construction, which no indicator yet shows.

  • Trough and turn. 2024 same-day sales were -2.4% with volume -1.5%, and margin fell 90bp to 5.9% (October 2024 guidance cut). Volume was +1.2% in 2025, and same-day sales improved for six consecutive quarters into Q2 2026. Q1 2026 had all three regions positive for the first time in 11 quarters. Q2 had volume +3.1% in every region. Q3 2026 brought "significant acceleration" and a second guidance upgrade.
  • Regional phase. North America is mid-upswing (+7.0% in 2025, +7.9% in Q4 2025, +5.8% in Q1 2026 with weather drag, +7.8% in Q2 2026), but its breadth is narrow (data centres, automation, Canada +9.1%). Europe is at the start of an upswing (-1.1% in 2025, 0% in Q4 2025, +0.6% in Q1 2026, +4.4% in Q2 2026), helped by heat-wave HVAC, solar in Belgium and Austria, and French share gains (+6.3%). It is not yet supported by construction indicators: the Eurozone construction PMI has contracted for seven months. Asia-Pacific is volatile (+11.4% in Q1 on Australian solar and battery subsidies).
  • What is turning versus what is echoing. On the Wisdom Library's "use, refill or price" test, which uses Rexel as its worked example: price (cable and non-cable, about 3.3-3.6 points) is the largest single leg and reverses when copper falls. "Use" is real in data centres and electrification. Some "refill" is likely: customers pre-booking scarce equipment, and the Middle East shipping delays pushing Q3 procurement into Q4 (channel check). Customer inventory commentary is not available; this is a research gap.
  • How far it can run. Rexel's volumes are "barely above 2019 levels" (Morgan Stanley) and FY2025 revenue (€19.4bn) is only 3.8% above FY2022 (€18.7bn) despite acquisitions. Unlike a late-cycle peak, the volume base is depressed, so a European recovery in 2027-28 would add to growth rather than reverse it. That is the bull asymmetry.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

C-5817th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: moderate tailwinds (data centres, electrification, product inflation, consolidation) slightly outweigh moderate-to-strong headwinds (rates and construction, France, dollar translation, risk-off). The tailwinds are more cyclical-commodity in character than they look (copper is a large part), and the headwinds are concentrated at company level. Net: mildly positive for 2026-H1 2027 earnings, neutral to mildly negative for the multiple.

Internal change forces/risks

Memo topic 2, Internal change forces: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The internal change picture is the stronger half of Rexel's case. A stable, credible CEO-CFO pair is executing a well-articulated self-help and portfolio-upgrade plan, guidance has been raised twice from a sandbagged base, productivity is at a record, and the strategic step (GCG) directly targets the business's diagnosed weakness (white-space and specialty under-indexing). The price is a temporarily weaker capital-return profile: leverage near 2.3-2.4x, 4.6% dilution, buybacks paused, flat dividend. For the model: assume margin progress of 30-40bp a year in 2027 and 2028 from self-help plus GCG; no buybacks before 2028; dividend growth resuming in line with consensus (€1.30 for 2027); and a 2027 share count about 4% above 2026's average.

Group judgement

Risks

C4713th of 38

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Rexel's risks are cyclical and macro rather than existential. The bear case is a correlated combination (copper deflation, data-centre pause, European construction double-dip, French stress) that would cut 2027 EBITA 20-30% and the multiple to about 7-8x, giving €18-25. The near-term fundamental engine is not decelerating, but its inputs (copper, data-centre growth rates, supplier pricing) are near peak rates of change. Sentiment and positioning risks are elevated now (risk-off, France, placing overhang, technical weakness). Downside to a fair trough is about 25% in a plausible bad case, against about 28% upside to the consensus target. The skew is roughly balanced, with the downside path faster.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

B+686th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: clearly improving and measurable. Productivity, digital adoption, turnaround progress and working-capital discipline all moved the right way in the last 6-12 months. Governance is solid, with one legacy blemish (antitrust) and one design gap (no return-on-capital metric in long-term pay).

  • Cost discipline. Record productivity of 4% in H1 2026 (against 2.5% opex inflation). Headcount -2.3% in 2025 to 26,601 (Europe -4%, -600 FTE). €40m of productivity savings. Restructuring costs down to €2.7m in H1 2026 from €11.4m. Action plans contributed +22bp of margin.
  • Execution metrics. Digital share 34% (2025), 35.3% (H1 2026), 36% (Q3), with North America +604bp year on year. AI order entry in use by more than 65% of US inside sales; RexelGPT passed 1 million interactions. Inventory days 58.3 (2024), 56.7 (2025), 56.5 (June 2026). Germany and UK margins up more than 100bp in H1 2026.
  • Trough performance. The 2024-25 margin floor was 5.9-6.0% against about 4% in earlier downturns (2019 5.0%; earlier troughs about 4.2%). The 200bp floor lift is the best evidence of better standards, though part of it is mix (North America growth) and digital.
  • Governance. 78% independent board, 80% independent audit committee, clean related-party record, short-term bonus 40% gross margin / 40% EBITA margin / 20% working capital, which aligns with cash discipline. Weaknesses: the €124m vertical price-fixing fine (practice pre-dates 2018 searches; training since strengthened) and no ROCE metric in the long-term plan, a gap that matters more after a $1.4bn acquisition.
  • Execution slips. Early-2026 product availability problems (Legrand software, halogen-free cables) lost some orders to Wesco and independents. North American margins were flat in Q1 2026 on opex inflation, AI investment and data-centre mix.
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

B447th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the negatives are real but contained: (1) capital returns are suspended and equity diluted for a large deal at a full pre-synergy price; (2) integration and management bandwidth are stretched (GCG plus four bolt-ons in 2026 plus two country turnarounds); (3) dependence on data centres and copper for growth makes 2026 an over-earned base for some lines; (4) legal tail (the €48m guarantee); (5) thin insider alignment.

  • Dilution and returns. 4.6% more shares at €34.75; buybacks paused (consensus assumes none in 2027); dividend flat since 2022. Per-share growth will lag group growth by about 4 points in 2027.
  • Bandwidth. In 2026: Revere ($330m), Techno-Contact 360, DEE, GCG (about $1.1bn), plus the UK and Germany restructurings and an expanded European cluster under one leader (Moreau). Succession readiness slipped from 83% to 80%. The latest Earnings Review notes "simultaneous scaling of multiple high-value specialty acquisitions" as an aggregate weak signal.
  • Over-earning risk. H2 2026 gross profit benefits from copper above $14,000/t; the non-recurring copper gain (+€20.7m in H1, excluded from adjusted EBITA) shows the scale of the inventory effect. Data-centre growth above 100% is not repeatable. Consensus 2027 EPS (+18.7%) depends on these not reversing.
  • Legal. Antitrust appeal pending (the €124m fine was paid and booked as receivable and provision). The magistrate's parallel investigation holds a €48m cash guarantee. A further outflow of €48m or more is plausible.
  • Alignment. The CEO holds 110,700 shares (below 0.1% of the float), sold 13,500 shares in September 2025, and no executive has bought in the open market in 2026. The long-term plan has no ROCE metric, a gap when M&A intensity rises. Boston Partners voted against a 10.06% capital resolution.
  • Taxes. The French surcharge (30% group rate) may persist beyond 2026 under fiscal pressure.
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

C+6012th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement: reasonably congruent: the bonus (40% gross margin, 40% EBITA margin, 20% working capital) matches the strategy of margin and cash discipline, AI adoption is in incentives, and engagement is high (83% engagement, 88% pride). Congruence is weaker between the centralised European model and the decentralised, entrepreneurial US project market, which experts flag as a friction for integrating acquisitions like GCG. Not at a Lifco or Ryanair level of system congruence.

  • Frictions. Centralisation versus local relationships (former Rexel USA director; Sonepar and Wesco experts); multi-brand complexity historically slowed decisions; workforce ageing (37% over 50; under-30s down to 14%).

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

F3829th of 38

Layer 3 · Risks (the third set)

Judgement: concentrated, and more concentrated in 2027 than in any other forecast year. Three drivers (GCG consolidation, the copper price level and the data-centre build-out) generate roughly 70% of the difference between 2027 and the 2022-2025 plateau on revenue and about 75% of the margin step; a fourth (the French tax surcharge) and a fifth (the post-placing share count) govern the EPS line below operating profit. Against the last four to six years, where one driver (inflation) explained almost everything in 2019-22 and its reversal explained 2023-24, the forecast is less dependent on a single external price but more dependent on two items with no history inside Rexel (GCG, hyperscale data centres) and on one political decision (the Finance Bill). The drivers are not independent: GCG, data centres and copper all sit on the AI-capex and electrification trade, so the effective concentration is higher than a count of five suggests. From 2028 the drivers become more diffuse (self-help, European volume, bolt-ons, buybacks), which is better for robustness but worse for visibility.

  • The revenue difference (FY2025 €19,415m to FY2027 €22,970m, +€3,555m), attributed. GCG about €1,050m (30%); price, cable and non-cable, over the two years about €1,100m (31%; cable price alone about €500m, almost all in 2026); volume about €1,300m (37%), of which US data centres about €450m (13% of the total) and all other volume about €850m; 2026 bolt-ons and the Finland disposal net about €350m (10%); currency about -€150m (-4%); rounding the rest. So two drivers (GCG and price) are 60% of the revenue step and the single largest "organic" driver, data centres, is a third of the volume leg.

  • The margin difference (6.0% to 6.80%, +80bp), attributed. Copper level effect and the price-over-cost spread in 2026 about +38bp (47% of the gross positives); volume leverage about +35bp (2026 +18bp, 2027 +17bp); self-help net of wage inflation about +25bp; GCG mix about +22bp; offsets of about -40bp (digital investment -20bp, large-project dilution -10bp, the 2027 price-over-cost spread -7bp, rounding). Two drivers (copper and GCG) are 60bp of the 120bp of gross positives; a third (volume) is 35bp; the many-small-items category (self-help) is 25bp.

  • The EPS difference (brokers' basis €2.19 to €2.77 in 2027, +26%; model basis to €2.97). EBITA +34% is reduced by: net financial items +€47m (-4 points), tax rate held at about 29% rather than falling to 27% (-3 points against what consensus assumes), the share count +5% (-5 points), and GCG PPA amortisation (-7 points on the brokers' basis). Below operating profit the drivers are concentrated on two decisions: the French Parliament's vote on the surcharge extension (final vote expected in December 2026) and the board's decision on when to restart buybacks (tied to 2.0x leverage).

  • Comparison with the last four to six years. 2019-2022: revenue from €13.7bn to €18.7bn and margin from 5.0% to 7.3%; about 60% of the revenue step and nearly all of the margin step was inflation (copper, supplier prices, the 2021 inventory gain of €57.8m), with Mayer and bolt-ons providing most of the rest. 2023-2025: revenue flat (+1.2% a year), margin down 130bp then flat, with solar and PVC deflation, European construction and the 2024 guidance cut as the drivers. In other words one external price variable dominated both the up-leg and the down-leg. The 2026-2028 forecast swaps that single dependence for a portfolio of four: copper (still about a third of the 2026 improvement), an acquisition, a secular end market and self-help. That is a genuine diversification of drivers, but with two caveats. First, the new drivers are correlated: hyperscaler capex finances both the data-centre volume and the demand for GCG's engineered products, and the AI trade has also lifted copper; a financing crack in AI capex would hit all three at once (the Fragility digest the first set cited: the theme is "cracking at its financing, not its demand"; Deutsche Bank on 26 October 2026 flags hyperscaler and neocloud credit metrics; New York paused permitting for data centres above 50MW and Illinois suspended incentives in 2026). Second, GCG and hyperscale data centres have no track record inside Rexel's numbers, so the Consumer cannot calibrate their behaviour from Rexel's history as it can calibrate copper from 2011-12, 2015, 2021 and 2022.

  • How the concentration evolves. 2026: concentrated on copper, data-centre volume and pass-through (three external items), all now largely delivered. 2027: concentrated on GCG (mechanical once closed), the copper level (external, mean-reverting) and the data-centre comparison; the tax vote and share count below the line. 2028-2030: diffuse: UK and Germany, GCG synergies, European construction (French starts +31% in September but permits flat; German permits +1.9%; EPBD transposition missed by every member state; the German KfW subsidy cap steps down from February 2027), resumed bolt-ons, buybacks, a lower tax rate. Diffuse drivers reduce the chance of one event breaking the forecast but also mean that no single data point will confirm it.

  • For the Consumer. The 2027 forecast can be monitored with four numbers: the GCG closing date and its first-half margin, the LME copper price against $13,700/t, the US data-centre share of sales and the backlog in months, and the final text of the 2027 Finance Bill. Three of the four will be known by the 10 February 2027 results. That is the positive side of concentration: it is trackable.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

B688th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the strategy has not changed direction, but its centre of gravity has shifted decisively in the last six months. Axelerate 2028's "value-added, North America, digital, self-help" priorities were turned from aspiration into capital commitment with GCG ($1.4bn), the largest deal in over 15 years. It is well articulated and is being resourced at the cost of buybacks and €500m of equity.

  • The plan. Axelerate 2028 (launched April 2025 at the AGM, succeeding Power Up 2025): 5-8% annual sales growth, of which 2-3% from acquisitions; current adjusted EBITA margin above 7%; digital at 50% of sales; 20 automated logistics centres (from 10); FCF conversion above 65%; dividend payout of at least 40%. The portfolio target is about 60% core distribution and 40% adjacent higher-margin activities.
  • The shift. From broadline bolt-ons (Schwing, Warshauer) to technical, engineering-led specialties: Talley (wireless), Revere (Rockwell automation partner, $330m), Techno-Contact 360 (Canadian data-centre connectivity), DEE (harnesses and cooling), and GCG (75% value-added: custom assemblies, cable bus, prefabricated whips). Bernstein: "a distinct structural shift from traditional distribution bolt-ons to high-value technical services". Exits: Finland (€254m of sales, €39m gain), New Zealand and the Middle East.
  • Ambition re-dated. The CEO now says the >7% margin will come "between 2027 and 2029 without meaningful volume recovery". This is a hard self-help commitment that widens the date range relative to the market's 2028 assumption.
  • Articulation: strong. Explicit levers (UK and Germany turnaround, AI and digital productivity, mix, GCG), quantified bridges (17bp leverage, 22bp action plans), a stated acquisition hurdle (above WACC by year three).
  • Resourcing. €500m of equity, about €0.8bn of new debt, buybacks paused (previously €50-150m a year), a 100-person central data team, AI tied to management incentives, 200,000 sq ft of US data-centre logistics capacity. It is resourced, but the balance sheet is now the constraint: leverage 2.4x before GCG, target 2.0x by end-2027.
  • Unresolved choice. Rexel says it cannot match Wesco's white-space data-centre margins because of legacy US positioning. GCG partly closes this but leaves Rexel a challenger in the fastest-growing segment.
C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

B708th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets been set or raised? (choices)

Judgement: guidance has been raised twice in 2026 (July and October) from a deliberately low base, and mid-term targets were reaffirmed with a firmer, volume-independent framing. This is a classic conservative-guider upgrade sequence, typical of the early part of an upswing.

  • FY2026 sequence. February 2026: same-day growth of 3-5%, EBITA margin "around 6.2%", FCF conversion above 65%, copper assumed at $11,000/t. April 2026: confirmed despite a Q1 miss. July 2026: same-day growth "about 5%", margin "at least 6.2%". October 2026: "upgraded again" per Bernstein and Jefferies headlines (detail not in the files).
  • Mid-term. Axelerate 2028 (sales growth 5-8%, margin above 7%) reaffirmed. The CEO added "between 2027 and 2029, without meaningful volume recovery" (8 September 2026). GCG adds more than 20bp of margin from year one. Leverage of about 2.0x from or by end-2027.
  • Choice behind the guidance. Management guides on conservative inputs (copper $11,000 against $14,000 spot; no European recovery assumed in H2) and wide bands. The "at least 6.2%" wording leaves room; Morgan Stanley sees H2 seasonality supporting 6.3-6.4%.
  • What it is not. No raise of the >7% mid-term margin and no explicit 2027 guidance. That is the next step to look for at full-year results on 10 February 2027 (financials block next results date), probably alongside GCG integration targets.
C-14To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline?B+686th of 38full answer under 2a Internal risks
C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

C+5710th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: steady, disciplined, modest pruning. Low-return small countries were exited (Finland, New Zealand, Middle East). The larger simplification, turning around or fixing the UK and Germany (about €1bn of revenue near breakeven), is being done through restructuring rather than exit. More pruning is plausible but not signalled.

  • Actions. New Zealand and the Middle East sold in early 2025; Finland sold to Ahlsell (September 2025, €254m of FY2024 sales, €39m gain). Since 2021, €2.8bn of turnover acquired and €0.8bn disposed (net €2.0bn). 33 UK branches closed earlier; restructuring in Germany and Spain; €61.9m of European goodwill impairments (Spain, Nordics) and €55m on Germany and UK cash-generating units.
  • The real lever is UK and Germany. About €1bn of revenue at breakeven (Jefferies) against a European average of about 6.3%. Lifting them to European average adds about €60m of EBITA, about 30bp of group margin (Morgan Stanley estimates 40bp, and up to 100bp with other country turnarounds over four years). H1 2026 progress was more than 100bp of margin in those countries. Rexel is choosing turnaround over exit. If the turnaround stalls (UK construction starts -29%, residential starts -47%; German residential new-build "entirely frozen"), an exit of the UK becomes the logical next step and would be margin-accretive.
  • Composition is otherwise being widened, not narrowed (GCG, Revere, DEE add new technical lines), so on balance the perimeter is getting more complex, not simpler (see C-79).
C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

C+628th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Rexel is entering new product markets (white-space data-centre kitting, custom engineered cable systems, defence and utility infrastructure, industrial automation, cooling assemblies) inside its existing North American geography. This is sensible: it adjoins the core, serves the same customers, raises margin and uses Rexel's purchasing and logistics scale. The execution risk is that these are manufacturing-like, engineering-led businesses, unlike branch distribution.

  • GCG. Two segments (Custom Engineered Solutions; Connectivity & Power Solutions); 75% of sales from proprietary, value-added products and assemblies; cable bus systems that cut installation time by 60%; prefabricated whips for ten times faster power-up; 11% organic CAGR since 2019; 11% EBITA margin. More than 60% of sales in data centres, power and utilities, communications and defence.
  • Other entries. Revere (Rockwell Automation partner, Illinois and Wisconsin, $330m) for industrial automation. DEE (wire harnesses, PCB assemblies, control panels, about 20% cooling) into Rexel USA's Advanced Services. Techno-Contact 360 (Quebec data-centre connectivity). Talley (wireless, $360m, ahead of plan with double-digit growth and high-single-digit margins).
  • Why sensible. It addresses Rexel's diagnosed weakness (white-space under-indexing; data centres 8% of US sales against Wesco 18%). It follows customers' labour scarcity (prefabrication saves field labour) and raises GCG margin to 13% with Rexel purchasing.
  • Risks. Integration of engineering cultures (a Wesco expert says Rexel's "internal fragmentation hampers consistent execution"; a Sonepar USA expert says centralised European models struggle in the fragmented US project market). Concentration on hyperscaler cycles. Bernstein's caution that intense bidding could dilute GCG margins. The board's own hurdle (returns above WACC by year three) is the right test.
  • Geography. No new countries; the trend is consolidation into 17 countries with the US weighted up. That is sensible: no sub-scale flag-planting.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

C5015th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or only latent?

Judgement: mostly nominal. Rexel is passing through supplier and copper inflation promptly and in full, which lifts gross profit in euros. But its own price-over-cost spread is only +6bp (H1 2026) after being negative in 2024-25, and it does not set prices above market. Real pricing power is latent, in digital and GCG value-added services, and is noted increasingly by experts and management, not yet in the numbers.

  • Evidence of pass-through. Selling prices +3.3% in H1 2026 (cable 2.0%, non-cable 1.3%); +2.8% in Q1 (higher than management expected); +3.6% in Q2. Pass-through of supplier rises of 3-5% is "highly effective and prompt" because electrical products are a small share of project budgets (6-7% of construction cost per Legrand). Copper indexation protects gross margin.
  • Evidence it is nominal. Delta inflation (price minus opex inflation) +6bp in H1 2026 and -19bp earlier. Bernstein calls the Q1 pricing "lower in quality" because it was cable-led (1.7%) not core (1.1%). Rexel's non-cable price (1.1-1.4%) lags its suppliers' list price rises (Schneider and Legrand 2-4%); the distributor earns the same percentage margin on a higher price rather than widening its margin. Gross margin rose only modestly: 24.9% (2024), 25.1% (2025), 25.4% (H1 2026), and large projects cost 5bp.
  • Latent real pricing. An expert says Rexel "commands a premium for digital services and real-time inventory visibility" and is "highly rigid" on margin, while Wesco discounts. GCG's engineered products save 20-60% of field labour, which supports value pricing. AI quoting tools (22bp of margin attributed to "AI-powered sales actions via pricing and quoting tools") are early evidence of better price realisation per transaction.
  • Risk. With copper above $14,000/t, the cable price leg is at its peak contribution. When it reverses, the nominal pass-through works in reverse, and the 2024 precedent (solar and PVC deflation) shows a negative spread compresses margin quickly.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

B+665th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: a clear step-up. 2026 spend runs at about 5-6 times the historical annual rate (about €0.4bn of bolt-ons in H1 plus about €1.2bn for GCG, against €150-690m a year in 2021-25). It is strategically sensible and priced with discipline on a post-synergy basis, and the bolt-on integration record is good. GCG is a step-change in size and type, and its integration is the main execution risk of 2027.

  • Track record. 21 acquisitions over 2021-25; 14 in North America (€2.1bn of sales). Morgan Stanley: 70% of €2.7bn of acquired sales were in the US at an average 6.0x EV/EBITDA, below Rexel's own trading multiple, so accretive. Talley exceeded plan. Jefferies notes "an extensive history of integrating 26 bolt-on deals", which supports GCG synergies at the upper end of expectations.
  • GCG terms. $1.4bn EV at 1.30x sales and 11.6x EBITA before synergies; below 8x EBITDAaL after. Year-one EPS accretion is low single digit (Bernstein), about 3% (Jefferies, with full €500m equity), rising to mid single digit and about 8% after synergies. More than 20bp of group margin from year one. Returns above WACC by year three. Seller: Audax private equity.
  • Discipline test. EPS accretion arises mainly because Rexel funds two-thirds with debt at about 4% (it issued 4% 2030 notes) against an acquired earnings yield of about 6.5% after tax. Return on capital in year one is about 6.5% after tax ($121m EBITA × 0.73 / $1.4bn), rising to about 9-10% by year three if the margin reaches 13% on about $1.3bn of revenue. The strategic value (mix, margin, growth, multiple) is stronger than the return-on-capital case. The Wisdom Library's "leverage-to-EPS transmission" warning applies: part of the accretion is financial.
  • Integration risk. GCG is engineering-led, makes products, and is about 5% of group sales. The Wesco precedent (an expert says Wesco "became a very different, hard organization" after Anixter) and expert doubts on Rexel's centralised model in US projects argue for tracking GCG's standalone growth (above 8%) and margin (above 10.5-11.5%), as the latest Earnings Review recommends.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

D4227th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Rexel is using its balance sheet more, for acquisitions, not for returns. Leverage rose from 1.6x (H1 2025) to 2.4x (H1 2026) before GCG, equity was issued, and buybacks were paused. Shareholder returns are flat (the €1.20 dividend has been unchanged since 2022). The shift is from returning cash to reinvesting it, which is negative for near-term per-share capital returns and neutral to positive for long-term EPS if GCG delivers.

  • Leverage path. Net debt to EBITDAaL 2.0x at end-2025, 2.4x at June 2026 (net debt €3,321.9m, up €690m sequentially on dividends of €355m, bolt-ons of €394m and a seasonal working-capital build). Pro forma for GCG, my rough estimate is about 2.3x at end-2026 (H2 FCF of about €400-450m after interest and tax; €500m equity; about €1.2bn GCG outflow; about €1.55bn pro forma EBITDAaL). Target about 2.0x by end-2027, which Jefferies (Chapman) sees reached or beaten through organic cash flow. Plausible on consensus FCF of €742m in FY2027 minus dividends of about €380m.
  • Returns. Dividend €1.20 (52% payout of recurring EPS of €2.30; policy at least 40%); consensus DPS €1.20, €1.30, €1.35 for 2026-28. Buybacks of €100m in 2025 (share count about 296m before the placing) are paused through the GCG deleveraging; consensus does not assume them until about 2028. The share count rises about 4.6% (to about 308m basic; FactSet's 312.2m diluted includes the €400m OCEANE convertible due 2031).
  • Funding quality. Liquidity is ample: €1,582m securitisation (€194m off balance sheet), €400m 4% notes due 2030, €400m OCEANEs, €200m Schuldschein. The risk report claims a "2.4x senior credit covenant ceiling" equal to the H1 ratio. That looks like a conflation of the reported ratio with the covenant and should be verified; no other source states a covenant breach risk.
  • What would change it. Faster deleveraging (FCF plus 2027 EBITDA growth) could bring buybacks back in H2 2027 or 2028. Under the risk-off regime the market rewards balance-sheet headroom (sector digest: "the discount narrows only as leverage falls").
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–58

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: four further positives are present or plausible: (1) structural redesign of the cost base through AI and automation, still early in its effect; (2) the US model reorganisation into multi-banner regional divisions, now paying off; (3) a working-capital release in a later down-leg or as copper normalises; (4) strategic optionality from the shareholder register.

  • AI and automation cost redesign. Two-thirds of SG&A is employee-related, and half the workforce is in call and service centres (12,000 customer service staff). AI tools deliver 10-15% inside-sales productivity (and 20-30% in quoting, per Jefferies). Automated distribution centres go from 10 to 20. Morgan Stanley: digital efficiency of 20-40bp a year on operating margin is plausible for distributors. Sector digest: cost worth 1% of sales is about +16% of EBITA. This is the main reason the >7% margin can come "without volume". A stretch scenario (latest Earnings Review): 150-200bp beyond 7% if AI yields about 300bp of cumulative SG&A savings by 2029. I treat that as a tail, not a base case, because savings get competed back to customers (Morgan Stanley's caution).
  • US reorganisation. The move from a national model to eight multi-banner regional divisions with presidents running logistics and branches, plus the 2023 leadership change (the Canada CEO took over the US), refocused the US on high-margin utility and industrial customers (expert). North American growth of +7% in 2025 and +7.8% in Q2 2026 suggests it works.
  • Working capital as a buffer. Working capital is at 16.3% of sales (H1 2026) against 14.3-15.0% for FY2025 because of copper and growth. A normalisation releases about €250-350m. Rexel's inventory turns are well ahead of Wesco's (about 4.9x; Wesco working capital 20% of sales).
  • Register optionality. Cevian (23%), QXO and CVC interest (C-87). With the shares at 9.9x 24-month-forward P/E and EV/EBITA about 38% below European capital goods (against a historical 25% discount), a bid, a partial sale or a demand for a buyback after deleveraging are all plausible within 18 months.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

B+645th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the key actor, Cevian (23%, board seat since October 2025), has signalled clearly that it wants the value gap closed. It supported the board's rejection of QXO at €28-28.40 as undervaluing, and reportedly backed private-equity (CVC) interest. Cevian is constructive on the current strategy. It has not publicly pushed a change of strategy, but its revealed preference is for value realisation, by operations or by sale.

  • Evidence. Cevian has owned shares since 2016 and is the sole remaining strategic holder. Schuchna replaced Alexanderson on the board and audit committee (continuity of engagement). Cevian's support of the QXO rejection framed the €28 bid as too low. December 2025 reports had Cevian supporting CVC talks; the board retained advisers; management denied ongoing talks. No other large holder (73% free float).
  • What it means. A board with an activist owner will keep the self-help plan honest (margin, working capital and FCF are in the bonus). It is likely to resist further dilutive equity, and it creates a credible floor through private-market interest. Bernstein: takeover interest "underpins a valuation floor". If the shares stay at a deep discount after GCG deleverages (2027-28), pressure for buybacks, a disposal of the UK, or a sale process becomes plausible.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

C+6810th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: strong and broad: margin self-help (productivity, AI, turnarounds), portfolio upgrade (GCG and technical bolt-ons, exits), digital and logistics, and conservative guidance being beaten. Resourcing is adequate but has consumed the balance-sheet headroom. Their earnings effect is front-loaded into 2026-2027 and depends on GCG integration and on the UK and Germany turnarounds converting. Rexel fits the Wisdom Library pattern "sector tailwind plus a self-improving company", with the caveat that the self-improvement is incremental rather than a "massive corporate change".

  • Breadth. Five independent margin levers. Indicative contributions to the move from 6.0% (2025) to above 7% (2028-29): (i) operating leverage on 1-3% volume, about 10-25bp a year; (ii) productivity net of wage inflation, about 10-20bp a year; (iii) UK and Germany, about 30-40bp cumulative; (iv) GCG mix and synergies, about 20-30bp; (v) digital investment drag, about -10bp a year. That sums to roughly 7.0-7.4% by 2028, so the target is reachable without volume recovery only if levers (ii) and (iii) deliver in full.
  • Depth of resourcing. €500m of equity, €0.8bn of debt, 100 data scientists, logistics automation, management incentives tied to AI.
  • Weak spots. No buybacks; leverage at the top of the comfort range; one leader carrying the European turnaround; a large engineering-led acquisition.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

D5621st of 38

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

D5621st of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Rexel operates in a moderately supportive industry structure: concentrated national markets in Europe where it leads, a fragmented US where it is a mid-sized consolidator, rational large competitors, supplier-led pricing that inflates more than it deflates, and low capital intensity. Its weaknesses are relative rather than absolute: it has captured less of the US data-centre wave than Wesco and Sonepar, and sits in the low-margin middle of a value chain where OEMs and hyperscalers hold more power. For the model: the structure supports a 6-7.5% through-cycle EBITA margin for Rexel (not much higher, given the value-chain split), organic growth roughly in line with markets in Europe, and US organic growth above market only in data centres and automation. Disruption risks (direct sourcing, modularisation) are a 2028+ issue, not a 2026-27 one.

C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?D5524th of 38full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

C-5817th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Rexel is the intermediary, so the question is whether the distribution channel is gaining or losing relevance. It is gaining in the core (scale distributors take share from independents; digital and EDI integration deepens lock-in; suppliers rely on distributors for 45-90% of their flow) and losing at the extreme top end (hyperscale direct sourcing, prefabricated modules, OEM direct to large industrial accounts). Net slightly positive; fragility is low in the core and high at the top.

  • Evidence. Legrand: 90% of flow revenues go through professional distributors, and Rexel is about 5% of Legrand's sales. Schneider: partners and indirect distributors generate about 45% of revenue. Hyperscalers: distributors take only 35-40% of the electrical bill of materials. Direct-ship business (about 40% of Wesco sales) yields lower gross margin but higher return on net assets.
  • Rexel's response. EDI and webshop at 36% of sales; ERP integration and Coupa punch-outs; Parspec contractor platform (more than 1,000 active users); GCG's kitting and value-added assembly.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

D4821st of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: competitors are behaving rationally on price but competing hard on capability. Wesco in data centres and utilities, Sonepar in breadth and digital and acquisitions, and Graybar and regional players on local service. This mildly hurts Rexel in the US growth segments and helps it in Europe, where price discipline holds. Rexel's position is robust in its European cores and fragile in US data centres until GCG proves itself.

  • Wesco: data-centre sales above $5bn run-rate, 23% of revenue, +45% in Q2 2026; leads complex utility and infrastructure packages; 20% white space / 80% grey space mix. Rexel admits it "cannot match Wesco's white-space margins".
  • Sonepar: $37.9bn, Spark platform, AutoStore, acquisitive; "fiercest European competitor"; leads Germany.
  • Graybar: $12.9bn, +10.6% in 2025, investing in ERP.
  • Independents: aggressive locally; benefit when Rexel has availability gaps (early 2026).
  • Rexel's defences: France, Benelux and Nordic leadership, digital, purchasing scale, working-capital efficiency, GCG.

C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

D4523rd of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: disruption is acting most strongly upstream (OEMs facing 800V DC and modular shifts; scarce capacity in transformers and switchgear giving OEMs pricing power) and downstream (hyperscalers centralising procurement; contractors consolidating; installer labour scarcity). Rexel sits in the middle and is affected second-hand. Its position is moderately robust in the flow business and fragile in the project business.
