C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

D6024th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: globally Rexel is number two or three in professional electrical distribution, behind Sonepar (about €33.6bn, or $37.9bn, in 2025) and of similar size to Wesco. It is number one or two in its core European countries (above 30% share; France about co-equal with Sonepar). In North America it is a distant fourth or fifth (about 7%, against Sonepar and Wesco at about 25% each and Graybar about 10%).

  • Europe. About 15% share across European wholesale territories, above 30% in core countries (France, Benelux, Austria, Sweden). Rexel and Sonepar control most of France (an expert says about 90% of electrical wholesale; Schneider says above 50% of B2B electrical supplies). Germany is a weak spot: Sonepar leads and Rexel is a "smaller strategic outsider", hence the turnaround.
  • North America. About 7% (prospectus figure), behind Sonepar USA ($15.2bn), Wesco and Graybar ($12.9bn). Top-five player alongside CED. GCG ($1.1bn) moves Rexel North America above €10bn (consensus North America revenue €11.4bn in FY2027) but does not change the rank. In data centres Wesco is about four times larger.
  • Implication. Rexel's profit comes from its leader positions (Europe core, Canada), and its growth comes from a challenger position (the US). The model should expect higher margins in Europe-core (above 6.3% in Europe overall, higher in France) and growth with lower relative profitability in the US, now converging (North America margins described as "resilient"; Europe 6.3% in H1).
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

D5620th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: concentration depends sharply on geography. Core European national markets are highly concentrated (top two above 60-90% in France), which supports pricing discipline. The US is fragmented (top five about one-third, top ten about 30%, more than 5,000 operators) with a gently stepped distribution. Globally it is very fragmented (Rexel about 4%; global distributors about 3% in China).

  • US. Top five (Sonepar, Wesco, Graybar, Rexel, CED) about one-third; top 100 rose from 65% to about 75%. The distribution is steep at the top (Sonepar and Wesco at 25% each against Graybar 10% and Rexel 7%) and then a long tail; five times numbers six to ten is plausible but unmeasured.
  • Europe core. France is effectively a duopoly plus independents (CEF and Edmundson types in the UK; Solar and Ahlsell in the Nordics). The French antitrust case shows regulators view the structure as concentrated enough to monitor.
  • Implication. Concentrated European cores allow above-group margins and price discipline. US fragmentation gives a long acquisition runway at about 6x EBITDA. The Wisdom Library's caution, "fragmentation is usually structural", applies to the smallest local independents, but Rexel's record of 21 deals shows the US roll-up is real for its mid-sized targets.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

C-6217th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: shares among the scaled incumbents are stable and drift slowly toward the large players; new entrants have taken negligible share in the professional electrical market. Rexel's own share is gently rising in France and in US data centres and automation, and probably flat to slightly down in the broad US and in Germany and the UK.

  • Evidence. Top-100 share rose from 65% to 75% after the financial crisis; Sonepar targets a global share rising from 6.7% (2024) to 7.7% (2029). Legrand's CEO says Rexel is "massively gaining share in France"; Rexel's CEO says European shares are "relatively stable". Rexel's 3-year revenue CAGR of 2.2% lags Wesco's 8.6%, Graybar's 8.0%, Sonepar's 6.0% and Ferguson's 5.5%, which says Rexel lost relative share in North America and data centres in 2022-25 (organic, mix and FX all contribute). Amazon Business holds only simple consumables; digital-only entrants have not broken into technical products.
  • Implication. Model Rexel's organic growth at roughly market growth plus 0-1 point in Europe and market minus 0-3 points in the US outside data centres. Data-centre growth runs at Wesco's growth rate or above from a smaller base.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

C-5517th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: yes. Share consolidates steadily toward the three global players (Sonepar, Wesco, Rexel) and toward the best-run regional specialists. In the last cycle the most advantaged were the data-centre-exposed (Wesco) and the private, aggressive acquirer (Sonepar), rather than Rexel. Rexel is among the consolidators, but in the 2022-25 cycle it was the least advantaged of the three. GCG and the digital lead are attempts to change that.

  • Mechanisms favouring scale. Purchasing rebates ($20bn of buying gives "substantial bargaining power"); national accounts (only Rexel, Sonepar and Wesco serve multi-site Fortune 500 clients); digital and AI investment that smaller players cannot fund; acquisition capacity (10-20 deals a year at Sonepar).
  • Where Rexel ranks. Wesco has the data-centre lead and a premium multiple. Sonepar is the largest, private and "fiercest" competitor (an expert says its broader catalogue is the main share threat). Rexel leads on working-capital efficiency (14-15% of sales against Wesco's about 20%) and on digital share in Europe (44%). In the next cycle, if advantage shifts from data-centre exposure to digital and cost efficiency in a slow construction market, Rexel moves up the ranking.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

D5820th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the large competitors are margin-first and benign. Industry prices do not fall when volume falls on most branded electrical products, which suppliers price, but commodity-linked categories (cable with copper, solar panels, PVC) do deflate sharply. That transmits volume downturns into price downturns in exactly the categories that matter at the margin. Sonepar is the one large player with a volume-first streak.

  • Branded non-cable products. Supplier-led list prices rarely fall (Legrand "has never cut its average prices in twenty years"). Distributors pass through; local branch autonomy below $100k is used to optimise, not dump.
  • Commodity categories. Solar deflation (panel prices) and PVC (-70% price collapses) dragged 2024 same-day sales to -2.4% and caused a guidance cut. Cables (15-16% of sales) follow copper both ways.
  • Who is aggressive. Local independents defending municipal relationships; Sonepar (private ownership allows "more autonomous, aggressive local market decisions"); and in US data centres, Wesco and others in tenders. Graybar's gross margin fell 80bp in 2025 from competitive pressure, a sign that North American pricing is less disciplined than European.
  • Implication. In a downturn, model Rexel's gross margin falling about 30-50bp (2023-24: 25.5% to 24.9% on the company basis, -60bp), with the larger damage from operating deleverage.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

C+6412th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years?

Judgement: stable. No sign of erratic, reactive behaviour or a "shark attack" by the large players over 2023-26, even through the 2024 downturn, when distributors competed through cost cuts, not price. The tail risk is Sonepar or a newly capitalised entrant (QXO, which tried to buy Rexel and has been building a building-products distribution platform) changing the US game, and data-centre tender intensity rising as the wave matures.

  • Evidence of stability. Through 2024 deflation Rexel held a 5.9% margin with headcount cuts. Peers did the same (Wesco, Graybar). Rexel's own pricing stance is described as "disciplined" over 2023-26.
  • Changes over 18-36 months. More intense US data-centre bidding (Jefferies). Digital catch-up by Sonepar (Spark, AutoStore with 12 systems installed and 20 more planned). Consolidation in the Nordics (Solar Norge and Sonepar Norge). QXO's and Home Depot's moves into specialty distribution (Home Depot bought HVAC distributor Mingledorff's). None has yet produced price aggression in electrical distribution.
  • Would competitors attack weakness? Rexel's weakness points (Germany, UK, US broadline) are where Sonepar and independents already compete hard. A Rexel stumble in GCG integration would invite Wesco in data-centre accounts, but via service, not price.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

C-5818th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: moderately supportive and fairly robust. Concentrated national markets in Europe, a fragmented US offering a consolidation runway, low capital intensity, rational large competitors and a supplier-driven price structure that rarely deflates outside commodities. The fragility is in two places: commodity deflation (copper, solar, PVC), which turns the price support into a headwind; and upstream (OEM) and downstream (hyperscaler, consolidated contractor) power over the most attractive large projects.

  • Support. The structure has kept distributor margins at 5.9-7.3% through cycles. Rexel lifted its trough margin about 200bp. Capex is about 1% of sales.
  • Limits. Distributors capture far less of value-chain profit than OEMs (Schneider and Legrand at 18-25% EBIT; Rexel about 6%). Installers earn 6-9%. Rexel's profit is a thin spread on a large flow, so small changes in price, mix and cost move it a lot (C-75).

C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

D4526th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the main substitute for the distributor is the customer buying direct (from OEMs or through prefabricated modules), and that threat is growing at the high end. Technology substitutes (800V DC, solid-state transformers) change what is sold more than whether the distributor sells it. Medium threat, slowly rising, mainly to the growth narrative rather than to the core.

  • Direct procurement: hyperscalers source transformers and switchgear direct; modular builds rise from 20% to 60% of data-centre builds by 2030 (which favours integrated OEMs and prefabricators); OEMs sell direct to large industrial accounts (26% of Rexel's sales are industrial). Consolidated contractors negotiate with OEMs (2-5 year horizon).
  • Mitigants: standard materials still flow through scale players; prefabrication creates a new role that GCG fills (kitted, exact-length assemblies); OEM forward integration is costly (credit, last mile, fragmentation).
  • Technology: 800V DC changes the white-space product mix by about 2028-30. For a distributor it is a product-mix question.

Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

D5025th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Rexel has a moderate, durable cost-and-availability advantage built on scale purchasing, physical density in its core European markets, national-account capability and a digital lead, but no strong pricing moat. Its margin is a thin spread borrowed from suppliers' pricing power and protected by operational excellence. The model should assume Rexel holds share in Europe, gains modestly in France and in US specialties, earns a through-cycle EBITA margin of about 6-7.5% (moving toward the top of that range with GCG and self-help), and does not expand gross margin materially. Pricing behaves as pass-through: positive nominal contribution in inflation, negative in deflation.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

D5224th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Rexel's advantages are real but moderate. They are cost and availability advantages of a scaled physical network, not pricing moats. The strongest is local leadership plus branch and hub density in its core European countries. Purchasing scale and national-account capability are moderate and shared with Sonepar and Wesco. Its digital integration lead is real but narrowing as rivals catch up. Net, the moat is stable to slightly widening, helped by GCG and AI, but it supports a 6-7.5% EBITA margin, not a premium one.

Digital and data integration Customer (switching cost) 36% of sales digital (44% in Europe); EDI/ERP, Coupa, Parspec; experts say Rexel "commands a premium for digital services"; customer stickiness Moderate Narrowing relative to Sonepar; widening against independents

  • Wisdom check. "Density, not network effects, in physical networks": Rexel's density is a route-cost advantage that works only where it leads (core Europe), not in the US. "Scale needs a bounded market": national electrical markets are bounded, so purchasing scale holds within them. "Purchasing power with suppliers" is the most common real advantage in distribution and is durable here. "AI claims are operating basics, not moats": treat AI as a 2-3 year margin window.
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

C5516th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: large in absolute terms (revenue €19.4bn, about €22.8bn in 2027 with GCG; 26,601 staff; 1,876 branches) and number two or three globally. That confers purchasing, digital and AI investment capacity, national-account eligibility and M&A firepower. But Rexel is about 60% of Sonepar's size, smaller than Wesco in the US, and dwarfed by its suppliers in value-chain power.

  • Revenue: Sonepar €33.6bn (2025); Wesco ahead of Rexel in the US with Q2 2026 sales of $6.7bn a quarter; Rexel €19.4bn; Graybar $12.9bn; Ferguson larger but plumbing and HVAC. Suppliers: Schneider €40.2bn at 18.7% EBITA; Legrand €9.5bn at 20.7%.
  • What scale confers: purchasing terms (a "fundamental pillar", per a former director); funding of a central data team and AI tools that sub-scale rivals cannot afford; the ability to serve national accounts; the ability to absorb a $1.4bn acquisition (about 7% of EV).
  • What it does not confer: pricing over suppliers (who hold specification power) or a share of the hyperscale white-space pool (Wesco's data-centre scale is about four times Rexel's).

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

D5620th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: demonstrably not inferior, and leading in parts of Europe, on what electrical customers value most (availability, delivery reliability, breadth, digital ordering, technical support). It is behind Wesco on complex US data-centre and utility packages, a gap GCG starts to close. Utility slipped in early 2026 because of product availability gaps.

  • Evidence for: 92-95% fill rates in core European maintenance (better than Wesco's transactional agility); French engineering buyers rank Rexel's e-catalogues above RS Group's; customers pay a premium for digital and real-time inventory (food and beverage procurement director); steel buyers raised Rexel procurement up to 16% for availability; Legrand's CEO says Rexel is massively gaining share in France.
  • Evidence against: Wesco "outpaces Rexel on complex multi-category infrastructure packages"; Rexel had "no meaningful utility market footprint domestically" in the US (before GCG); early-2026 delivery problems pushed customers to local suppliers; Sonepar's broader catalogue is seen as the main threat; UK branches remain relationship-led, not digital-led.
  • Survey anchor: customers rank inventory availability (63%) and delivery speed (61%) far ahead of digital ordering (14%) (Morgan Stanley distributor survey). This favours Rexel's physical network as long as it keeps availability high.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

C-4716th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility?

Judgement: limited pricing power of its own. Rexel has demonstrable pass-through power (it passes supplier and commodity inflation promptly without losing customers), but little evidence of raising its margin over cost. Gross margin moved within 24.9-25.5% over 2023-H1 2026, and the price-over-opex spread was +6bp. The pass-through itself is valuable on a fixed-cost base, but it is borrowed from suppliers' pricing power, not Rexel's. (Detail in C-17.)

  • Signs of genuine power: discipline against discounting (rigid on margin); a digital premium; GCG's labour-saving engineered products; AI quoting attributed +22bp of margin.
  • Signs of limits: commodity lines face 5-10% share shifts on benchmarking; a gross-margin squeeze in 2024 deflation; large projects dilute; non-cable pricing (1.1-1.4%) runs below suppliers' list price rises (2-4%).
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

C4212th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto?

Judgement: little explicit sharing. Rexel's culture and incentives (40% of the bonus on gross margin) prioritise margin capture over handing back scale benefits, and management's stance is "selectivity". De facto, some sharing occurs through availability, fill rates and digital service levels that small rivals cannot match, and through framework rebates for national accounts. This is not a Costco- or Ryanair-style model, and the absence limits the flywheel effect on share.

  • Implication for the model: do not expect Rexel to buy share with price. Share gains come from service and availability (France) and from acquisitions (US). Over time, AI-driven cost savings may be competed away partly (Morgan Stanley's caution), which argues for assuming only part of the cost saving reaches margin (about half).
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

B608th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: moderate gravitational support. Several forces work for Rexel each year without heroics: (1) the channel concentrating on scale players (top-100 share 65% to 75%); (2) digital share rising 200-300bp a year, lowering cost to serve; (3) product price inflation over time on a fixed branch base; (4) a growing share of electrification in the mix; (5) acquisitions compounding in the fragmented US at about 6x. The counter-gravity is wage inflation (2.5-3% a year on a cost base where personnel and delivery are 68% of opex), which needs productivity just to stand still. So the forces are a gentle tailwind, not a strong one.

  • Mechanical margin arithmetic: digital penetration rising about 250bp a year at much lower cost to serve, plus logistics automation (10 to 20 centres), is roughly enough to offset wage inflation. Real margin gains need volume, mix (GCG, automation) and turnarounds.
  • Against peers: Wesco's data-centre gravity (23% of sales) is stronger now; Rexel's European duopoly gravity is stronger in a recovery.

Company - Management

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Management is a clear strength: a credible, action-oriented CEO with a strong operational record, a stable CFO, conservative and accurate guidance, excellent disclosure of the drivers, and a sensible strategy that targets the business's real weaknesses. The leadership's limitations are a moderate rather than bold ambition, conventional rather than dynamic capital allocation (now paused buybacks, equity issued), low personal shareholding, and a thin bench for a period of heavy integration. For the model and for predictability: weight management guidance as a reliable floor (expect beats of about 0.5-1 point on organic growth and about 10-20bp on margin in normal conditions), and treat the >7% margin target for 2027-29 as likely to be reached by 2028-29 rather than 2027.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

D5223rd of 38

That is meaningful for Rexel but below the growth ambitions of Wesco (data-centre-led double-digit growth), Sonepar (7.7% global share target, 10-20 deals a year) or the Swedish compounders (Beijer Ref: doubling in 5-7 years).

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

C6413th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How great and smart is the strategy?

Judgement: good and smart, and well matched to Rexel's position: protect and digitise the European franchise, fix the laggard countries, redeploy capital into North American specialties where growth and margin are higher, and use AI to cut cost-to-serve. It widens the moat at the margins (technical value-add, digital lock-in) and is robust across environments: margin progress "without volume recovery" is designed for a weak Europe, while North American specialties give upside in a strong capex cycle. Its weakness is that it leaves Rexel a follower in US data centres and does not reduce European cyclicality quickly.

  • Dynamic elements. The portfolio pivot (exits plus specialties); the 60/40 target mix between core and adjacencies; the multi-banner US regional model; acquisition firepower kept by issuing equity rather than overloading debt.
  • Gaps. No explicit plan for Germany beyond turnaround (Sonepar leads there); UK exit not considered publicly; pay plan lacks a ROCE measure.

C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

D5020th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator?

Judgement: a disciplined, conventional allocator. It buys growth at sensible multiples (about 6x for bolt-ons; below 8x post-synergies for GCG), prunes low-return units, pays a steady 40-50% dividend and buys back modestly (€50-150m a year) when leverage allows. It is not a dynamic, counter-cyclical repurchaser: buybacks are small and now paused, and it issued equity at €34.75 (about 11.9x 2027 EPS), a fair rather than opportunistic price. Returns on acquired capital are at or modestly above WACC.

  • Record. 2021-25: dividends of €140-362m a year; buybacks of €66-131m a year in 2022-25 (about 1% of market capitalisation); acquisitions of €150-690m a year; no large write-offs on recent deals (impairments were on legacy European units).
  • Judgement on GCG funding. One-third equity was prudent for the rating but dilutive: issuing at about 12x earnings to buy at about 11.6x EBITA before synergies is roughly value-neutral before synergies. Enlightened would have meant buying more stock in 2024-25 below €28 (the QXO bid level). It did buy €100m in 2025.

Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

D4521st of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Rexel's economics are those of a good, thin-margin, capital-light distributor: high returns on tangible capital (about 18% post-tax) and on organic growth, mediocre returns on total capital employed (about 10%) because growth is bought, 6% EBITA margins with strong and asymmetric operating leverage to price and volume, decent but countercyclical cash conversion, and moderate leverage temporarily raised by GCG. For the model: EPS is driven by three things in order of sensitivity: (1) EBITA margin (±50bp = ±10% of EPS), which is driven by the price-over-cost spread and mix more than by volume; (2) organic growth (±1pp = ±3%); (3) currency (±5% = ±4%). Base expectations for 2026-28: margin rising about 30bp a year to about 6.9-7.1% in 2028; FCF of about 70-90% of net income; leverage falling to about 2x by end-2027; a share count about 4-5% higher than 2025. Earnings quality is acceptable but watch copper effects, tax and GCG amortisation.

C-5516th of 38

Judgement: EPS is recovering strongly from a cyclical trough (+11% in 2025, about +16% consensus in 2026, +19% in 2027), on revenue growth of about 7-9% (organic about 5% plus acquisitions, including GCG). The growth is durable for 2026-27 (self-help, GCG, conservative guidance) and less durable beyond (copper and data-centre comparisons, dilution, buyback pause).

  • History (Viewforth EPS, undiluted). 2019 €1.21; 2020 €0.93; 2021 €2.13; 2022 €3.09; 2023 €2.69; 2024 €2.02; 2025 €2.31. Revenue €13.7bn to €19.4bn (5.9% CAGR, inflation- and acquisition-heavy), flat since 2022. Over 2019-25, EPS CAGR was about 11%; over 2022-25 it was -9% a year. Rexel's EPS is cyclical: about ±25-35% swings across a cycle.

Net about +19%. The 2027 consensus is earnings growth from margin and acquisition, not from volume.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

D5518th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: organic reinvestment returns are very high but the opportunity is small (capex about 1% of sales; working capital about 15% of incremental sales). Acquisition returns are moderate (about 9-12% after tax on bolt-ons at about 6x EBITDA, about 6.5% rising to 9-10% for GCG) but scalable (a fragmented US). Rexel can therefore deploy €0.5-1bn a year at mid-to-high single-digit to low-teens returns for 3-5 years once leverage allows. That supports compounding of about 8-12% EPS a year mid-term, but at returns near or modestly above WACC (8.7% per Bernstein), so it does not justify a compounder multiple.

  • Organic growth economics. Each €1 of incremental revenue needs about €0.15-0.16 of trade working capital and €0.01 of capex, and earns 6.5% to 20% (average to marginal) EBITA. Return on incremental tangible capital is about 40% pre-tax, about 28% post-tax, at the average margin. Organic growth is highly value-creative, but its volume depends on markets.
  • Acquisitions. 2021-24 bolt-ons averaged 6.0x EV/EBITDA, about a 10-11% post-tax return before synergies. GCG at 11.6x EBITA before synergies gives about 6.5% after tax in year one, and about 9-10% after synergies and growth by year three. The 2026 run-rate of spend is about €1.6bn (bolt-ons plus GCG); sustainable after deleveraging is about €0.4-0.8bn a year (2-3% sales growth target).
  • Operating spend. Digital, AI and logistics automation (about 10bp of margin a year in digital investment) earn returns through productivity (4% in H1 2026). High return, and the main source of margin uplift.
  • Fair multiple. With about 3-4% organic growth, 2-3% acquisition growth at about WACC-plus returns, and margin expansion, a fair through-cycle multiple is around Rexel's own 10-year median (24-month-forward P/E 9.3x; band 8.6-10.5x), not the 14-20x of compounders. The case for a re-rating rests on lower cyclicality (mix to North America and specialties) more than on reinvestment.

GCG will lower group ROCE for 1-2 years. This is the profile of a good operator whose growth is bought rather than generated.

C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

D4522nd of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: cash conversion is decent on the company's definition and mediocre on a strict per-share basis, and it is countercyclical. Working capital absorbs cash in upswings with inflation (2021-23, 2026) and releases it in downturns (2024). FY2025 was depressed by the fine. Expect conversion of about 70-90% of net income in 2026-28, held down by working-capital absorption as copper and growth push working capital up.

  • Company basis. FCF before interest and tax over EBITDAaL: above 65% target, met three years running; 66% in 2025 (76.4% excluding the €124m fine); €814m in 2025; €247m in H1 2026 (H1 is seasonally weak).
  • FactSet basis (FCF = operating cash flow - capex; over reported net income). 2019 145%; 2021 79%; 2022 52%; 2023 54%; 2024 114%; 2025 50%. Consensus FCF/net income about 0.72x, 0.81x and 0.92x for 2026-28 (FCF €536m, €742m, €934m against net income €747m, €916m, €1,014m). Lease payments are not deducted, so true equity FCF is lower by about €250-300m a year (an estimate).
  • Working capital. Trade working capital at 14.3-15.0% of sales (FY2025), 16.3% (H1 2026, seasonal and copper); FactSet net working capital as % of LTM revenue 14.8% (FY2025) to 16.7% (H1 2026). Inventory 56.5 days; receivables 47.9 days; payables down from 50.5 to 47.9 (supplier terms tightening). Rule of thumb: each €1bn of organic revenue growth absorbs about €150m.
  • Supports and masks. €1,582m of receivables securitisation, €194m off balance sheet, supports cash in the cycle; a reduction in factoring would cut reported cash flow (Schneider lost four days of debtors from factoring). Bonus pay is 20% tied to working capital, which helps.
  • Copper effect. At a stable gross-margin percentage, higher copper raises inventory and receivables. A copper fall would release cash but cut profit.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

C+4812th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or other leakage erode EPS?

Judgement: reasonably clean, with three issues the model must handle. (1) Adjusted figures exclude the copper revaluation effect (gains now, losses in a reversal), restructuring, impairments and disposal gains. The gap to reported was large in 2024 (EPS €1.13 reported against €2.22 recurring) and modest in 2025 (€2.00 against €2.30). (2) The tax rate is high (30% reported 2025, 46.6% in 2024 on non-deductible items) and a real leakage. (3) GCG will add acquired-intangible amortisation, which may or may not be added back in brokers' adjusted EPS. No material capitalisation of costs (capex is about 1% of sales).

  • Recurring one-offs. The copper effect (+€20.7m non-recurring EBITA in H1 2026, against +€0.4m in H1 2025; excluded from adjusted EBITA). Restructuring (€11.4m in H1 2025, €2.7m in H1 2026; €82.5m in 2018). Impairments (€55m in Germany and UK; €61.9m in Europe earlier). The antitrust fine (€124m). Finland gain (€39m). Rexel has some recurring "non-recurring" items, but they are not systematically flattering; the 2026 copper gain is excluded, which is conservative.
  • Tax. Reported rate 26.1% (2023), 46.6% (2024), 30.2% (2025). The French surcharge lifts the group to about 30%. Consensus implies about 27% in 2026. A cleaner normalised rate is 27-28% (France about 25% plus surcharge, US about 25%).
  • Other leakage. Minorities are negligible (€12.8m). Interest includes leases (FactSet net interest €218m). Off-balance-sheet securitisation (€194m) slightly flatters reported net debt. Pensions are €100m.
  • Watch. Whether brokers' 2027 adjusted EPS excludes GCG acquired-intangible amortisation (the widening gap between pre-tax profit and adjusted net income in consensus suggests some add-back). That changes like-for-like EPS growth by several points.
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

D4423rd of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: moderate and manageable, temporarily elevated. Net debt to EBITDAaL is 2.4x (company basis, H1 2026), about 2.3x pro forma end-2026 with GCG (my estimate), and about 3.0x on FactSet's lease-inclusive basis. Interest cover (EBITA over net interest including leases) is about 5x. Leverage amplifies EPS about 1.2-1.3 times the EBIT change. It is comfortable for a non-recession path. In a joint downturn (EBITDA -15%) it would rise toward 2.8-3.0x, delaying buybacks and pressuring the multiple but not threatening solvency.

  • Structure. Net financial debt €3,321.9m (June 2026); FactSet total debt €5,890m including leases, cash €1,034m. Instruments: €400m 4% notes due 2030, €400m OCEANE convertible due 2031 (potential dilution; FactSet diluted shares are 312.2m against about 308m basic after the placing), €200m Schuldschein, €1,582m securitisation programme. Rating: management sized the equity "to preserve the credit rating".
  • Interest. FactSet net interest €218m (2025) including lease interest; consensus implies below-EBIT charges rising from about €286m (2026) to €309m (2027) and €333m (2028) (EBIT minus pre-tax), which includes GCG debt and possibly acquired-intangible amortisation.
  • EPS gearing. 2027: EBIT +14% to EPS +19% (helped by tax and interest relative to growth, hurt by dilution). In a downturn, a 10% EBIT fall gives about 12-13% lower EPS.
  • The "covenant at 2.4x" claim. The Risks digest says reduced cash flow takes leverage "toward the 2.4x senior credit covenant ceiling". 2.4x is the reported H1 ratio, not plausibly the covenant. Verify the actual covenant level (likely about 3.5x for this type of facility).
  • Comfort. The target of about 2.0x by end-2027 is achievable on consensus FCF (€742m in 2027, minus about €380m of dividends, plus EBITDA growth). It fails only in a joint construction-downturn and copper-deflation scenario.

Pricing power

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

D4220th of 38

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: price will contribute strongly to revenue for the next two to three quarters and then fade to about +1-1.5 points a year from H2 2027. Its contribution to margin turns from slightly positive in 2026 to slightly negative in 2027-28, because Rexel's pricing power is borrowed: it passes supplier and copper inflation through at a constant gross-margin percentage, but cannot price above its own cost inflation when supplier list prices and copper stop rising. Rexel's own pricing power exists only in the engineered and value-added activities (GCG, kitting, technical services), about 6-8% of sales after GCG, too small to change the group's price-over-cost spread before 2029.

The price contribution by period (company same-day basis, my base case).

Period Cable price Non-cable price Total price Price-over-cost spread (bp of margin)
2025 +0.7 +0.6 +1.3 -19
H1 2026 (actual) +2.0 +1.3 +3.3 +6
H2 2026 +2.4 +1.4 +3.8 about +10
H1 2027 +0.6 +1.5 +2.1 about -5
H2 2027 -0.3 +1.2 +0.9 about -15
2028-2030 0 (flat copper) +1.2-1.5 +1.2-1.5 about -10 a year, covered by productivity

Why it is borrowed rather than owned.

  1. Gross margin did not widen in the inflation years. The company's gross margin was 25.5% (2023), 24.9% (2024), 25.1% (2025) and 25.4% (H1 2026). In 2021-22, the EBITA margin rose 230bp while gross margin barely moved: the gain came from opex absorption, not from a better spread over purchase cost.
  2. Non-cable price lags suppliers. Rexel's non-cable price ran at +1.1-1.4% in 2026 while Schneider priced +4.2% (Q2) and +5.5% (Q3) and Legrand +3.6%. The distributor passes the rises on with a lag and at a constant percentage margin.
  3. When supplier and commodity inflation stops, the spread turns negative. 2025: selling prices +1.3% against personnel costs +2.9% gave -19bp. 2024: solar and PVC deflation (under 10% of sales but -1 to -2 points of growth) took the group to -0.9% price and helped cut the margin 90bp.
  4. The evidence of own pricing power is real but small. Experts describe Rexel as "highly rigid and disciplined" on margin; AI quoting tools are credited with part of the +22bp of action plans; GCG's engineered products save 20-60% of field labour and support value pricing (75% of GCG sales are proprietary or value-added). That is about €1.5bn of revenue in 2027 out of €22bn.

The copper mechanics the model needs. Cables are about 16% of sales (about 17% after GCG); copper is about 60% of cable composition; Jefferies' rule of thumb is ±$1,000/t = ±100bp of organic growth and ±10-15bp of EBITA margin. Pass-through has a double lag: suppliers reprice cables over weeks to a few months, and Rexel turns inventory in about two months. On the way up this produces a non-recurring gain (€20.7m in H1 2026, €44m in H1 2021) that is excluded from adjusted EBITA. On the way down it produces a non-recurring loss, also excluded, but the recurring effect (lower prices on a fixed cost base) hits adjusted EBITA immediately. At the current price of about $14,000/t, the recurring benefit against the company's $11,000/t guidance assumption is about +30-45bp of margin and about +3 points of 2026 growth. If copper holds, that level effect stays in the base and stops adding. If copper returns to $11,000/t, it comes out over two to three quarters.

Tariffs. Section 232 duties at 50% on steel and aluminium content and Section 301 duties add a US price umbrella that Rexel has passed through (non-cable price growth in North America in 2025-26). This supports US non-cable price at about +2-3% into H1 2027, which is the main reason I hold group non-cable price at +1.5% in H1 2027 rather than lower. It is a nominal effect: it raises revenue and working capital more than margin.

Large projects. Hyperscale data-centre tenders dilute gross margin (-5bp in H1 2026; Wesco has reported about 100bp of project-mix dilution; experts cite 3-5% margins on direct-ship business). As US data centres go from about 9% to about 11-12% of US sales, I assume -5bp a year of gross-margin dilution in 2027-28, partly offset by GCG's higher-margin white-space kitting.

How price shapes the lines. Revenue: price adds about 3.6 points in 2026, about 1.5 points in 2027 and about 1.2-1.5 points a year in 2028-30, against about -0.9 points (2024) and +1.3 points (2025). Margin: about +10bp of help in 2026 becomes about -10bp of drag in 2027 and 2028, which productivity must cover. Working capital: price inflation raised trade working capital from about 14.8% of LTM sales (end-2025) to 16.7% at June 2026 (FactSet), partly seasonal; I assume 15.3% at end-2026 and a slow return to about 14.9% by 2029-30 as copper stops rising.

The skew. Upside: supplier list prices are still rising fast (Schneider +5.5% in Q3 2026), so non-cable price could run at +2% or more in H1 2027 (worth about +10bp of margin and +0.5 points of growth). Downside: copper at $11,000/t (about -3 points of growth and -30 to -45bp of margin), or a European price fight as Bernstein (22 October 2026) reports competitors "discounting commodity products to clear excess inventory". The downside is larger and faster.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7550P-0148
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

D5022nd of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: highly material and asymmetric. Fixed costs are 72% of opex (about 13.7% of sales), on a 6% EBITA margin. A 1% volume change moves EBITA about 2-3.5% (about 9-14bp of margin), a 1% price change at constant gross-margin percentage moves EBITA about 4% (about 19bp), and wage inflation of 2.5% costs about 8% of EBITA a year unless offset. History shows the full cycle: margin +230bp in 2019-22 (inflation) and -90bp in 2024 (on -2.4% organic). For 2027, that creates a margin cone of about 5.8-7.0% and an EBIT cone of about ±15% around consensus.

  • Cost structure (company basis, 2025). Gross margin 25.1%; opex including depreciation about 19.1% of sales (about €3.7bn); fixed 72% of opex (about 13.7% of sales); variable opex about 5.4% of sales; personnel and delivery 68% of opex.
  • Theoretical drop-through. Volume: contribution about 25.1% - 5.4% = about 19.7% of incremental sales, so 1% volume adds about €38m, about +3.3% EBITA, about +13bp. Price at constant gross-margin percentage: 1% price adds about €48m of gross profit, about +4% EBITA, about +19bp. Wage and cost inflation of 2.5% on fixed and semi-fixed costs is about €80-95m a year, so Rexel needs about 2% price, or 2-3% productivity, just to hold margin.
  • Empirical drop-through. H1 2026: about 1.8% volume gave +17bp of operating leverage (about 9bp per point; part absorbed by large-project mix and investment). 2025: +2.5% organic gave +10bp. 2024: -2.4% organic gave -90bp (deflation in solar, PVC and copper plus volume, partly offset by -2.2% headcount). 2020: revenue -8.4% gave about -60bp (FactSet basis) with heavy cost action. The downside slope (about 35-40bp per point of organic decline when price falls too) is three to four times the recent upside slope (about 5-6bp per point), because inflation in costs and investments absorbs part of the upside.
  • Broker sensitivities. Morgan Stanley: volume declines deleverage branches, taking EBITA 15% below base; trough 5.7%. The Change Forces report: ±1pp revenue = ±€0.07 EPS (3%); ±50bp margin = ±€0.24 (10%); ±2pp volume = ±€0.14 (6%). Jefferies: a branch model below 2% organic volume amplifies EPS downside.
  • Cone for 2027 (EBIT €1,500m consensus). Bear (organic -1% with copper deflation, volume -2%): margin about 5.8-6.0%, EBIT about €1,330-1,380m (-8% to -11%). Base (organic +4%): about 6.6-6.8%. Bull (organic +7%, European recovery): about 7.0-7.2%, EBIT about €1,600-1,640m (+7% to +9%).
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

D4824th of 38

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: operating leverage will contribute much less to margin over 2027-2030 than the 2026 numbers suggest, because the composition of growth is shifting from price (which drops through at about twice the rate of volume) to volume and acquisition. In 2027 only about 10-15bp of the roughly +33bp margin step is organic operating leverage and self-help net of inflation; about 24bp is GCG's mix. Margin progress from 2028 depends on self-help and a modest European volume recovery, at about +15-25bp a year. The leverage is strongly asymmetric: a reversal (copper deflation plus volume decline) would take margin down three to four times faster than growth lifts it. The predictability question for the third set is therefore less "will volume come?" than "will price and copper hold?".

The mechanics, measured.

  • Cost structure (company, FY2025). Gross margin about 25.1%; opex including depreciation about 19% of sales (about €3.7bn); 72% of opex fixed; personnel 59% and delivery 9% of opex. Variable opex is about 5.4% of sales, so each euro of volume-driven sales contributes about 19.7 cents before inflation.
  • Theoretical drop-through. 1% of volume adds about €38m of EBITA (+13bp of margin). 1% of price at a constant gross-margin percentage adds about €48m of gross profit with no extra cost (+19bp of margin). Opex inflation of 2.5-3% on the fixed and semi-fixed base costs about €80-100m a year, so Rexel needs about 2% of price, or about 2.5% of productivity, to stand still.
  • Empirical drop-through is lower on the way up and higher on the way down. H1 2026: volume of about 1.8% produced +17bp of operating leverage; with action plans +22bp, large-project gross margin -5bp, digital investment -10bp and delta inflation +6bp, organic margin rose +28bp on +5.1% organic growth (about 5.5bp per point). 2025: +2.5% organic gave +10bp (4bp per point). On the way down, 2024's -2.4% (volume -1.5%, price -0.9%) took 90bp off (about 37bp per point), and 2020's -6.5% same-day took 78bp off (12bp per point, cushioned by a 6% staffing cut and temporary measures). My refinement of the first set's slope: a volume-only decline costs about 15-25bp of margin per point; a decline that includes price deflation costs about 35-40bp per point. The difference is that price deflation removes gross profit with no offsetting variable-cost saving.

Why 2026 overstates the run-rate. About half of 2026's same-day growth is price (cable about 2.2-2.5 points with copper above $14,000/t in H2 against $9,500-10,000 a year earlier; non-cable about 1.3-1.5 points). Price drops through at about 19bp per point theoretically. In 2027, at flat copper of about $13,500-14,000/t (Morgan Stanley's 2027 copper forecast is $6.20/lb, about $13,670/t; Jefferies' is $6.50/lb, about $14,330/t), the cable price contribution falls to about +0.6 points in H1 and about -0.3 points in H2, while non-cable price runs at about +1.2-1.5 points. With opex inflation of about 2.6%, the price-over-cost spread turns from +6bp (H1 2026) to roughly -10bp. Volume becomes the larger leg (data centres about +1 point, core about +0.5-1 point), and volume drops through at the lower rate.

The 2027 margin bridge (company basis, my base case).

Item bp Comment
FY2026 starting point 6.36% H1 6.15% actual, H2 6.55% assumed

Insight: GCG's mix (+24bp) and the copper level are of the same order of magnitude. Jefferies estimates copper at $14,000/t is worth about +45bp of margin against the company's $11,000/t guidance assumption (10-15bp per $1,000/t). A return of copper to $11,000/t during 2027 would therefore cancel the whole GCG accretion, and the non-recurring inventory loss (excluded from adjusted EBITA, included in reported profit) would come on top. The third set should treat 2027 margin as a contest between a near-certain positive (GCG consolidation, once closed) and an uncertain commodity level, with the organic contribution small either way.

How it shapes the main lines, against 2019-2025.

Line 2019-2025 behaviour 2026-2030 base case Main operating-leverage effect
Revenue 5.9% CAGR 2019-25, inflation- and acquisition-heavy; flat 2022-25 (CAGR about 1.2%) 5.5% CAGR 2025-30, of which GCG about 1 point a year on average Growth mix shifts from price to volume and acquisition

The cone for 2027. Bear (organic -1%: copper to about $11,000/t, data-centre pause, European double-dip): margin about 6.0%, EBITA about €1,290m, flat on 2026. Base: 6.68%, €1,490m. Bull (organic +6.5%: copper about $16,000/t, European recovery starting, data centres +40%): about 7.05%, €1,620m. The cone is skewed: the bear outcome is about -13% from base and the bull about +9%, and the bear path is faster because deflation hits margin at 35-40bp per point.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set asked for "30-40bp a year in 2027 and 2028". I agree with the total for 2027 (+33bp) but not with its source: organic leverage and self-help net of inflation deliver only about 12bp of it. 2028 is +27bp in my base (GCG synergies +5bp, UK and Germany +10bp, productivity +10bp, volume +10bp, price-over-cost and digital -8bp), which assumes a modest European volume recovery begins in 2028.

5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7644P-0254
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?D4423rd of 38full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

C-5417th of 38

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: financial leverage peaks at the GCG closing (about 2.3x net debt to EBITDAaL pro forma at end-2026 on the company's definition, about 3.0x on FactSet's lease-inclusive basis) and falls to about 2.0x by end-2027 and 1.6-1.8x by 2028-30 without heroics. It amplifies EBITA changes into EPS by about 1.2 times. GCG's year-one EPS accretion (about +2.5% on the recurring basis) is almost entirely financial: two-thirds of the price is funded by debt costing about 4% pre-tax against an acquired EBITA yield of about 10%. On reported (IFRS) EPS the deal is roughly neutral to slightly dilutive in year one because of new PPA amortisation. Leverage is comfortable in any non-recessionary path, but it is the binding constraint on capital returns until 2028 and, by keeping buybacks off, it removes the share-count support EPS had in 2022-25.

The net debt path (company definition, € millions).

End-2025 End-2026 End-2027 End-2028 End-2029 End-2030
Net financial debt 2,631 about 3,570 about 3,255 about 3,060 about 3,050 about 3,130
EBITDAaL 1,233 about 1,420 (about 1,535 with GCG pro forma) about 1,620 about 1,745 about 1,855 about 1,960
Net debt / EBITDAaL 2.0x (2.03x reported) about 2.5x reported, about 2.3x pro forma about 2.0x about 1.75x about 1.65x about 1.6x

Assumptions: GCG paid at closing around the year-end (€1,200m for the $1.4bn enterprise value), consolidated from 1 January 2027; €500m of equity received (net about €490m); dividends €1.20 per share paid in 2026 and 2027; buybacks resume at €100m in 2028, €150m in 2029 and €200m in 2030; bolt-on acquisitions resume at €200m in 2028 and €350-400m a year in 2029-30. If closing slips into Q1 2027, end-2026 leverage is about 1.7x and the €1.2bn outflow moves into 2027; the end-2027 figure does not change.

The target is reachable but not with much room. Management's "about 2.0x from or by end-2027" is met in my base case at about 2.0x, and Jefferies has 1.9x. It needs FY2027 FCF after leases of about €735m and working capital to stop absorbing cash. A copper-and-volume bear case (EBITA -13% in 2027) leaves end-2027 leverage at about 2.4x and delays buybacks to 2029. No plausible path threatens solvency or (on my reading) the covenant. The first set's doubt about the risk report's "2.4x covenant ceiling" stands; 2.4x is the H1 reported ratio, and the actual covenant level should be verified (research request OWN-5).

Interest cost. Net financial items were €218m in 2025 (including about €70m of lease interest). H1 2026 was €112.5m (lease interest €37.0m, interest on borrowings €74.4m). I model €235m (2026), €265m (2027: the €800m of GCG debt at about 4% adds about €32m), then €255m, €250m and €250m as leverage falls but lease interest grows with the network. Pre-Q3 consensus had €267m for 2027, so interest is not a source of difference. Two details matter for the model: (1) the €400m OCEANE (1% coupon) costs about 3.5-4% in the P&L, not 1%, because the €347.2m debt component accretes to €400m over five years (about €10-11m a year of non-cash interest on top of the €4m coupon); (2) about 30% of net financial items is lease interest, which rises with the logistics network regardless of deleveraging.

EPS gearing. In 2027, net financial items are about 18% of EBITA, so a 10% change in EBITA moves recurring EPS by about 12%. That is moderate (FactSet's lease-inclusive basis exaggerates it). The bigger per-share effect over the next 18 months is the share count, not the interest charge: basic shares outstanding rose from about 294m to about 308.4m (+4.9% on an outstanding basis; 311,385,744 issued including about 3m treasury shares) on 1 October 2026, while the GCG profit only arrives from closing.

GCG accretion, measured on my 2027 numbers. GCG EBITA about €121m, less about €32m of interest on the new debt, taxed at 28% = about €64m of net income. Without GCG: about €816m on about 294.9m shares = €2.77. With GCG: €880m on 309.3m = €2.84. Accretion about +2.5% (Jefferies: about 3% pre-synergies). After about €35-45m a year of new PPA amortisation (my estimate; the PPA is not yet published), reported EPS accretion is about -1% to 0%. Return on the €1.2bn invested is about 7% after tax in year one, rising to about 9% if the 13% margin target is reached on about $1.3bn of revenue by 2029: around the cost of capital, so GCG is a strategic and mix decision more than a value-creating one at the price paid (the first set's view, which I share).

Convertible dilution. The OCEANE converts at €50.31 (about 2.68% of capital, about 8m shares). It is far out of the money at €32.63, so it is irrelevant to the share count unless the shares rise above about €50 before May 2031. Under IAS 33 it is nonetheless dilutive in the diluted-EPS calculation (the after-tax interest saved, about €0.40 per incremental share, is below basic EPS), so reported diluted EPS will run about 2.5% below basic. Brokers' adjusted EPS is not stated as basic or diluted in FactSet; the third set should not read a 2-3% basic/diluted gap as a forecast difference.

Balance sheet beyond 2028. At 1.6-1.8x on my numbers, Rexel has €0.5-0.8bn of capacity below a 2.0x ceiling by 2029-30 that is not in my base case. That is upside optionality (larger buybacks, larger acquisitions, or a special return pushed by Cevian, 23%), worth about 3-5% of EPS if used for buybacks at today's price.

5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7756P-0355
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

C+5610th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: about 5-10% of the €3.7bn opex base (€190-370m, about 1-2% of sales) is cuttable over 2-3 years without damaging the franchise, mainly in back-office and customer-service labour through AI, in logistics through automation and hub-and-spoke, and in sub-scale branches (UK, Germany). Delivery, branch staff and rent in core markets are largely fixed in the short run. The precedent (2024-25: -2.3% headcount, €40m of productivity) shows Rexel will cut quickly in a downturn.

Cost line (approx. share of opex) Cuttable over 2-3 years Evidence
Personnel: customer service, call centres, inside sales (about 25-30%) 10-20% through AI order entry and quoting (12,000 customer service staff; 10-15% inside-sales productivity; 20-30% in quoting) Revalgo, RexelGPT; incentives tied to AI adoption

  • Arithmetic. Removing costs worth 1% of sales adds about 16% to EBITA at a 6.2% margin (sector digest). The self-help plan does not need this full amount; 2-3% productivity a year against 2.5% inflation is the requirement.
  • Constraint. Over-cutting hollows out institutional knowledge before a European recovery (Leadership digest). The workforce is old (37% over 50), so attrition gives a natural, painless reduction route.
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

C558th of 38

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Rexel can remove about 5-10% of its €3.7bn opex base over three years (€190-370m gross), but most of it is needed just to offset wage and cost inflation of €80-100m a year. What reaches margin net of inflation and reinvestment is about +15-20bp a year in 2026-28 and about +10bp a year after that: about +60-70bp cumulatively by 2029, of which the UK and Germany turnaround is about 25-30bp and AI-led productivity net of inflation is about 30-40bp. That is enough to carry the margin from about 6.4% (2026) to about 7.0-7.1% by 2029 with GCG's +30bp, but not without it. Morgan Stanley's "productivity +100bp plus country turnarounds +100bp over four years" is a gross figure and overstates what reaches the line, because peers' precedents show savings are largely absorbed by inflation.

The levers, sized and timed.

Lever Scope (gross) Net to margin Timing Confidence
UK and Germany turnaround (about €1bn of revenue at about breakeven to 2% in 2024-25; European average about 6%) €40-60m of EBITA if they reach 4-6% +25-30bp of group margin +10bp 2026 (H1 already +100bp in those countries), +10bp 2027, +5-10bp 2028; stalls if UK and German construction stays weak Medium
AI order entry and quoting (12,000 customer-service and inside-sales staff; management claims 10-15% productivity, Jefferies 20-30% in quoting) €60-120m of labour cost over 3 years about half retained: +15-25bp 2026-28, front-loaded Medium-low on retention
Logistics automation (10 to 20 automated centres) and hub-and-spoke €20-40m +5-10bp after depreciation 2027-29 Medium
Branch rationalisation (UK, Germany, Spain) and attrition (37% of workforce over 50) €20-40m included in the turnaround ongoing Medium
Offsets: wage inflation 2.5-3% on personnel (59% of opex); digital investment (about -10bp a year); IT costs rising (IT maintenance €144.6m in 2024) -€80-100m a year absorbs most of the gross saving every year High

Why only part reaches the line. (1) Rexel's 2025 record shows the arithmetic: headcount -2.3% with volume +0.7% gave productivity of about 3%, yet the price-over-cost spread was -19bp because personnel costs rose 2.9% and selling prices only 1.3%; margin rose only 10bp. (2) RS Group delivered £55m of cumulative savings since April 2023, beating its target, yet adjusted operating costs still rose 2% because of wage inflation and software spending. (3) Digitalisation swaps variable labour for fixed IT cost, which raises operating leverage both ways. (4) Morgan Stanley's distributor survey (8 October 2026): 83% of distributors are adopting AI but 39% see no measurable efficiency gain yet, and the sector digest's rule is that cost-out "stays only where pricing discipline holds"; rivals will buy the same tools within two to three years. I therefore assume half of the AI saving is competed back to customers from 2028.

When it reaches the lines. H1 2026 action plans were +22bp; restructuring costs fell to €2.7m in H1 2026 (from €11.4m), so the cash cost of this phase is mostly spent (€33.1m in 2024 and €41.1m in 2025). I model restructuring and integration cash of about €45m in 2026 and 2027 (GCG integration included) and €30m a year thereafter, excluded from adjusted EBITA but included in my FCF. The net margin contribution I use is +20bp (2026, already largely in H1), +20bp gross in 2027 (about +10bp after the negative price-over-cost spread), +15bp in 2028, +10bp in 2029 and 2030.

What it is not. It is not a lever for a step change. A "massive" cost cut would mean branch closures in the core, which the 2018 programme (UK sales -6.4%) and the 2024 UK closures (UK sales -10.5%) show also cut revenue. The cost case is a steady 10-20bp a year of insulation against inflation, which mainly raises the floor of the cone (the trough margin rose from about 4% to about 6% between past downturns and 2024) rather than its central path.

Disagreement with the first set. The first set (C-78) showed a 2025-28 margin bridge reaching 7.0-7.7% and called consensus 6.9% for 2028 "the low end". On the evidence of how much self-help peers actually retain, I put 2028 at 6.95%, at consensus, not above it. The upside to 7.5% needs either copper staying high or European volume recovering, neither of which is cost elimination.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4760P-0548
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?B608th of 38full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

D4821st of 38

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

The Investment Analyst's heading After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: the 2026 improvement is about half self-limiting and half self-sustaining. The self-limiting half is the commodity and price loop (copper, supplier inflation) and the data-centre growth rate, which are mean-reverting by construction. The self-sustaining half is a set of weak reinforcing loops (digital and AI cost-to-serve, national-account consolidation, GCG's mix) that compound slowly, at about 10-20bp of margin a year. The strongest reinforcing loop of the last cycle, bolt-on acquisitions at about 6x EBITDA, is switched off until 2028 by the balance sheet. The most useful balancing loop for predictability is working capital: it makes free cash flow far steadier than EPS, because a copper fall that cuts EBITA also releases cash.

Splitting 2026 into what repeats and what reverses.

2026 driver Size in 2026 Loop type Behaviour 2027-30
Cable price (copper above $14,000/t) about +2.2-2.5 points of growth; about +30-45bp of margin level against $11,000/t Balancing, mean-reverting Adds nothing at flat copper; reverses if copper falls
Non-cable price (supplier and tariff inflation) about +1.3-1.5 points Weakly balancing (pass-through at constant margin; spread fades) About +1.2-1.5 points a year; spread negative
US data centres (+80-100%) about +1.5 points of group growth Reinforcing for demand, balancing for margin (dilution, tender intensity) About +1 point in 2027, +0.6 points in 2028 as the base grows and comparisons double
Self-help: action plans, UK and Germany about +20bp of margin Reinforcing while savings are retained +10-20bp a year, retention falling as rivals adopt the same tools
Core construction volume about 0 to +0.5% Cyclical +0.5-1% in 2027, about +1-1.5% in 2028 if European recovery starts

The reinforcing loops, ranked by strength over 2027-29.

  1. GCG mix and the specialty pivot (strongest, once closed). Higher-margin engineered revenue (about 11% EBITA, 13% targeted) raises group margin mechanically and gives Rexel a position in white-space and utility projects where its 35-40% share of the electrical bill of materials is less exposed to direct sourcing. Loop: better project capability → more hyperscale and utility framework wins → more kitting volume → more purchasing scale for GCG. Strength: +24bp in 2027, +5-10bp a year of synergies to 2029. Stall condition: hyperscaler capex financing cracks (the AI theme "cracking at its financing, not its demand", per the Fragility digest the first set cited).
  2. Digital and AI cost-to-serve (moderate, time-limited). Digital share rose from 33.7% (H1 2025) to 36% (Q3 2026), with North America at 31% (+604bp in a year). Lower cost per order and stickier EDI and ERP integration support share in the core, and the savings fund the next tools. But Sonepar's Spark platform grew online sales 50% to €12.3bn in 2025 and is ahead in Europe (45%), and AI tools are being bought by everyone. Strength: +10-15bp a year in 2026-28, fading thereafter.
  3. Vendor consolidation onto scale distributors (moderate, durable). Utilities and industrial buyers consolidate onto two or three distributors that can hold stock (steel buyers raised Rexel-related spend up to 16%). This supports volume share but also concentrates buyer power, so it adds volume more than margin.
  4. Bolt-on M&A density loop (strong historically, paused). 21 deals over 2021-25 at about 6x EBITDA. Paused by leverage until 2028; I model €200m in 2028 and €350-400m a year in 2029-30 (+1-1.5 points of revenue growth a year), below the Axelerate 2028 target of 2-3%.
  5. Deleveraging and re-rating (affects the multiple, not the P&L). Precedent: Wesco re-rated from about 7x to about 12x forward EV/EBITDA between early 2023 and late 2026 as leverage fell. This is a fourth-set question; for the model it simply means buybacks restart in 2028.

The balancing loops, ranked by their effect on the lines.

  1. Copper and price mean-reversion (the largest). High copper lifts revenue and margin, but the effect is a level, not a trend. Its year-on-year contribution falls to about zero by mid-2027 at flat prices. This loop explains most of the deceleration in organic growth from about 6.6% (2026) to about 3.4% (2027).
  2. Working capital absorbs inflation and releases it in a downturn. Trade working capital went from about 14.8% to 16.7% of LTM sales between December 2025 and June 2026 (FactSet; H1 2026 trade working-capital outflow €307m). A copper fall of $3,000/t would cut cable prices about 20%, reduce revenue by about €650m and release about €100m of working capital, while EBITA falls by about €40-70m. So free cash flow is countercyclical and steadier than EPS: in my bear case FCF falls by less than 10% while EPS falls by about 15-17%. This matters for D-04 (predictability): FCF and leverage forecasts are more predictable than EPS forecasts.
  3. Wage inflation against productivity. Personnel and delivery are 68% of opex; wages rise 2.5-3% a year whatever the volume. This loop caps the net self-help contribution at 10-20bp a year.
  4. Data-centre growth against margin and competition. Faster data-centre growth means more low-margin direct-ship and tendered projects (Wesco is about four times Rexel's size in the segment), diluting gross margin by about 5bp a year.
  5. AI savings competed away. I assume half of the AI saving is passed back from 2028.

Net effect on the lines. Revenue: organic growth roughly halves from about 6.6% (2026) to about 3.4% (2027) as the balancing loops (copper, data-centre comparisons) dominate, then settles at about 3.5-4% (2028-30) where slow reinforcing loops and a modest European recovery dominate. Margin: +12bp organic in 2027 (balancing and reinforcing roughly cancel), +20-25bp a year thereafter. Cash: working capital absorbs about €190m (organic) in 2026, about €115m in 2027, then about €60-100m a year, so FCF converts at about 75% of net income in 2026 and about 85-90% from 2027.

Where I disagree with the AlphaSense report. The feedback-loop report concludes with "high confidence" that reinforcing loops dominate and cites a "14% EPS CAGR through 2029" (Morgan Stanley). That rests on copper and data-centre growth staying at their 2026 rates of change. On my decomposition, recurring EPS compounds at about 10.6% a year from 2025 to 2030, and the reinforcing loops are not strong enough to repeat 2026's organic growth.

C-85

To what extent is informal/qualitative company commentary above or below formal guidance and sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

B668th of 38

Layer 1 · Guidance

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is informal or qualitative company commentary above or below formal guidance and sell-side consensus, and why?

Judgement: informal commentary is more bullish than formal guidance and broadly in line with or slightly above consensus. The CEO says "6% has become what we deliver"; the >7% margin "should be achieved between 2027 and 2029 without meaningful volume recovery"; Rexel is "perfectly on track"; data-centre backlog gives "unusually strong visibility"; AI productivity is "ahead of plan". Against that, management repeatedly flags "prudence on construction markets", US ex-data centres "slightly negative", and no European recovery assumed. Read: confident on self-help and margin, cautious on volume. This suggests upside to consensus margin rather than to consensus revenue.

  • Why: a self-help narrative lets management promise margin independent of the cycle while keeping the volume assumption low. This matches the bonus design (margin and working capital) and the conservative-guider style.
C-73

What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

A553rd of 38

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: several points matter for the model and the calendar. (1) Rexel reports same-day organic growth and current adjusted EBITA (excluding the copper effect), so reported sales and EBITA differ materially from guided measures (calendar, currency, scope, copper). (2) Results are semi-annual with quarterly sales updates; the next full-year results are on 10 February 2027 and should include GCG and the first 2027 outlook. (3) The copper assumption is published, which allows the market to "mark" guidance to spot. (4) Cash conversion uses EBITDAaL (after leases), unlike FactSet's FCF. (5) IR is open about weak spots, which builds credibility.

  • Uncommon: guidance explicitly independent of volume recovery; a capital-markets-day mid-term target (June 2024) re-dated rather than restated; the €48m judicial guarantee is disclosed but not quantified as a risk to guidance.

  • Wisdom. "Composite signal masks component deterioration": decompose revisions into organic, scope and copper before treating momentum A as fundamental. "SS buy rating vs revisions divergence": no divergence now; ratings and revisions align upward.
