Risks

C5515th of 38

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The financial forecasts for the next 18 months are more predictable than the second set concluded, for three reasons: the FY2026 outcome is now about 90% known and sits above the raised guidance; the first half of 2027 rests on a 3.4-month backlog, a mechanical GCG contribution and copper already in the price books; and the company's disclosure lets each driver be checked within one reporting cycle. They are less predictable than a low-risk case in three respects: the drivers of 2027 are concentrated on GCG, the copper level and hyperscale data-centre capex, which are correlated through the AI and electrification trade; the operating leverage is asymmetric, so a deflationary reversal would take the margin down three to four times faster than growth lifted it; and the EPS line carries policy and basis noise (the French surcharge, the share count, PPA treatment) that will make the February 2027 headline look worse than the operating delivery. The cone for FY2027 recurring EPS is about -19% to +13% around €2.97 (model basis), skewed down; the cone for FCF and leverage is tight in every scenario. The share price has de-coupled from its peers (84% idiosyncratic over six months) and sits on company-specific overhangs that are time-limited, which makes the identified operating milestones the share-price catalysts as well. The earlier sets were congruent on the logic of the case and jointly too conservative on the current run-rate; the Consumer should carry a fatter upside tail on 2027 volume than their text implies while keeping their downside mechanics. For the memo's risk topic (#9): the case is simple to state (price, data centres, GCG, self-help), its fragilities are few and observable (copper below $12,000/t, backlog below 2.8 months, a French surcharge enlargement, GCG slippage), the risks to the share price are more sudden than the risks to earnings (France, AI-capex sentiment, read-across from Wesco and Fastenal), and the sector precedents (2015, 2022, 2024) say the downside, when it comes, arrives within two quarters.

C-79

How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

C+5010th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: geographically narrowing (17 countries after exits; the US, France and Canada are more than 65% of sales), product-wise broadening (engineered GCG, automation, wireless, cooling assemblies). Predictability improves on geography but worsens on mix: more of the growth will come from project- and capex-driven verticals (data centres, utilities, defence) that are lumpier than the branch flow business. On balance predictability is moderate and roughly unchanged.

  • Perimeter. Europe (France, Benelux, DACH, UK and Ireland, Nordics, Italy, Spain), North America (US in eight regional divisions; Canada), Asia-Pacific (Australia, China, India). Finland, New Zealand and the Middle East exited. Three regions, about a dozen clusters.
  • Lines. Core electrical distribution (warehouse €15.8bn, direct €3.9bn in FactSet's segments); specialties: GCG ($1.1bn), Talley ($0.36bn), Revere ($0.33bn), data-centre and automation services. Specialty share rises toward about 15-20% of North America.
  • Predictability. Flow business: 3-4 month visibility, stable margin. Project business: lumpier, lower gross margin, dependent on hyperscaler capex. Copper adds a commodity overlay to 15-17% of sales. Currency translation adds ±2-4% a year to reported growth.
D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

D6424th of 38

Layer 3 · Risks (the third set)

Judgement: about two-thirds of the expected change (6.0% to above 7% adjusted EBITA margin, recurring EPS compounding at 10-12% a year, FCF rising toward €1bn, leverage back below 2x) rests on strengths Rexel has demonstrated in its own numbers over 2019-2026: prompt and complete price pass-through, cost discipline through downturns, working-capital control, bolt-on integration, data-centre execution and the ability to raise guidance from a sandbagged base. About one-third rests on strengths it still has to prove: integrating an engineering-led $1.4bn platform (GCG) and lifting it to a 13% margin, retaining AI productivity against competition, and taking the UK and Germany from near breakeven to the European average. The Q3 print moved the balance toward the demonstrated side, because volume-led growth of 6.3% with a record backlog and data-centre profitability near regional margins is evidence, not promise. The plausibility is therefore good for the 2026-2027 part of the change and moderate for the 2028-2029 part.

  • Demonstrated strengths, with the evidence that they are strengths and not luck.
  • Pass-through pricing. Selling prices +5.1% in Q3 2026 (cable +2.9%, non-cable +2.2%), +3.3% in H1; the delta between selling-price inflation and opex inflation returned to +6bp in H1 2026 from -19bp in 2025; gross margin rose from 24.9% (2024) to 25.4% (H1 2026); Jefferies (12 October 2025) records positive margin impacts of 4-20% on certain categories from past US tariffs. The precedent (2021: +196bp of margin on 15.6% same-day growth with 5.2 points of cable price) shows the mechanism works at scale.
  • Cost discipline in downturns. Trough margins of 5.7% (2012), 4.9-5.0% (2015, 2019), 5.9% (2024) against 4.2% in older cycles; headcount -2.3% in 2025; record 4% productivity in H1 2026; €40m productivity plan delivered. The floor has risen about 200bp over two cycles (Bernstein, 6 January 2026).
  • Working capital. 14-15% of sales through the cycle against Wesco at about 20%; inventory 56.5 days; FCF conversion above 65% in each of the last three years including the fine year.
  • Bolt-on integration. 21-26 deals since 2021 at about 6x EBITDA; Talley ahead of plan; Revere and Techno-Contact 360 integrated in 2026; the 2012 and 2015 precedents of defending French and European gross margins in volume declines (Morgan Stanley, 13 February 2013: European gross margin +60bp on -5.5% volume).
  • Data-centre execution. From about 5% of US sales (late 2025) to 13% (Q3 2026), triple-digit growth, 100 active projects with none delayed, 18,000 square metres of new hub capacity in Atlanta, Mesa and Reno, and, importantly, Bernstein's 22 October observation that North American data-centre profitability is approaching the regional margin level. The first set's fear that this growth is structurally dilutive is weakening.

  • Guidance behaviour. Six consecutive quarters of sequential improvement, two raises in 2026, every guided year since 2023 met or beaten after the one honest 2024 cut.

  • Strengths to be acquired, with the precedents that set the odds.

  • GCG integration to a 13% margin by 2030 (11% now). The positive precedents are Wesco-Anixter (cost synergies raised from $200m to $315m; sales synergies from $170m targeted to $2.3bn captured by 2023; leverage from 5.7x to 2.8x in three years) and Beijer Ref-Heritage (margin-accretive from day one, but cash conversion fell to -45% in early 2023 and first-year margins were diluted by integration costs). The negative precedents are Rexel's own 2018 programme (€90m of restructuring; German sales -15.9% and UK -6.4% in the restructuring year) and the expert view that engineering-led acquisitions resist centralised platforms and take up to three years to yield synergies. GCG's "11% organic CAGR since 2019" also includes twelve add-ons under Audax (own-3 report), so its standalone organic rate is lower than the headline. Odds that GCG delivers the 20bp of group margin in year one: high (it is mechanical once closed); odds that it reaches 13% by 2029-30: moderate (about 50%).
  • Retained AI productivity. 10-15% inside-sales productivity is claimed; the Q3 2026 distributor survey shows rising scepticism across the sector; SSI Research (2 June 2026) and Morgan Stanley (28 July 2026) expect savings to leak to customers within two to three years. I treat this as a floor-raiser worth 15-25bp cumulatively, not the 30-40bp the first set carried.

  • UK and Germany. More than +100bp of margin in H1 2026 in both countries, and Germany +6.4% same-day in Q3, are real progress; but the UK fell 3.8% in H1 on a construction market with starts down 29%, and the RS Group Asia-Pacific precedent (restructured in FY2016, breakeven only in FY2019) says three years is the norm. Odds of reaching the European average (about 6%) by 2028: about 40%; of adding the 25-30bp in my bridge by 2028: about 60%.

  • The arithmetic of reliance. Of the +100bp from 6.0% (2025) to 7.00% (2028) in my base: copper level and pass-through +35-40bp (demonstrated; reversible), volume leverage +35bp (demonstrated mechanism, cyclical input), self-help net of inflation +25bp (half demonstrated through the 2026 action plans, half to be proven), GCG +22-27bp (mechanical mix once closed, then synergies to be proven), offsets -40bp (digital investment, project dilution, price-over-cost). Strengths the business has carry about 65-70% of the gross bridge; strengths to be acquired carry about 30-35%. On revenue the reliance on acquired strengths is lower (GCG's revenue is bought, not built), on EPS it is higher (the deleveraging and buyback restart that lift 2028-30 EPS depend on GCG's cash conversion).

  • What would most undermine plausibility. A copper reversal to $11,000/t would remove the single largest demonstrated contributor (about 35-45bp) within two to three quarters and would test the cost-discipline strength again at the same time as GCG integration consumes management bandwidth. That joint event, not any single acquired strength failing, is the main threat to the "plausible on demonstrated strengths" conclusion.

D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

D4223rd of 38

Layer 3 · Risks (the third set)

Judgement: the cone is narrow for the next six months, moderate for the first half of 2027 and wide for the second half of 2027. On FY2027 recurring EPS (model basis) it runs from about €2.42 (bear) to about €3.36 (bull) around a base of €2.97: -19% and +13%, so skewed to the downside and with a faster downside path. On FY2027 EBITA it runs from about €1,335m to €1,690m around €1,560m (-14%, +8%). On free cash flow and leverage the cone is remarkably tight (about €700-780m of FCF and 1.8-2.3x leverage at end-2027 in every scenario), because a copper or volume downturn releases working capital while it cuts profit. The case is therefore predictable at the operating-cash and balance-sheet level, moderately predictable at the EBITA level, and only moderately predictable at the EPS level, where tax, PPA and the share count add noise that has nothing to do with the business. The width is a little narrower than the second set's because FY2026 is now about 90% delivered and the first half of 2027 is backed by a 3.4-month backlog and a mechanical GCG contribution; the skew is unchanged.

  • The next six months (to the FY2026 results on 10 February 2027): narrow. FY2026 same-day growth between +7.0% and +7.6% (Q4 between +5.5% and +9%), adjusted EBITA margin between 6.42% and 6.58% (EBITA €1,335-1,370m), recurring EPS on the model basis €2.62-2.72 (brokers' basis €2.51-2.61). The only material swing items are Q4 copper (already mostly in the price books), the exact H2 drop-through, and the closing date of GCG (a slip into Q1 2027 changes FY2026 by nothing and FY2027 revenue by about €80-250m). The probability that FY2026 guidance is met or beaten is about 90% (I-01).

  • FY2027: the three scenarios, with what has to happen.

Bear (about 20%) Base (about 55%) Bull (about 25%)
Same-day organic growth about -1.5% (core volume -2%, copper averages about $11,000/t so cable price about -3 points, US data centres +10% on a financing pause, Europe -2%) +4.5% (H1 +5.5%, H2 +3.5%; copper about $13,700/t flat; data centres +30-40%; non-cable price +1.7%) about +7.5% (European construction recovery adds 2-3 points, data centres +50%, copper about $15,500/t, non-cable price +2.5%)

  • Why the skew is to the downside on EPS. Three asymmetries. (1) Deflation drops through at 35-40bp of margin per point of organic decline when price falls with volume, against 5-7bp per point on the way up (P-01's measurement, confirmed by the H2 2026 drop-through). (2) The copper tailwind is a level effect already in the base: at a flat $13,700/t it adds nothing in 2027 but a fall to $11,000/t removes 30-45bp within two to three quarters (the 2015 and H2 2022 precedents in the own-4 report; the 2022 inventory effect swung from +€68m to -€23.7m in a year). (3) The tax line can only hurt against consensus (27% assumed) and can only help against my base by about 2 points. Against that, the upside tail is fatter than the earlier sets' prose implied: Rexel has beaten its own run-rate in three of the last four quarters, the backlog has risen 0.7 months in nine months, and supplier list prices (Schneider +5.5% in Q3 2026, Legrand targets raised at its September 2026 capital markets day) are still rising, which supports non-cable price into H1 2027. I therefore weight the bull scenario at 25% against the bear's 20%, which leaves the probability-weighted EPS at about €2.96, essentially the base.

  • Why FCF and leverage are the predictable lines. Working capital is about 15% of sales. A 1% fall in revenue from price releases about €30m of cash while removing about €10m of EBITA (at the 35-40bp slope on a smaller base); a 1% rise from volume absorbs about €30m while adding about €8-10m. That balancing loop (P-05) is why FCF in the bear case is within €20m of the base and leverage stays below 2.3x, comfortably inside the 3.5x covenant and the ratings' comfort zone. The Consumer can therefore treat the deleveraging path (about 1.9x at end-2027) and the dividend as high-confidence, and the buyback restart as a 2028 event in all but the bear case.

  • What the earlier sets missed, and the widest single uncertainty. The French Finance Bill is now the single largest controllable swing in the 2027 EPS cone against consensus (about €0.09 a share on the brokers' basis between lapse and full extension), and it resolves within about two months. The widest fundamental uncertainty remains the H2 2027 organic rate: the comparison base (H2 2026 at about +9.4%) is the hardest Rexel has faced since 2022, and European construction indicators (Eurozone construction PMI 43.4 in September 2026; French INSEE new-housing demand balance -43 in October; the US Architecture Billings Index at 44.7 in September; US commercial planning ex data centres -15% year on year) give no support yet. My H2 2027 figure of +3.5% assumes data centres still add about 1 point, non-cable price about 1.2 points and core volume about 1.5 points, with cable price slightly negative; a core-volume outcome of zero would take H2 2027 to about +2% and FY2027 to about +3.7%, costing about €0.05 of EPS. That is the most likely form of a "miss", and it would arrive in the October 2027 Q3 release, outside the window in which guidance can protect the shares.

  • Predictability summary by horizon. To June 2027: high (FY2026 known; H1 2027 backed by backlog, GCG arithmetic and copper already in the price books). July to December 2027: moderate (copper and data-centre comparisons, European volume, the first GCG synergies, French post-election policy). The 18-month cone is wide enough that the memo should not describe the case as low-risk, but it is predictable in the sense that its drivers are few, dated and observable (D-03), and its cash and balance-sheet outcomes are robust.

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

A721st of 38

Layer 3 · Risks (the third set)

Judgement: very helpful, among the best in the European capital-goods cohort for the drivers that matter here, with four gaps that matter for this particular case. Rexel discloses quarterly same-day growth by region split into volume, cable price and non-cable price; the copper assumption behind guidance; the data-centre share of US sales and the backlog in months; the digital share by region; and, at the half-year, a margin bridge by lever (operating leverage, action plans, gross margin, digital investment), the delta between selling-price inflation and opex inflation, working-capital days, the non-recurring copper effect, and a reported-to-recurring net income reconciliation. That is enough to test every leg of the thesis within one reporting cycle. The gaps are: no quarterly margin (so Q4 2026 and Q1 2027 give sales only); no GCG segment disclosure until the FY2026 results at the earliest; no UK or German margin disclosure, so the turnaround is tracked only through management's qualitative comments and country sales; and a copper effect that is extrapolated from a 70% sample of cable sales. The next six months contain two scheduled prints (Q4 2026 sales and FY2026 results on 10 February 2027; Q1 2027 sales in late April 2027) and three external decisions (the French Finance Bill vote, GCG closing, the US EO 14420 implementing rules on 24 December 2026) that together cover the main uncertainties.

  • What can be monitored directly, and the reading that would confirm or break the base case.

  • External monitors that lead Rexel's own disclosure. Wesco's Q4 2026 results (February 2027) for data-centre growth and backlog (its backlog was +60% year on year at Q2 2026); Graybar's quarterly 10-Q (record sales +22% in Q3 2026 but gross margin down 50bp to 18.9%, the read-across for US pricing pressure); Fastenal's gross margin (Rexel's North American gross margin "follows Fastenal directionally" per Morgan Stanley; Fastenal's fell 40bp in Q3 2026 on large-account mix); Schneider's Q4 pricing (5.5% in Q3) and Legrand's quarterly organic growth; French housing starts (+31% year on year in September 2026, leading Rexel's European organic growth by about one quarter per Morgan Stanley) and permits (flat); the Eurozone and German construction PMIs; the US Architecture Billings Index (leads non-residential spending by nine to twelve months; 44.7 in September) and the Dodge Momentum Index; LME copper and the three-month forward ($14,327/t on 27 October 2026).

  • Why the disclosure is unusually useful for this case. The thesis turns on composition (price against volume, data centres against core, GCG against organic) and Rexel discloses composition. Most cohort peers disclose organic growth as a single figure. The copper assumption lets the market mark guidance to spot and so removes most of the "surprise" from copper moves. The delta-inflation line is a direct read of the price-over-cost spread that the second set identified as the margin's fragility.

  • Where the Consumer will be blind. (1) GCG's standalone organic growth, customer concentration (hyperscaler share) and backlog are not disclosed, and the "11% organic CAGR" includes add-ons; the first useful reading is H1 2027. (2) The UK and German turnaround is disclosed only as "more than 100bp" improvements; without country margins the Consumer cannot separate restructuring gains from cost allocation. (3) Quarterly margins are not reported, so the price-over-cost spread is observable only twice a year; the Q4 sales release will say nothing about H2 drop-through until 10 February. (4) The non-recurring copper effect is measured on a 70% sample, so restatements are possible. (5) Working capital at the quarter is not disclosed; the countercyclical cash cushion in D-04 is visible only at the half-year.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

C+5210th of 38

Layer 3 · Risks (the third set)

Judgement: the trace from what the company promises to the revenue and EBITA lines is clear and quantified; the trace from EBITA to earnings per share is the weak link, because the company guides nothing below operating profit and the market carries at least three EPS bases. The external transmission is clear for copper (a dated, quantified chain with a two-month lag), reasonably clear for supplier pricing and the data-centre build-out, and only loosely quantified for construction indicators (a three-to-twelve-month lead that has shortened since the pandemic). For the memo this means the milestones to track are operating ones (same-day growth and its split, the margin and its bridge, the backlog, GCG's parameters), and that EPS should be presented on a stated basis with an explicit bridge from EBITA, or it will be mis-compared with consensus.

  • The internal trace, line by line.
  • Revenue. Guidance is same-day growth; the disclosed split (volume, cable price, non-cable price) maps directly onto the model's revenue drivers, and scope, currency and calendar are disclosed separately each quarter. GCG's revenue ("more than $1.1bn in 2026") is a stated parameter. Milestones: Q4 2026 same-day growth about +6% implied by guidance; FY2027 outlook on 10 February 2027; GCG's first consolidated quarter in Q1 2027.
  • Gross margin and opex. Not guided, but the half-year bridge separates operating leverage, action plans (productivity), gross-margin mix and digital investment, and the delta-inflation line gives the price-over-cost spread. Milestones: H1 2027 bridge; the delta-inflation reading; productivity rate (4% in H1 2026).
  • Adjusted EBITA margin. Guided for the year (at least 6.4%) and mid-term (above 7% between 2027 and 2029 "without meaningful volume recovery"); GCG's contribution is stated (more than 20bp in year one; 11% margin rising to about 13% with synergies). This is the cleanest line in the trace. Milestone: whether the FY2027 outlook names a margin at or above 6.7%.
  • Cash. Guided as FCF before interest and tax above 65% of EBITDAaL; leverage guided at about 2.0x from or by end-2027; dividend policy at least 40% of recurring net income; buybacks conditional on the leverage target. Milestones: end-2026 and end-2027 leverage; the dividend declared in February 2027.

  • EPS: the weak link. Rexel guides a tax rate informally (about 30% for 2026, about 27% from 2027 "in the absence of renewal") and nothing on net financial expense, PPA amortisation or the share count. Four EPS bases circulate: reported basic (€2.00 in 2025), company recurring (€2.30; after PPA, before the surcharge), brokers' adjusted (€2.19; after PPA, after the surcharge, with some houses adding back PPA), and the model's (before PPA, after the surcharge). GCG will add about €35-45m of PPA amortisation a year and about €32m of interest; the placing added 4.9% to the share count. None of these is "guided", so the EPS milestone the market will judge in February 2027 is poorly defined. The memo should state its EPS basis and carry the EBITA-to-EPS bridge (net financial items, tax rate, PPA, share count) explicitly.

  • The external transmission, ranked by clarity. 1. Copper to cable price to revenue and margin (clear, quantified, dated). Cables are about 16% of sales (17% with GCG); copper is about 60% of cable cost; cable selling prices follow LME with about a two-month lag through supplier repricing and Rexel's two-month inventory turn; each $1,000/t moves organic growth by about 1 point and the adjusted EBITA margin by 10-15bp; the inventory revaluation is reported separately as non-recurring. The 2009 management rule of thumb ($500/t = €110m of sales and €15m of EBITA) still matches today's sensitivity. Transmission time: one to two quarters. 2. Supplier list prices to non-cable price (clear in direction, lagged in size). Schneider priced +5.5% in Q3 2026 and Legrand +3.6%; Rexel passes rises of about 3% through with a lag of a few months on large projects and at a constant percentage margin, so the gross profit effect is proportional and the spread over opex inflation (about 2.6%) is what reaches the margin. Transmission time: one to three quarters. 3. Hyperscaler capex to data-centre sales (clear in direction, lumpy in timing). Order intake appears in the backlog (months of sales and the data-centre share of it) one to three quarters before revenue; grid-connection and equipment lead times (transformers, switchgear) can defer conversion; data-centre project gross margins are below the proximity business but, per Bernstein, now approaching regional margins. Transmission time: two to four quarters; visibility about 18 months per the backlog. 4. Construction indicators to core volume (direction clear, timing loose). French housing starts lead Rexel's European organic growth by about one quarter; building permits historically led electrical sales by twelve months, compressed to about three months since the pandemic (Jefferies, 20 October 2026); the US Architecture Billings Index leads non-residential spending by nine to twelve months; distributor volumes lag PMIs by three to twelve months. The lag is short enough that a 2027 inflection would show in the same year, but the indicators are weak and mixed, so this leg carries the least precision. 5. Subsidies and regulation to electrification volume (least clear). German KfW subsidy caps step down from February 2027; French €10bn electrification funding is less than 5% disbursed; EPBD transposition is late everywhere; heat-pump demand fell 46% in Germany in 2024 on policy uncertainty. Direction is positive over years, but quarter-to-quarter transmission is unpredictable, as management said on the Q3 call.

  • Insight for the memo. The company's promises trace cleanly to EBITA and cash, and the external chains trace cleanly to revenue; the only place the trace blurs is between EBITA and EPS, which is exactly where our forecast differs from consensus (tax, share count, PPA). That is both the risk (a basis-driven "miss" headline in February 2027) and the opportunity (the operating beat is real and the below-the-line items are one-off or policy-driven, not franchise-driven).

C-82

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

C+3212th of 38

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: sentiment risk is high now. Rexel is a cheap, geared, French-listed cyclical in a risk-off regime, held partly as a "second-tier data-centre wave" play, with a fresh placing overhang. The most dangerous sentiment events are (1) an AI capex or credit scare hitting the whole data-centre basket; (2) a French spread blow-out; (3) read-across from US distributors (Wesco, Fastenal, Watsco) on gross margin or data-centre growth; (4) a copper sell-off; (5) Cevian selling its 23%, which would create a large overhang and remove the takeover floor narrative.

  • Evidence that sentiment already drives the stock. -11.8% over three months after the placing while 2027 EPS was upgraded about 4%. Technical momentum D; price below 50-, 100- and 200-day averages; FfF Momentum Underweight; Fragility and Country (France) Double Underweight. The electrical and climate wholesaler cohort is the only falling cohort in its sector.
  • Read-across precedents. Rexel's North American gross margin "follows Fastenal directionally" (Morgan Stanley: a 50bp Fastenal contraction in Q1 2026 was flagged). Wesco's results drive the data-centre debate. Legrand fell 11% in a day on disappointing growth.
  • Takeover narrative. QXO, CVC and Cevian support a floor. If Cevian (since 2016) chooses to exit by placing instead, that floor turns into an overhang.
  • Issue price anchor. €34.75 is a resistance level until results reset the narrative (Wisdom Library: "issue price as technical anchor").

C-61How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?C+7212th of 38full answer under 4b Management quality
C-70

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

B728th of 38

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: the sandbagging precision archetype. Set beatable initial ranges, raise floors in small steps as visibility improves ("around" to "at least"; 3-5% to about 5%), deliver at or slightly above the top, and cut quickly and honestly when conditions turn (October 2024). Under Texier, misses have not clustered and upgrades are frequent but incremental, never a big jump.

  • Record. 2023: raised, delivered 4.3% same-day and record conversion. 2024: cut in October (European construction, solar deflation), landed exactly at the revised 5.9%. 2025: cash conversion raised mid-year, delivered 76% against above 65%; same-day 2.5% against "stable to slightly positive". 2026: confirmed in April despite a Q1 miss; raised in July and again in October.
  • Behaviour. Guidance assumptions are explicit (copper price). Wide bands. Commentary keeps "prudence on construction". The mid-term target is reaffirmed and re-dated, not raised.
C-69

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

C6213th of 38

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: a good record. Against guidance over 1-5 years: met or beaten every year except the October 2024 downgrade (a deteriorating environment), after which the revised guidance was met exactly. Against consensus: a mix of beats (FY2025 growth, Q2 2026 sales and margin) and small misses (Q1 2026 sales by about 1%; the Q3 2024 slowdown). In comparable upswing conditions (2021-22 inflation; 2026), Rexel beat both guidance and consensus repeatedly.

  • One year. Q1 2026 missed consensus (3.4% against 4.3%, weather); Q2 2026 beat (6.7% against 5.4%; EBITA €614.8m against €604m); H1 margin 6.2% against 6.1%; FY2026 guidance raised twice; Q3 described as "significant acceleration".
  • Three years. 2023: beat or in line. 2024: guidance cut, then in line. 2025: beat on growth, in line on margin, beat on cash.
  • Five years. 2020: guidance suspended (COVID). 2021-22: strong beats on inflation.
P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

B606th of 38

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: weight the FY2026 forecast highly (about 85-90% of the year is known after Q3, and management has raised guidance twice); lean it slightly upward on margin. Weight the FY2027 forecast moderately: the GCG contribution is mechanical once the deal closes, but organic growth and margin depend on copper and European construction, where Rexel's record shows it cannot outrun its markets. Lean 2027 slightly upward on margin and downward on EPS against consensus (tax, share count, revenue base). Weight 2028-30 low: they rest on a European recovery and on self-help retention, which no indicator yet supports. Lean them neutral, with a skew to the downside on the path and to the upside on capital returns.

What the record says.

  • Against its own guidance: reliable, conservative, symmetric in honesty. 2023 margin 6.8% against 6.6-6.9%; 2024 cut in October and then met exactly (5.9%, same-day -2.4% against -2.5% to -2%); 2025 same-day +2.5% against "stable to slightly positive" and margin 6.0% against "about 6.0%", cash conversion 76% (ex fine) against "above 65%"; 2026 confirmed in April after a Q1 miss, raised in July (same-day "about 5%", margin "at least 6.2%"), raised again in October. In upswings, the beat is typically +0.5-1 point of organic growth and +10-20bp of margin. Rexel's guidance is a floor in upswings and a reliable mid-point after a cut.
  • Against consensus: mixed, and the misses come from volume, not margin. Q1 2026 missed consensus organic growth (3.4% against 4.3%, weather); Q2 beat (6.7% against 5.4%; EBITA €614.8m against €604m). In 2024 the Q3 slowdown missed. Margin forecasts have been more accurate than revenue forecasts.
  • Against mid-term targets: the margin target has been re-dated, not raised. The above-7% target (capital markets day, June 2024) is now "between 2027 and 2029, without meaningful volume recovery". The only time Rexel exceeded 7% (2022, 7.3%) was in an inflation spike. Its record of country turnarounds (2018 programme, €82.5m of restructuring; UK and Germany still near breakeven seven years later) argues for patience on the UK and Germany.
  • Against the cycle: it cannot outrun its markets. Three-year revenue CAGR of 2.2% against Wesco 8.6%, Graybar 8.0%, Sonepar 6.0%. Organic growth has tracked European construction and copper. That is why the volume legs of the forecast carry less weight than the margin legs.

How much weight, by year and line.

Line FY2026 FY2027 FY2028-30
Revenue High (±1%) Medium (±4%: copper ±3 points, GCG timing, Europe) Low (±6-8%)
Adjusted EBITA margin High (±10bp) Medium (6.0-7.05%) Low (6.2-7.6%)

Which way to lean, and why.

  • FY2026: up, slightly. H2 copper above $14,000/t against the $11,000/t guidance assumption, "significant acceleration" in Q3, and the sandbagging-precision style point to the margin landing at 6.35-6.45% rather than at the "at least 6.2%" floor. Revenue: consensus (€20,843m) looks about €400m too high unless GCG is consolidated from Q4; Jefferies (€20,501m) and UBS (€20,451m) are on my basis. A revenue "miss" against consensus with an EBITA beat is the likely FY2026 headline (10 February 2027).
  • FY2027: margin up a little, EPS down against consensus. My EBITA margin (6.68%) is above consensus (6.59%) because GCG's mix is mechanical and Rexel's margin record is good. My EPS (€2.84) is about 5% below consensus (€3.00): about -3% from tax (I assume the French surcharge is partly extended; the draft 2027 Finance Bill of 1 October 2026 proposes an extension, and Jefferies on 25 October 2026 began modelling a full recurrence for exposed French companies), about -1.4% from shares (309m outstanding after the placing against about 305m implied by consensus, which still contains pre-placing estimates such as UBS of 28 July 2026), and about -0.7% from the lower 2026 revenue base.
  • FY2028-30: neutral on the central path, skewed down on timing. The >7% target in my base is reached in 2029 (7.10%), within the company's window but a year later than Jefferies, who argues it is achievable by 2027.

For the third set: the forecast's weakest joint. It is FY2027 organic growth (3.4% in my base), because it depends on three drivers that all fade together (copper comparisons, data-centre growth rates, the 2026 price spread) while the replacement driver (European construction) has no supporting indicator (Eurozone construction PMI 43.4 in September 2026, seventh month of contraction; Jefferies reports European construction in contraction for the 53rd consecutive month).

Rexel's next four years are a credible, moderately positive financial path: about 5.5% revenue growth, about +120bp of margin to about 7.2% by 2030, recurring EPS compounding at about 10-11% a year, and free cash flow nearly doubling to about €1bn, with leverage back below 2x from 2028. The path is less inflation-dependent than 2019-22 but more execution-dependent, and in 2027 it is concentrated: GCG's consolidation and the copper level together explain most of the change from 2026, while the organic engine slows to about 3.4% as copper comparisons, data-centre growth rates and the 2026 price spread fade at the same time. Against consensus, the forecasts agree on operations (EBITA within 1% in each year) but disagree on what sits below operating profit: consensus 2027 EPS (€3.00) appears to assume the French tax surcharge lapses, uses a share count about 1.4% too low, and rests on a 2026 revenue base that probably includes part of GCG. On my numbers consensus 2027 EPS is about 5% too high, and the risk is that the gap closes downward at the February 2027 results rather than through upgrades. For the third set: the margin and cash forecasts deserve more weight than the revenue and EPS forecasts; the cone for 2027 EPS is about €2.35-3.25 and skewed down; and the single most important missing fact is the Q3 2026 print.

D4023rd of 38

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: below operating profit, the next 18 months are a drag on EPS (tax stays high, interest rises with GCG, the share count is up about 5% with no buybacks), and from 2028 a modest help (lower tax, deleveraging, buybacks resuming). Net income grows a little faster than EBITA after 2027; EPS grows a little slower than net income until 2028. Free cash flow is where the improvement against the past is largest: FCF after leases rises from about €450-575m (2025, with and without the €124m fine) to about €735m (2027) and about €910m (2028), driven by EBITA growth and a slowing of working-capital absorption. Reported (IFRS) EPS will run €0.20-0.30 below recurring EPS from 2027 because of GCG's PPA amortisation and integration costs, which will make reported EPS growth look weak in 2027.

The €48m judicial cash guarantee is a contingent outflow, not in the base. A successful antitrust appeal would return up to €124m, not in the base (upside, timing unknown). GCG PPA amortisation: about €35-45m a year (my estimate; acquired intangibles not yet published) on top of about €48m for existing deals, so about €80-90m from 2027; it lowers reported EPS by about €0.20 from 2027. The non-recurring copper effect is a loss if copper falls; excluded from adjusted EBITA.

Judgement: plausible scope of about +100-150bp of EBITA margin by 2028-29 (to about 7.0-7.5%) and working-capital release potential of about 1 point of sales, from a handful of big areas (UK and Germany turnaround, GCG mix and synergies, US specialty mix, AI and digital productivity) plus many small ones. C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

B+747th of 38

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: no deceleration. The engine has accelerated over the last twelve months: same-day growth went from about +2-3% (H2 2025) to +3.4% (Q1 2026), +6.7% (Q2) and higher again in Q3, above Rexel's normal 3-5% organic rate. But the inputs driving it are at or near their peak rate of change: copper (+42% year on year in Q2), data-centre growth (above 100% in Q2), and supplier price rises. So the second derivative turns negative in 2027 even if levels stay high. The deceleration risk is ahead, not behind.

  • Inputs. Copper's year-on-year change will compress from about +40% to about 0% by mid-2027 at flat prices. Data-centre comparisons double. European HVAC benefited from heat waves that "may not repeat". Supplier pricing (Schneider 4.2%, Legrand 3.6%) is at a cyclical high.
  • Offsets. US industrial automation is just turning (+20% in Q3); European volumes only turned positive in Q2; GCG adds about 11% organic growth once in the base; a European construction recovery has not started.
  • Model implication. H2 2026 organic growth is likely the peak (about 6-8%). 2027 is likely 3-5%, with a quarterly profile decelerating through the year as copper comparisons lap (Q3-Q4 2027 weakest). Watch for the "false friend": rising backlog and copper-inflated sales masking flat core volumes.
C-67

How do the sell-side's forecasts for the next one to three years compare with the last one to five years, for the main financial statement line items in particular and the granular working up to them, and how credible is the gap given the change forces and foundations found above?

Judgement: consensus forecasts a clear break from the last three years: revenue growth of 7%, 9% and 4% against about 1% a year in 2023-25; EBIT margin of 6.3%, 6.6% and 6.9% against 5.7-5.8% (FactSet) or 5.9-6.0% (company); EPS growth of 16%, 19% and 10% against -27% and +11%. The gap is largely credible because it is mostly identified (GCG, bolt-ons, self-help, organic recovery already visible in 2026). The least credible pieces are FY2026 H2 revenue composition, FY2027 organic growth as copper and data-centre comparisons fade, and the basis of 2027 EPS (tax and amortisation).

P-09

After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

C-5419th of 38

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the next four years look like a steadier, slower-growing but higher-quality version of 2019-25: revenue grows at about the same nominal rate (about 5.5% a year, with GCG doing the work price did in 2021-22), the margin is about 100bp higher on average and less dependent on inflation, cash generation nearly doubles from its 2021-25 average, and leverage returns to below 2x. EPS compounds at about 10-11% a year, in line with the 2019-25 CAGR but from a better base and with a narrower cone. The net impact is positive but modest, and it is concentrated in two places: GCG (about one-third of the 2025-28 EBITA increase) and self-help (about one-quarter). Organic volume and price together contribute less than half.

The lines, side by side.

Where the 2025-28 EBITA increase (+€448m) comes from. GCG about €150m (about €121m in 2027 plus synergies and growth); self-help net of inflation about €110m (UK and Germany about €50m, productivity net about €60m); organic volume leverage about €120m; price and copper about €60m (almost all in 2026); bolt-ons of 2026 about €30m; less digital investment and large-project dilution about -€25m. Price and copper, the drivers of the last up-cycle, contribute only about 15% of this one. That makes the forecast less dependent on inflation than the 2019-22 path, but it moves the dependence onto two execution items (GCG and the turnarounds) that have no long record yet.

What is different from the last cycle, and why it matters for the third set.

  1. Lower inflation dependence, higher execution dependence. 2019-22 was a 230bp inflation gift; 2026-30 is +80bp of self-help and mix after 2026.
  2. A higher floor. The 2024 trough margin (5.9%) was about 200bp above previous troughs; with GCG and specialties at about 7% of sales and North America above 50%, my bear-case floor is about 6.0% rather than about 5.5%.
  3. More shares, fewer buybacks. EPS lags EBITA by about 4 points in 2027, the reverse of 2022-25.
  4. Bigger cash. FCF per share roughly doubles between 2025 and 2028.
  5. Concentrated, not diffuse, drivers in 2027. GCG consolidation (+6 points of revenue, +24bp of margin) and the copper level (±3 points of growth, ±30-45bp of margin) together account for most of the 2027 change; that concentration is the key fact for D-03.

Memo topic 7, Scope for upgrades over 6 to 12 months: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

A602nd of 38

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Our assessment of Rexel's foundations and forecasts is only partly reflected in current guidance, consensus and the narrative, and the direction of the gap is favourable at the operating line and unfavourable, but one-off and policy-driven, at the earnings-per-share line. Guidance sits below our forecasts at every horizon (FY2026 about 7% and at least 6.4% against our 7.25% and 6.50%; the FY2027 outlook will be set about 1 point of growth and 10-20bp of margin below our base; the above-7% margin target is reachable in 2028, inside the company's window) and the company's record says guidance is a floor in an upswing, so the next three events are more likely to raise than to cut. Consensus is below our EBITA for 2026-28 by 3-4% and above our like-for-like EPS for 2027-28 by 6-8%, because it assumes the French surcharge lapses, carries a pre-placing or partly updated share count, and has not yet absorbed GCG's interest and amortisation; the operating upgrades over the next six months will be real, and the EPS resets that accompany them in February 2027 will be about tax, shares and accounting basis rather than about the business. The narrative articulates the headline dynamics and is silent on the second-order ones; investors, on the evidence of price and flows, are pricing France, dilution and the AI trade and not the operating delivery, which is why 84% of the share's variance over six months has been idiosyncratic and negative while its peers re-rated. For the memo (topics #4, #7 and #8): guidance is conservative and reliably beaten; scope for upgrades to guidance and to operating consensus over 6-12 months is good; momentum in sell-side revisions is strong but will become mixed at the EPS line in February 2027; technical momentum is weak and the narrative is adverse; the probable developments over the next six months (a FY2026 beat, GCG closing, a cautious but beatable FY2027 guide, the French budget resolved) are not fully in the price, and the developments in months 6 to 18 (a July 2027 raise, the first GCG margin, leverage at about 1.9x, a new framework) are not in the narrative at all. The main thing that could make the gap close the wrong way is not in Rexel's control: a copper reversal coinciding with an AI-capex financing shock, which would turn the under-appreciated operating strength into an under-appreciated operating fragility within two quarters.

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

A683rd of 38

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: FY2026 guidance (same-day growth about 7%, adjusted EBITA margin at least 6.4%) will be met or beaten with about 90% probability at the 10 February 2027 results; I expect a small beat (about +7.25% and about 6.50%). The first FY2027 outlook, given the same day, will be set below our base case (I expect same-day growth guided at "low-to-mid single digit" or a 3-5% range, a margin "around 6.7%" or "at least 6.6%" including GCG, with the copper assumption raised only modestly), because management guides on conservative copper and no European recovery and will not guide volume into a year of hard comparisons. Over the following three events (Q1 sales in late April 2027, H1 results in late July 2027, Q3 sales in late October 2027) the probabilities for that FY2027 guidance are: raised at least once about 50%, met as set about 35%, reduced about 15%. The reduction scenario needs copper below about $11,500/t for a quarter, or a hyperscaler capex pause, or a European double-dip; the raise scenario needs only the current run-rate to persist into H1 2027 with copper above $13,000/t. On the mid-term framework (Axelerate 2028: 5-8% sales growth, above 7% margin between 2027 and 2029, FCF conversion above 65%, leverage about 2.0x from end-2027): the margin target is more likely to be met early (2028, about 60%; 2027 about 25%) than late (2029 or later, about 15%); the sales-growth range will be missed in 2028-29 unless bolt-ons resume at 2-3% a year; and a new, higher post-GCG framework (a capital markets day with 2030 targets) is more likely than not within 18 months. Guidance is therefore conservative against our forecasts at every horizon, which is favourable for the case.

  • The events and the base-case path.

  • What would drive each outcome for FY2027.
  • Raised: copper holding above $13,000/t through H1 2027 (each $1,000/t above the guidance assumption is worth about 1 point of growth and 10-15bp of margin); data centres growing above 30% on the Q3 backlog (25% of the US order book); non-cable price holding at or above +1.5% as Schneider and Legrand list-price rises pass through; GCG consolidating at or above 11% from Q1; European energy-transition categories growing above 8%. The raise would most likely come in July 2027, as in 2026, and be incremental ("about 4%" to "about 5%"; "around 6.7%" to "at least 6.8%").
  • Met: the guide set at the floor of our range and H2 2027 decelerating as modelled (core volume about +1.5%, cable price slightly negative); copper $12,500-13,500/t; France flat into and after the election.

  • Reduced: a copper fall to about $11,000/t (removes 30-45bp and about 3 points of growth over two to three quarters; the 2015 precedent is an October cut); a hyperscaler capex pause (New York permitting pause, Illinois incentive suspension and Deutsche Bank's credit-metric warning are the early signs; a backlog reading below 2.8 months would precede it); a European double-dip (ECB at 2.75% in December 2026, Fed hiking into March 2027, Eurozone construction PMI at 43); or GCG integration disruption (ERP or customer loss). Probability of a cut at any 2027 event: about 15%, concentrated in October 2027.

  • Why the initial FY2027 guide will sit below our base. The record is explicit: FY2026 was first guided at 3-5% and about 6.2% when consensus sat at 5.6% and 6.3%; FY2025 at "stable to slightly positive" against a delivered +2.5%; the copper assumption is set below spot every year. Management said on 22 October that Q4 and 2027 data-centre comparisons are tougher, that European electrification will fluctuate and that large projects dilute gross margin: that is the language of a company preparing a cautious guide. Jefferies (22 October) expects a "positive guidance framework" and Bernstein (27 October) models 5.3% organic growth and a 7.2% margin for 2027; a guide at "3-5%" and "around 6.7%" would disappoint those two houses on the day while still being a floor our base clears by about 1 point of growth and 10bp of margin.

  • The secondary horizon (18 to 36 months). (1) Margin above 7% "between 2027 and 2029": reached in 2028 in my base (7.00%), with 2027 possible only if copper stays above $14,000/t and the H2 2027 price-over-cost spread holds; Jefferies and Bernstein have 2027, Morgan Stanley 2028. Likelihood: met 2027 about 25%, 2028 about 60% (cumulative 85%), slips to 2029 or beyond about 15%. (2) Sales growth 5-8% a year, 2-3 points from acquisitions: met in 2027 (about +10.5% reported) and missed in 2028 (about +4%) unless bolt-ons of €400-600m a year resume from 2028; at about 1.9x leverage at end-2027 and 1.7x at end-2028, the capacity exists (€0.5-0.8bn), so a return to 5% is plausible in 2029. (3) Leverage about 2.0x from or by end-2027: met in the base (about 1.9x), missed only in the bear case (about 2.25x); a buyback restart in 2028 follows. (4) A new framework: Axelerate 2028 predates GCG and the data-centre step-change; a capital markets day in H2 2027 or early 2028 with 2030 targets (my expectation: a margin ambition of 7.5% or more, digital above 50%, North America above 55% of sales) is more likely than not (about 55%) within 18 months, and would read as a "raise". (5) Dividend: €1.20 held since 2022; with recurring EPS above €2.60 for 2026 and a 40% floor, a rise to €1.25-1.30 for FY2026 is a coin toss (balance-sheet priority against the optics of a seventh flat year); a rise for FY2027 is likely.

  • The gap between guidance and our assessment, which the score measures. FY2026: guidance about 7% / at least 6.4% against our +7.25% / 6.50%: conservative by a small, known margin. FY2027: the guide will be set about 1 point of growth and 10-20bp of margin below our base, and 1-2 points and 40-50bp below Bernstein. Mid-term: our 2028 margin of 7.00% meets the target inside its window; our 2028-29 sales growth undershoots the range. The direction of surprise is upward at every scheduled event except October 2027, where the comparison base makes a "confirm" the likely tone.

C+4810th of 38

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: the consensus will split along the income statement. Revenue, EBITDA and EBITA estimates for 2026-2028 are likely to be upgraded over the next six months (by about 3-5% for 2026 EBITA and 4-8% for 2027 EBITA), first on the Q3 beat and second when the nine of thirteen brokers who excluded GCG from 2027 consolidate it after closing. Consensus EPS for 2027 is likely to be held within a few percent of today's €3.00 (brokers' basis) rather than upgraded in step, because the operating upgrades will be offset below the line by the French surcharge extension (consensus carries 27.0%; about -3%), the post-placing share count (consensus carries about 305m against about 309m; about -1%, and more for the brokers still on about 293m), GCG's net interest (consensus €267m) and, for the houses that do not add it back, GCG's PPA amortisation (about -6%). The net breadth of EPS revisions, strongly positive now (A on the dashboard; 2027 EPS +7% over six months; buy ratings 71%, +29 points), will stay positive into February 2027 and then turn mixed: EBITA up, EPS flat to down, which is the "composite signal masks component" pattern the first set warned about. Over 18 months, my central expectation for consensus 2027 EPS is "held" (within ±3%), with about 25% probability of a cut of 10% or more (copper or data-centre reversal) and about 25% of an upgrade of 10% or more (European recovery with copper above $15,000/t and the surcharge lapsing). For 2028-29 the consensus is more likely to be upgraded than cut as the above-7% margin and GCG synergies are modelled, but the 2029 figure rests on one broker and is not a consensus.

  • The sequence of revisions. 1. Now to December 2026 (post-Q3): Jefferies (22 October) calls the guidance upgrade "mid-single-digit upgrades to consensus EBITA"; Bernstein (27 October) raised 2027 EPS to €3.47 (its own before-PPA basis) and 2027 margin to 7.2%; Morgan Stanley (26 October) raised 2027 EPS to €2.87 on a 6.7% margin. Expect the FactSet 2026 EPS mean to move from €2.52 to about €2.60, 2027 from €3.01 to about €3.08, and the buy share to hold or rise. Breadth: positive. 2. 10 February 2027 (FY2026 results, FY2027 guide, GCG closed): 2026 actual beats on sales and EBITA; the FY2027 guide is set below consensus organic growth (Bernstein 5.3%, Morgan Stanley 5.8%) but the nine ex-GCG brokers add about €1,050m of revenue and about €120m of EBITA; the tax rate guidance (about 30% if the surcharge is extended) and the first PPA estimate pull EPS down for the brokers that carried 27% and no GCG PPA. Net: revenue and EBITDA consensus up 4-6%; EPS consensus roughly flat (+2% to -3%); target prices up modestly on the operating line. Breadth: mixed, with the EPS revision count possibly negative for the first time since early 2025, while EBITDA revisions stay positive. 3. April to July 2027 (Q1 sales, H1 results): if H1 same-day growth lands at +5.5% and the margin at about 6.55%, a July guidance raise brings a second round of EBITA upgrades (+2-3%) and the EPS consensus rises with it; breadth positive. 4. October 2027 to February 2028: the hard comparison and the European backdrop make this the window for a "hold" or small cut to 2027-28 organic assumptions; the FY2028 guide and a possible new mid-term framework set the 2028-29 trajectory.

  • The dashboard reading and its composition. Sell-side momentum A rests on 2027 EPS +2%, +4%, +7% over one, three and six months, EBITDA +4%, +6%, +10%, sales +4%, +6%, +8% and a buy share of 71% (up 29 points in six months). EBITDA and sales revisions have outrun EPS revisions by 3-4 points, which is the dilution and interest effect; part of the sales and EBITDA revision is GCG consolidation by four brokers and copper, not organic improvement. The A rating is genuine at the operating line and overstated at the EPS line, which is the composite-signal caution in practice. Breadth has been positive for six months; the risk to it is not the business but the February 2027 below-the-line reset.

  • The secondary horizon (2028-29). Consensus 2028 EPS of €3.31 is close to our before-PPA €3.32 and 6% above our after-PPA €3.12. The 2028 EBITA consensus (€1,630m, 6.9%) sits below the company's above-7% target; as GCG synergies (margin from 11% to 13%), the UK and Germany and a 2028 buyback restart are modelled, I expect 2028 EBITA consensus to be upgraded by 3-5% over 18 months and 2028 EPS to be held to slightly up. FY2029 (one broker; EPS €3.81) is not a consensus and should not be quoted as one. A new mid-term framework in H2 2027 or early 2028 would be the trigger for a general upgrade of the 2029-30 numbers.

  • What would make the direction of EPS consensus clearly up or down. Up: the surcharge lapsing in the final December vote (+3%), copper above $15,000/t through H1 2027 (+4-5%), an H1 2027 margin above 6.7% (+3%). Down: copper at $11,000/t (-8-10%), a data-centre pause (-5-7%), a European double-dip (-5%), an enlarged surcharge for French revenue above €3bn as reported by Morgan Stanley on 28 October (-4-6%).

  • The gap the score measures. Our operating forecasts sit above consensus, which supports continued EBITA and EBITDA upgrades and a rising buy share; our EPS forecasts on a like-for-like basis sit below consensus for 2027-28, so the EPS consensus is unlikely to be upgraded in step and could be cut on composition in February 2027. The net gap is modestly favourable: the market will see operating upgrades that are real, and the EPS adjustments that follow are policy- and basis-driven rather than franchise-driven, which sophisticated holders will look through and which the shares, having already fallen 12% on the placing, partly discount.

D-0742
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

D4223rd of 38

Layer 3 · Risks (the third set)

Judgement: over the last six months the share price has moved almost entirely on its own: the equal-weighted basket of dashboard peers explains 16% of Rexel's daily variance (R-squared), the correlation is 0.40 and the beta 0.78, against 59%, 0.77 and 1.2 in mid-2025. Rexel fell 4.7% while the basket rose 20.7%. The de-coupling began in October 2025 (takeover speculation, French politics) and deepened through 2026 (the Q1 miss, GCG, the placing, the AI-trade cooling), so the multiple is now company-driven, and the company-specific items the market is pricing are the negative ones: France, dilution, leverage, data-centre dependence and the placing overhang. For predictability of the share price that is unhelpful (a sector tailwind lifts the stock less); for the investment case it is the opportunity, because a company-driven de-rating reverses on company-driven evidence, of which the next three prints will supply plenty. The 24-month-forward P/E of 9.9x is in the upper third of Rexel's own five-year range but the lower third of the last two years, while the basket has re-rated; the gap to peers is wider than at any point in the two-year series.

  • The numbers. Latest 126-day window (6 May to 28 October 2026): correlation 0.40, beta 0.78, R-squared 16%, idiosyncratic share 84%; six-month return -4.7% against the basket's +20.7%. Month-end windows: R-squared 52-59% from April to September 2025 (beta 1.05-1.23), falling to 37% (October 2025), 21% (December), 6% (February 2026), then 14-23% through 2026. The highest individual correlations now are with Diploma (0.46), Bufab (0.38) and RS Group (0.37), that is, with the short-cycle industrial distributors, and near zero with the chemical distributors (Azelis 0.08, Brenntag 0.07, IMCD 0.16) and Bunzl (-0.06). The basket is the dashboard's sector cohort, not Rexel's business cohort (Wesco, Sonepar, Graybar are not in it), so some of the "idiosyncratic" share is really "electrical-channel" variance that an unavailable basket would capture; the first set's note that electrical and climate wholesalers were the only falling cohort in the sector supports that reading. Even so, the fall from 59% to 16% is too large to be a basket artefact.

  • What drove the de-coupling, in sequence. (1) October to December 2025: the QXO rejection and CVC speculation put a takeover premium into the price (Jefferies, 17 December 2025), moving the stock on deal news rather than on the sector. (2) January to April 2026: the French sovereign spread moved to decade highs and the Q1 miss (same-day +3.4% against 4.3%) hit; the FfF regime (Country France Double Underweight) priced French listings down as a group (SPIE and Elis de-rated with Rexel; Deutsche Bank finds a 0.88 negative correlation between SPIE's forward P/E and the French ten-year yield). (3) September to October 2026: the GCG announcement, the €500m placing at €34.75 (4.6% dilution, buybacks paused) and the cooling of the AI trade produced -11.8% over three months while 2027 EPS was being upgraded; Morgan Stanley (26 October) calls the placing price a technical resistance as placing participants exit. In the same window the basket re-rated on the short-cycle turn (Bossard +52%, Bufab +39%, RS Group +34% over twelve months).

  • The multiple. 24-month-forward P/E 9.94x on 23 October 2026: 73rd percentile of the five-year weekly series (median 9.04x) but 36th of the last two years (median 10.34x); ten-year bands from the dashboard: median 9.3x, 75th percentile 10.5x, 90th 11.8x, 35th 8.6x, 10th 7.3x. Current P/E 11.0x at the 82nd percentile of ten years (the dashboard's valuation F) is inflated by a depressed 2025-26 EPS base (surcharge, dilution, no buybacks) and should be read with that caveat. Against the cohort, Rexel is the lowest P/E in the dashboard sector (11.0x against 12.6-27.5x) and, per Morgan Stanley (26 October), trades at 8.1x 2027 EV/EBITA, a 38% discount to European capital goods, and per Bernstein (28 April 2026) at about a 30% EV/EBITDA discount to Wesco against a 15% historical median. The multiple is therefore company-driven in two senses: its daily moves are idiosyncratic, and its level embeds company-specific penalties (France, leverage, dilution, cyclicality) rather than the sector's re-rating.

  • What this means for the model's return. The Financial Analyst's guideline fair multiple of 8.38x (reached at the 18-month column), below the ten-year "conservative" band of 8.6x, is what produces the model's -1.1% total return to December 2027 despite +12.6% from earnings: the model assumes a further de-rating of about 19%. The co-movement evidence cuts both ways on that assumption. Against it: the stock has already de-rated on its own while peers re-rated, the operating prints have been beats, and the three identified overhangs (placing absorption, French budget, GCG closing) are time-limited. For it: the de-rating is driven by factors (French politics into April-May 2027, a sub-investment-grade balance sheet at 2.3x pro forma, a possible AI-capex financing wobble) that will not be resolved by Rexel's own prints, and the beta of 0.78 means a sector rally would pull Rexel up less than it pulled peers. My reading: an 8.4x multiple is a trough-phase assumption appropriate to a stalling case; the fundamentals here describe a case that is delivering and whose multiple is being set by non-fundamental overhangs; the fair-multiple topic (#11) should weigh that the historical range's "prudent" median (9.3x) is reachable within 18 months if the February 2027 print beats and the French budget passes without enlargement, and that the "crash" band (7.3x) is the plausible bad case if copper and the AI trade turn together.

  • Predictability implication. The share price will respond to Rexel's own news, so the identified milestones (D-05) are also the share-price catalysts. That is good for a case that expects beats, and bad for predictability in the statistical sense: an 84% idiosyncratic share means the position cannot be hedged with the sector and will carry event risk around each print and each French political date.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

B569th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically outperformed and underperformed, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

Judgement: distributors outperform in three conditions: (1) inflation plus restocking (they "win three ways" for 6-18 months); (2) an early short-cycle turn, when cheap, geared names with costs cut win; (3) self-help margin step-ups that the market slowly re-rates. They underperform in deflation plus volume declines, in rising discount rates, and when acquisitions dilute returns. In October 2026 Rexel fits (1) and (3) fundamentally and is mid-way into (2), but the market regime (risk-off, rising yields, France stress) is the condition in which geared distributors underperform. Fundamentals and share price are pulling in opposite directions.

  • Sector rules (sector digest). "Distributors move with factory orders one to two quarters behind". "In a restock with inflation a distributor wins three ways at once, but it is a 6 to 18 month story; generalists' pricing window is 6 to 12 months." "Cost-out from automation lifts thin margins most and stays only where pricing discipline holds." "Favour the engine whose earnings can outrun a de-rating". "In a short-cycle turn the cheap, geared names with costs cut win; premium compounders give the cycle back through multiples when yields rise."
  • Rexel's own history. 2019-22: margin from 5.0% to 7.3%, EPS from €1.21 to €3.09 (Viewforth basis), shares strongly up on inflation. 2023-24: deflation and European construction pulled EPS to €2.02 and margin to 5.9%, with a guidance cut in October 2024. 2025-26: recovery with an earnings beat, upgrades and a 12-month share price gain of +14.7%, but -8% over three months after the placing. Every past downturn showed operating deleverage of 70-100bp on low-single-digit organic declines.
  • Wisdom applied. "Use, refill or price": Rexel's Q2 2026 is about half price. History says the price window for generalists is 6-12 months, so the price leg of the 2026 recovery is likely mid-to-late by H1 2027. "Issue price as technical anchor": shares 6% below the €34.75 placing price, which often acts as resistance. "Composite signal masks component deterioration": the sell-side momentum A is genuine at EPS level (+7% in six months), but sales and EBITDA revisions (+8% and +10%) are partly GCG consolidation and copper, and EPS revisions trail EBITDA revisions because of the dilution and interest. "Sector tailwind plus a self-improving company" fits. "Lazy cyclical extrapolation" warns against extending 2026's price- and data-centre-driven growth into 2027-28.
  • Valuation rule. The re-rating of a channel comes in the multiple, not in price, and only as leverage falls (sector digest). Rexel at 2.4x has to deleverage before the discount (38% against European capital goods; 30% against Wesco, double its 15% ten-year median) can narrow.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

C5214th of 38

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The forward narrative has a credible, mostly incremental staircase: GCG closing and its first numbers, a FY2026 beat with a first 2027 outlook (10 February 2027), margin crossing 7% by 2028-29, deleveraging to about 2x and then buybacks. It has one high-novelty but speculative step (corporate action under an engaged 23% owner). The near-term steps arrive alongside known headwinds (French election in April 2027, fading copper and data-centre comparisons), so the narrative is more likely to improve from H2 2027 than in the next six months. For the fourth set's comparison with market narrative: today's market story is "European construction cyclical with leverage and a placing overhang". The fundamental story built here is "self-help plus North American specialty mix, with earnings rising about 15% a year to 2028". The gap between the two is the opportunity, and rates and France set its timing.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–64

Layer 3 · Risks (the third set)

Judgement: the two earlier sets are congruent on the shape of the case and jar on four specifics, three of which the new evidence settles. They agree that Rexel is a thin-margin, pass-through distributor whose 2026 recovery is about half price, whose 2027 depends on GCG and the copper level more than on volume, whose self-help lifts the floor rather than the ceiling, and whose share price is governed by France, leverage and flows rather than by delivery. That spine is consistent from the external forces through the model. The jarring points were: the size of the 2028 margin (first set 7.0-7.7%, second set 6.95%); the FY2026 revenue composition (both expected a miss; the print says beat); the EPS basis (the second set's inference was wrong); and the 2027 organic growth rate (3.4% in the second set, which the Q3 backlog and volume breadth contradict). On the margin and the organic rate I now sit between the two sets and above the second; on the basis and the revenue headline I correct both. Net: high congruence on the logic, moderate congruence on the levels, and a shared conservatism bias that the Consumer should allow for.

  • Where the sets support each other (the spine). External forces (first set C-01 to C-10): about half of 2026 organic growth is price, data centres about a quarter, core construction flat; 2027 is the test year as copper comparisons, data-centre growth rates and the guidance base fade together. The model (second set P-01, P-04, P-05) built exactly that: organic growth halving in 2027, price drop-through turning negative, GCG and copper as the two 2027 levers. Internal forces (C-11 to C-21) and management (C-55 to C-64) describe a sandbagging-precision CEO delivering a floor; the model treats guidance as a floor and leans FY2026 up. Economic strength (C-49 to C-79) gives the asymmetric operating leverage (35-40bp per point of decline with deflation, 5-6bp per point on the way up) that P-01 measured and that defines the skew in D-04. Risks (C-80 to C-83) and the market regime (C-07, C-82, C-84) say fundamentals and the share price pull in opposite directions; the co-movement table (D-07) now proves it: 84% of the share's variance is idiosyncratic and it fell 4.7% while its peer basket rose 20.7%. This is a coherent case, not a collection of observations.

  • Where they jarred, and how the new evidence resolves each. 1. The margin ceiling. C-78's bridge reached 7.0-7.7% by 2028 and called consensus 6.9% "the low end"; P-03 cut that to 6.95% on the evidence that peers retain little of their productivity. Q3 (+60bp of H2 margin on +9.4% growth, action plans +22bp, data-centre profitability "approaching regional margin levels" per Bernstein on 22 October) says the first set's higher reading was closer on the near term; my 7.00% for 2028 is a compromise that still keeps Morgan Stanley's "savings get competed back" caution for 2029-30. 2. Revenue composition. Resolved by the print (correction (c)). 3. EPS basis. Resolved by the own-2 reconciliations (correction (a)); the second set's conclusion that "consensus 2027 EPS is about 5% too high" survives but for different reasons: tax (surcharge extension, about -3%), share count (about -1% to -5% depending on which share count a broker carries) and GCG PPA (about -6% for brokers who do not add it back), partly offset by EBITA now running above consensus (+4%). 4. 2027 organic growth. The second set's 3.4% was built before the backlog rose to 3.4 months and before volume turned positive in all regions at 6.3%. The first set's "0-7% cone with a 3-5% centre" accommodates my 4.5%. The second set's own quarterly logic (copper comparisons lapping from Q3 2027) is retained in the H1/H2 split.

  • Where a genuine tension remains. The first set's C-20 and C-78 treat AI productivity as a lever worth 30-40bp net by 2029; the second set's P-03 halves the retention from 2028; the AlphaSense D1 report shows 39% of distributors reporting no measurable AI efficiency gain (Morgan Stanley Q3 2026 survey, up from 20%). I side with the second set: the retained AI saving is in the base at about 15-25bp cumulatively, and anything more is upside. The other unresolved tension is C-19/P-02's "about 2.3x pro forma at end-2026" against management's "about 2.0x from or by end-2027": my base reaches about 1.9x at end-2027 and the cone (D-04) runs 1.8-2.3x, so the target is met in the base and missed only in the bear case; the first set's worry that the target is "met with little room" is now less acute because EBITDA is higher.

  • The shared bias. Both sets, and the brokers, underestimated the breadth of the Q3 volume recovery. The pattern across 2026 (Q1 miss, Q2 beat, Q3 large beat, two guidance raises) is that the run-rate has repeatedly outrun careful bottom-up models. The Consumer should treat my 2027 organic figure (4.5%) as a central estimate with a fatter upside tail than the earlier sets' prose implies, while keeping the downside mechanics (deflation at 35-40bp per point) intact.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

B+605th of 38

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the sell-side narrative, as far as the files reach it (Jefferies, Morgan Stanley and Bernstein with their own documents; Barclays, UBS, Oddo, Goldman, CIC and three restricted houses only through FactSet figures), articulates the headline dynamics well (growth composition, copper, GCG accretion, data centres, the French discount, the takeover floor, deleveraging) and articulates the second-order dynamics that drive our forecast differences poorly or not at all: the consensus EPS basis (company recurring against brokers' adjusted, PPA treatment, the post-placing share count), the extent to which GCG's year-one accretion is financial rather than operating, the quarterly deceleration profile through H2 2027, the countercyclical cash cushion, and the sub-investment-grade rating. The French tax surcharge is discussed by three houses and modelled as persisting by one (Bernstein at 28%), yet the consensus tax rate remains 27%, which is the clearest case of a dynamic that is "known but not in the numbers". Investor commentary is not evidenced in the files at all (the I2 report found no Twitter or buy-side material and did not provide the coverage statement the brief required), so the investor narrative must be read from positioning and price: a French cyclical with leverage, dilution and an AI-derivative label, sold on the placing and on French spreads, with operating beats unrewarded (the SPIE pattern Deutsche Bank describes). The dynamics most favourable to the case (volume breadth beyond data centres, the backlog, the margin floor, FCF resilience, the time-limited nature of the overhangs) are known to the three bullish houses and, on the evidence of the price, not believed by investors; the dynamics least favourable (the 2027 EPS basis reset) are known to few. On balance that gap is favourable for a case built on operating delivery.

  • Coverage check (what the evidence actually spans). Houses identifiable from FactSet and the reports: Jefferies (Buy, €45.20), Morgan Stanley (Overweight, €42), Bernstein (Outperform; the I1 report says 9.7x 2027 P/E), Goldman Sachs (Buy, €46), Oddo BHF (Hold, €37), CIC (Buy, €44), Barclays (Underweight/Sell, €30), UBS (Hold, €35, stale from July), Oxcap (Buy, €42.60), three restricted houses (Buy, €40-46), plus Deutsche Bank and Rothschild & Co Redburn at the sector level. The AlphaSense I2 report cites the first three with their own documents and none of the others, so its narrative map covers about three of ten or eleven covering houses, well short of the 80% the brief asked for, and it contains no Sell-case document. The Barclays Sell (€30, 2027 EPS €2.80) and the UBS and Oddo Holds are the views we cannot see; this is my first research request.
