  • The investor narrative, inferred. Three observations. (1) The share fell 11.8% over the three months to late October while 2027 EPS was upgraded 4%, and 4.7% over six months while the dashboard peer basket rose 20.7%: investors are pricing France (OAT-Bund spread at decade highs; election in April-May 2027), the placing, 2.3x pro forma leverage and the cooling AI trade, not the operating delivery. (2) Bernstein (27 October) notes the shares are flat year to date against US peers up more than 30%; Jefferies (17 December 2025) warned that the M&A premium after the Betaville alerts could unwind; at €32.63 the shares are 16% above the QXO level, so part of that premium has already gone. (3) The dashboard's technical momentum D (price below the 50-, 150- and 200-day averages; relative strength percentile 39) alongside sell-side momentum A is the signature of a stock whose owners have been selling into upgrades. The investor frame is "French cyclical with a leverage and dilution overhang and a second-derivative AI exposure"; the fundamental frame built here is "self-help plus a North American specialty mix shift, delivering volume-led growth with a record backlog and a margin floor 200bp above past troughs". The gap between those two frames is the memo's opportunity, and the timing of its closure depends on the French budget and election, GCG's first numbers and the AI-capex financing backdrop more than on Rexel's prints.

  • Secondary horizon (18 to 36 months). The dynamics that matter beyond 18 months (GCG at 13%, the UK and Germany at the European average, a European construction recovery, bolt-ons resuming, buybacks, a 2030 framework) are discussed only in outline by the three houses; Bernstein's 2030 GCG margin path and Morgan Stanley's 13% EPS CAGR to 2028 are the only published long-dated numbers. The idea that Rexel's margin could exceed the 2022 peak of 7.3% in a non-inflationary year is not yet in the narrative, which is where the 2028-30 upside to consensus sits.

  • What is well known, partly known and unknown, in one line each. Well known and in the numbers: copper, data-centre growth, GCG's headline accretion, deleveraging, the takeover floor, the French discount. Known but not in the numbers: the surcharge extension, the H2 2027 deceleration, the placing overhang. Not known: the EPS basis mix and share-count staleness in consensus, the financial character of year-one GCG accretion, the countercyclical FCF, the sub-investment-grade rating, the breadth of the Q3 volume recovery.

Each layer's own material

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 25 October 2026, 17:54 to 18:10 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Rexel (RXL-FR): First set of questions, foundational components of the investment case

Written 2026-10-25 by the Investment Analyst answering the first set, for the Investment Analyst who builds the operating and financial model (second set).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Read this first: conventions, source checks and the facts every answer leans on

Source check (the caution in the instruction). The "latest Earnings Review" does cover the period ending June 2026: it states that Rexel reported H1 2026 results and Q2 2026 sales on 27 July 2026 for the six months to 30 June 2026. The "earlier" Earnings Review, which the script labels 2025 quarter 4, in fact reviews the Q1 2026 sales release of 22 April 2026, and in places it quotes the Q3 2026 sales release of 22 October 2026 (digital at 36% of sales, North American digital at 31%, US industrial automation about +20%, US data centre and broadband about 13% of US sales, and broker headlines "FY guidance upgraded again" from Bernstein and "Significant Acceleration; Guidance Upgrade" from Jefferies, both 22 October 2026). The Q3 2026 print (three days old) is the most important fact the files hold only in fragments. A web search on 25 October 2026 did not return the release. The second-set Analyst should treat the exact Q3 same-day growth and the size of the second 2026 guidance upgrade as unknown and obtain them first. Morgan Stanley's pre-print Q3 forecast was +7.7% same-day.

Bases. Rexel's own headline profit measure is "current adjusted EBITA". It excludes the non-recurring copper effect (the revaluation of cable inventory). FactSet's history line "EBIT (adjusted)" runs about 0.2 percentage points below it: 5.8% against the company's 6.0% in FY2025. Consensus EBIT is on the brokers' basis, close to the company's basis. Earnings per share (EPS) appears on four bases. Rexel's "recurring net income per share" was €2.30 in 2025, the brokers'-basis actual was €2.19, and reported basic EPS was €2.00. Use the financials block's like-for-like consensus growth line, never the history table against the consensus table. FactSet debt includes leases, so FactSet net debt was €4,856m at H1 2026 and FactSet's net debt to EBITDA was 2.98x. The company reports net financial debt of €3,321.9m and leverage of 2.4x net debt to EBITDAaL on its Senior Credit Agreement definition, which excludes leases. Quote the company's 2.4x and 2.0x targets on the company's definition.

The ten numbers the model is built around.

  1. Revenue €19,415m in FY2025. Same-day organic growth was +4.3% in 2023, -2.4% in 2024 and +2.5% in 2025, then +3.4% in Q1 2026, +6.7% in Q2 2026 and +5.1% in H1 2026. FY2026 guidance was raised in July to "about 5%" and, per broker headlines, raised again on 22 October.
  2. Q2 2026 organic growth split: volume +3.1%, cable prices +2.2%, non-cable prices +1.4%. Volumes were positive in all three regions for the first time since Q2 2023.
  3. Current adjusted EBITA margin: 5.0% in 2019, about 7.3% at the 2022 peak, 6.8% in 2023, 5.9% in 2024, 6.0% in 2025 and 6.2% in H1 2026 (+40bp reported, +28bp organic). FY2026 guidance is "at least 6.2%". The mid-term target is above 7% "between 2027 and 2029, without meaningful volume recovery".
  4. H1 2026 margin bridge: operating leverage +17bp, action plans (productivity) +22bp, gross margin -5bp (large data-centre projects), digital investment -10bp. Record productivity of 4% against 2.5% opex inflation. The price-over-cost "delta inflation" spread was a thin +6bp.
  5. Mix: Europe 48% of sales (France 41% of Europe, so about 20% of group), North America 46% (the US 83% of that), Asia-Pacific 6%. North America goes above 50% after GCG. End markets: non-residential 50%, industrial 26%, residential 24%; construction about two-thirds. Cables are 15-16% of sales (about 17% after GCG). US data centres were 9% of US sales in Q2 2026.
  6. GCG: $1.4bn enterprise value; more than $1.1bn of 2026 revenue at about 11% EBITA (13% targeted with synergies); 11.6x EBITA before synergies, below 8x EBITDAaL after. Funded one-third equity (€500m placed on 29 September 2026: 14,388,490 shares at €34.75, 4.6% dilution) and two-thirds debt. Closing in Q4 2026 or Q1 2027. Management says it adds more than 20bp to the group EBITA margin from year one and returns more than the weighted average cost of capital (WACC) by year three.
  7. Cash and balance sheet: free cash flow (FCF) before interest and tax was €814m in FY2025, 66% of EBITDAaL (76.4% excluding the €124m antitrust fine). H1 2026 FCF was €247.3m. Trade working capital was 14.3-15.0% of sales in FY2025 and 16.3% in H1 2026. Net debt €3,321.9m, leverage 2.4x before the GCG cash outflow; target about 2.0x from or by end-2027. Buybacks are paused. Dividend €1.20, held flat since 2022.
  8. Consensus (FactSet median, 25 October 2026): revenue €20,843m, €22,755m and €23,614m for FY2026 to FY2028; EBIT €1,314m, €1,500m and €1,630m (margins 6.30%, 6.59% and 6.90%); EPS €2.53, €3.00 and €3.31 (+15.7%, +18.7% and +10.1% on the brokers' basis); FCF €536m, €742m and €934m.
  9. Price €32.63, 6% below the €34.75 placing price and down 8.0% over three months. 24-month-forward P/E 9.94x against a five-year median of 9.04x (73rd percentile over five years, 36th over two). Dashboard pinned letters: valuation F (P/E 11.0x, 82nd percentile of ten years), sell-side momentum A, technical momentum D.
  10. Sell-side: 2027 EPS revised +2%, +4%, +7% and +7% over 1, 3, 6 and 12 months; EBITDA +4%, +6%, +10% and +11%; sales +4%, +6%, +8% and +9%. Buy ratings at 71%, up 29 points in six months. Mean target €41.91 (+28%).

The single most important framing for the model. Rexel's revenue and margin are driven far more by price and mix than by volume. Nominal growth = volume + non-cable price + cable/copper price + acquisitions − currency. Margin moves with the price-over-cost spread (selling price inflation on a fixed-cost branch base, against 2.5% opex inflation) and with mix (data centres dilute gross margin, GCG raises EBITA margin). The 2019-22 margin rise (5.0% to 7.3%) was an inflation story, and the 2023-24 fall (6.8% to 5.9%) was a deflation-plus-volume story. Model price, copper and mix explicitly; a volume-times-drop-through model will mis-forecast both ways.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Rexel SA (RXL-FR), a France-listed professional distributor of low and ultra-low voltage electrical products and services across Europe (about 48% of 2025 sales), North America (about 46%, rising above 50% after the pending GCG acquisition) and Asia-Pacific (about 6%), serving non-residential (50%), industrial (26%) and residential (24%) end markets, with a growing specialty and engineered-products business in the US (GCG, Talley, Revere, DEE Electronics)

Request: T-P1

Peers: WESCO International (closest listed US electrical distributor; data-centre and utility exposure), Sonepar (largest private global electrical distributor; Rexel's main competitor in France and Germany), Graybar Electric (employee-owned US electrical distributor, publishes annual accounts), Solar A/S (listed Nordic electrical distributor with a comparable branch model), Ferguson Enterprises (listed US distributor with a branch and logistics-hub model and higher margins), RS Group (listed UK distributor running AI and digital cost programmes), Beijer Ref (listed HVAC-R distributor with a branch model and restructuring programmes), Fastenal (listed US distributor whose gross margin trends Morgan Stanley says Rexel's North American gross margin follows) Inputs: What split of fixed and variable costs does Rexel's operating cost base have (Rexel's 2025 annual report states fixed costs are 72% of opex, and personnel and delivery are 68% of opex), and how does that compare with the named peers? How did Rexel's current adjusted EBITA margin move in each past up-cycle and down-cycle (company disclosures: about 5.0% in 2019, about 7.3% in 2022, 6.8% in 2023, 5.9% in 2024, 6.0% in 2025, 6.2% in H1 2026), and how much of each move came from volume, from selling-price inflation or deflation (cable/copper and non-cable), and from cost actions? How did margins respond in 2020 (COVID) and 2024 (European construction downturn with solar and PVC price deflation) at Rexel and at the peers, and how quickly were costs cut? What did Rexel's past cost programmes deliver against their targets and timing (the 2018 turnaround with €82.5m of restructuring costs; branch closures in the UK, Germany and Spain; the 2.2-2.3% headcount reductions of 2024-25; the €40m productivity savings cited by Morgan Stanley; the record 4% productivity reported for H1 2026)? What is the evidence on AI-driven productivity in distribution (Rexel's Revalgo and RexelGPT order-entry and quoting tools, which management says give 10-15% inside-sales productivity; RS Group; Sonepar) and how much of such savings stayed in margin rather than being competed away? What are the UK and Germany operations' revenue and margin today (Jefferies describes about €1bn of combined revenue near breakeven), what restructuring has been done, and what did comparable country turnarounds at peers achieve and how long did they take? Which cost lines (customer-service and inside-sales staff, branch staff, logistics, rent, IT and digital) proved cuttable and which did not at Rexel and peers? How does the operating-leverage response differ between volume changes and price changes for a distributor with Rexel's cost structure? Focus: How asymmetric is Rexel's operating leverage (the margin response to volume compared with the response to price inflation or deflation), and how much of the move to the company's stated above-7% EBITA margin target for 2027-29 can realistically come from cost reduction, productivity and the UK and Germany turnaround without a European volume recovery?

Request: T-P2

Peers: WESCO International (US electrical distributor; reports price contribution quarterly), Sonepar (private; pricing behaviour in France and Germany), Graybar Electric (reported gross-margin pressure from competitive pricing in 2025), Solar A/S (Nordic electrical distributor; copper and cable exposure), Fastenal and W.W. Grainger (US distributors that report pricing and gross margin quarterly), Beijer Ref (distributor that passes refrigerant-regulation price rises through), Legrand and Schneider Electric (key suppliers whose list-price behaviour Rexel passes through) Operating conditions: a sharp rise in copper prices (2021-22 and 2025-26) and a sharp fall in copper prices (for example 2008-09, 2015, 2022); non-cable product deflation (solar panels and PVC in 2023-24); tariff-driven product inflation in the US (Section 232 and Section 301 duties, 2025-26); a construction volume downturn (Europe 2023-26; 2020); large-project and hyperscale data-centre tenders; periods when wage and operating-cost inflation outpaced selling prices Inputs: What selling-price contribution has Rexel achieved by quarter, split between cable and non-cable products (company disclosures: cable +1.7% and non-cable +1.1% in Q1 2026; cable +2.2% and non-cable +1.4% in Q2 2026; non-cable +0.6% and cable +0.7% in 2025), and how did gross margin behave in each period (company: 25.5% in 2023, 24.9% in 2024, 25.1% in 2025, 25.4% in H1 2026)? What has Rexel's "delta inflation" (selling-price inflation minus operating-cost inflation) been each half-year over the last five years (company: +6bp in H1 2026), and what explains the periods when it was negative? How are cable prices indexed to copper, with what lags, and what happens to Rexel's recurring gross margin and to its non-recurring copper effect when copper falls (Jefferies states each $1,000/t change moves organic growth about 100bp and EBITA margin 10-15bp; the company's 2026 guidance assumed $11,000/t)? How fast and how fully does Rexel pass through supplier list-price increases (Bernstein cites pass-through of 3-5% supplier rises), and how does Rexel's non-cable price contribution compare with its suppliers' announced list-price rises? How did Rexel's and peers' prices behave in 2023-24 solar and PVC deflation and in 2020? What share of sales is under framework or contract pricing with escalation clauses compared with spot branch pricing, and what share of spend can large buyers re-tender (an expert cites 5-10% on commodity lines)? Is there evidence that Rexel earns a price premium for digital services, availability or engineered and kitted products (GCG's custom cable assemblies and prefabricated whips), and what do customers, competitors and the sell-side (Bernstein called Q1 2026 pricing "lower in quality" because it was cable-led) say about pricing now? How dilutive are large data-centre projects to gross margin (the company cited 5bp in H1 2026)? Focus: Is Rexel's pricing power its own (able to hold a positive price-over-cost spread when copper and supplier inflation fade) or borrowed from suppliers and commodities, and how would revenue and margin behave in 2027-28 if copper prices fall back toward the company's guidance assumption while wage inflation continues?

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Rexel's 2026 recovery mixes self-reinforcing dynamics (digital adoption, vendor consolidation, acquisition scale, deleveraging toward buybacks) with balancing ones (copper inflation absorbing working capital, data-centre growth diluting gross margin, wage inflation absorbing price), and their net direction decides whether 2026 margins and growth persist into 2027-28. Peers: WESCO International (data-centre growth and working-capital absorption), Sonepar (digital platform Spark and acquisition engine), Ferguson Enterprises (scale and digital in branch distribution), Beijer Ref (private-label and procurement-scale loop after acquisitions), Solar A/S (Nordic distributor's digital and logistics automation), Graybar Electric (US share consolidation) Inputs: Does rising digital penetration (Rexel: 33.7% of sales in H1 2025, 35.3% in H1 2026, 36% in Q3 2026; North America 31%) lower cost to serve and raise customer stickiness and wallet share enough to fund further share gains and purchasing rebates, and what evidence exists at Rexel and peers? Does customer vendor consolidation onto scale distributors (national accounts; utilities' framework agreements; buyers pre-booking scarce equipment) reinforce scale advantages in purchasing and availability? Do bolt-on acquisitions in the fragmented US market (Rexel: 21 acquisitions over 2021-25; Morgan Stanley cites an average of 6.0x EV/EBITDA) create purchasing and density effects that make further acquisitions more accretive? As leverage falls toward the company's stated target of about 2.0x by end-2027 after the GCG acquisition, how have buyback restarts and deleveraging affected distributor valuations historically? Balancing loops: how much does copper and price inflation absorb working capital (Rexel trade working capital rose from about 15% of sales to 16.3% in H1 2026) and reduce cash available for growth; does rapid data-centre growth dilute gross margin and attract more competitive tendering; does wage inflation (Rexel cites 2.5% opex inflation) catch up with productivity gains; do AI productivity gains get competed back to customers as rivals adopt the same tools? Focus: Which of these loops is strongest over the next two to three years at Rexel, and does the net effect make the 2026 margin and growth improvement self-sustaining or self-limiting?

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Consensus EPS growth for Rexel in 2027 rests on several below-EBIT and per-share inputs whose basis is ambiguous (GCG accretion and amortisation treatment, the French tax surcharge, new debt, new shares and the convertible), so the EPS bridge needs evidence input by input. Peers: WESCO International (post-Anixter acquisition EPS bridge, amortisation and deleveraging), Beijer Ref (post-Heritage rights issue and acquisition accretion), Legrand (French-domiciled peer facing the same tax surcharge), Schneider Electric (French-domiciled; tax and currency translation), Sonepar (private; acquisition multiples for comparison) Inputs: GCG: what revenue, EBITA margin, synergies and timing do the company and brokers assume (company: more than $1.1bn of 2026 revenue, about 11% EBITA margin, more than 20bp group margin accretion from year one, returns above WACC by year three; Bernstein: GCG margin to about 13% with synergies; Jefferies: about 3% EPS accretion in year one and about 8% after synergies, assuming full €500m equity), when is closing expected, and how will purchase-price amortisation be treated in reported, recurring and brokers' adjusted EPS? Tax: what is Rexel's normalised effective tax rate, how long does the French corporate tax surcharge apply (Jefferies cites a rise from 27% to 30%), and what do brokers assume for 2027-28? Interest: what is the cost and mix of the new GCG debt, the €400m 4% 2030 notes, the €400m OCEANE convertible due 2031 and the securitisation programme, and what is net interest excluding lease interest? Share count: what is the basic and diluted share count after the September 2026 placing of 14,388,490 shares at €34.75, and what is the OCEANE conversion price and dilution? Capital returns: when do management and brokers expect buybacks (previously €50-150m a year) to resume, and what dividend path is expected (dividend policy of at least 40% payout; €1.20 for 2025)? Currency: what translation effect do brokers assume (company: a 5% currency move changes sales by €507.4m and EBITA by €32.2m; the US dollar is 38% of sales)? Copper: what copper price do brokers assume for 2027? What are the one-off items to expect (restructuring, the €48m judicial cash guarantee, the antitrust appeal)? Focus: Reconcile consensus 2027-28 EPS for Rexel input by input (EBIT, interest, tax, amortisation, share count) and identify which inputs carry most uncertainty and in which direction.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 26 October 2026, 06:05 to 06:22 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Rexel (RXL-FR): Second set of questions, the operating and financial model

Written 26 October 2026 by the Investment Analyst answering the second set, for the Investment Analyst answering the third and fourth sets (predictability of the forecasts, and how far they are reflected in guidance, consensus and the market narrative).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Read this first: what the model rests on, the bases, and what I could not see

The one unknown that matters most. The Q3 2026 sales release (22 October 2026) is still only visible in fragments: digital 36% of sales, North American digital 31%, US industrial automation about +20%, US data centres plus broadband about 13% of US sales, a group currency effect of -1.4%, and broker headlines "FY guidance upgraded again" (Bernstein) and "Significant Acceleration; Guidance Upgrade" (Jefferies). The Q3 group same-day growth rate and the new FY2026 guidance are not in the files. One AlphaSense report (operating leverage, §3) says Q3 same-day sales "grew by 11.4%". I do not accept that as the group figure: 11.4% is exactly Asia-Pacific's Q1 2026 growth rate, and the same report family repeatedly mis-attributes regional figures. My model assumes H2 2026 same-day growth of about +8% (Q3 about +8.5%, Q4 about +7.5%), consistent with Morgan Stanley's pre-print +7.7% and the "significant acceleration" headline. The third-set Analyst should replace this with the actual Q3 print and the new guidance before anything else; every 1 point of H2 same-day growth moves FY2026 revenue by about €100m and FY2026 EBITA by about €10-15m.

Bases used throughout (and why they matter for the consensus comparison).

  • Margin. All my margins are on Rexel's "current adjusted EBITA" basis: before amortisation of acquired intangibles (purchase-price allocation, PPA), before the non-recurring copper effect, before restructuring and other items. Consensus EBIT (6.30% in 2026) is on this basis. FactSet's history line "EBIT (adjusted)" is not: it equals reported EBITA including the copper effect less PPA amortisation (H1 2026: €614.8m adjusted EBITA + €20.7m copper effect - about €23m PPA = FactSet's €612m; FY2025: €1,165m - about €47m PPA ≈ FactSet's €1,118m).
  • EBITDA. My EBITDA = adjusted EBITA + depreciation and amortisation excluding PPA (IFRS 16, before lease costs). It is close to FactSet's operating EBITDA less the copper effect.
  • EPS. My earnings per share are on the company's "recurring net income per share" basis (€2.30 in FY2025), which I infer is before PPA amortisation. FactSet's "brokers'-basis actual" for FY2025 (€2.19) appears to be after PPA amortisation: €1,165m EBITA - €47m PPA - €218m net financial items = €900m, taxed at about 28.7% = €642m, over 293m shares = €2.19; the same arithmetic without the PPA deduction gives €2.29-2.30. Jefferies states its 2027-28 adjusted EPS (€3.16, €3.50) excludes PPA and that nine contributors use the same definition. So consensus "like-for-like" EPS growth of +15.7% in 2026 is partly a basis change: on the company's recurring basis, consensus 2026 EPS of €2.53 is +10%, not +16%. This is an inference with moderate confidence; it should be verified (research request OWN-2).
  • Debt. Company net financial debt excludes leases (€3,321.9m at June 2026, 2.4x net debt to EBITDAaL); FactSet's includes about €1.5bn of lease liabilities (€4,856m, 2.98x). EBITDAaL = EBITDA less lease payments (principal and interest), about €270-290m a year.
  • Free cash flow. My FCF is operating cash flow after interest and tax, less capital spending, less lease principal repayments, before acquisitions, dividends and buybacks. FactSet's history "free cash flow" line is a trap: it deducts dividends (FY2025: €796m operating cash flow - €146m capex - €355m dividends = €296m; the same identity holds in every year 2019-2025). Consensus FCF (€536m, €742m, €934m) is consistent with my after-leases definition, not with FactSet's history line.

The base case in one paragraph. Revenue €20.4bn in 2026 (+5.2% reported; same-day about +6.6%), €22.3bn in 2027 (+9.2%, of which GCG and the 2026 bolt-ons about +6 points and organic about +3.4%), then +4.0%, +4.5% and +4.5% to €25.3bn in 2030. Adjusted EBITA margin 6.36%, 6.68%, 6.95%, 7.10%, 7.20%. Recurring EPS €2.55, €2.84, €3.20, €3.52, €3.81 (CAGR 2025-30 about 10.6%). FCF after leases €555m, €735m, €910m, €975m, €1,025m. Net debt to EBITDAaL about 2.3x pro forma at end-2026, 2.0x at end-2027, 1.8x at end-2028. Against consensus: in line for 2026 (EPS €2.55 against €2.53), about 5% below for 2027 (€2.84 against €3.00) and 3% below for 2028 (€3.20 against €3.31). The 2027 gap is not operating: my 2027 EBITA margin (6.68%) is slightly above consensus (6.59%). It is tax (I assume the French surcharge is partly extended in 2027, about -3% of EPS against consensus), share count (309m against the 305m implied by consensus, -1.4%) and a lower 2026 revenue base (-0.7%).

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: Financial forecasts

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

Rexel reports three regions. The files give revenue shares (FY2025: Europe 48%, North America 46%, Asia-Pacific 6%) and one segment margin (Europe 6.3% adjusted EBITA in H1 2026). The segment margins below are my estimates, built to reconcile to the group figures, and carry about ±50bp of uncertainty; the third-set Analyst should check them against the H1 2026 segment note.

Europe about 9,320 9,490 / about 6.5% 9,730 / about 6.7% 10,070 / about 7.0% 9,623 / 10,029 / 10,330
North America (GCG from FY2027) about 8,930 9,700 / about 7.1% 11,290 / about 7.7% 11,790 / about 7.9% 10,012 / 11,432 / 11,948

Group 19,415 / 6.0% 20,428 / 6.36% 22,312 / 6.68% 23,204 / 6.95% 20,843 / 22,755 / 23,614

Europe: same-day growth about +2.8% in 2026 (H1 +2.6% with Q2 +4.4%), less about 1 point of scope from the Finland disposal; +2.5% in 2027 and +3.5% in 2028 as French and German renovation support (France's €10bn a year electrification plan; Germany's Building Modernisation Act from 2027) and a slow construction recovery arrive; margin rises with the UK and Germany turnaround (about €1bn of revenue near breakeven). North America: same-day about +8.5% in 2026 (data centres +80-100%, automation +15-20%), the 2026 bolt-ons (Revere about $330m of sales, TC 360 about C$85m, DEE) and currency -3% in 2026; GCG (more than $1.1bn of 2026 revenue at about 11% EBITA, 13% targeted with synergies) consolidated from 1 January 2027 adds about €1,050m in 2027 and about 60bp to the region's margin. North America becomes about 51% of group revenue in 2027. Asia-Pacific: volatile, solar- and battery-subsidy driven in Australia; low margin.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

Revenue. Reported growth = same-day organic + scope + currency (calendar assumed nil).

Period Same-day organic of which cable price non-cable price US data centres other volume Scope Currency Reported

Copper: about $14,000/t in H2 2026, about $13,700/t flat thereafter (Morgan Stanley's 2027 forecast $6.20/lb; Jefferies' $6.50/lb); each ±$1,000/t moves organic growth about ±1 point and margin ±10-15bp. The H2 2026 same-day rate (+8.0%) is an assumption: the Q3 2026 release is not in the files (Morgan Stanley's pre-print forecast was +7.7%; Jefferies' headline "Significant Acceleration"). Scope: H2 2026 includes Revere, TC 360 and DEE less the last two months of Finland; 2027 includes GCG (about €1,050m) and the first-half carry-over of the 2026 bolt-ons (about €190m); 2028-30 include bolt-ons funded by the acquisition spending in Part 2. GCG is assumed to close around 31 December 2026 (the CFO said closing is "on track for year-end 2026"); if it closes in Q1 2027, FY2027 revenue falls by about €80-250m and FY2026 is unchanged.

Margin (company adjusted EBITA basis, before PPA amortisation and the non-recurring copper effect). 6.36% (2026: H1 6.15% actual, H2 6.55%), 6.68% (2027: H1 6.50%, H2 6.85%), 6.95%, 7.10%, 7.20%. 2027 bridge: volume leverage +17bp, action plans and UK/Germany +20bp, price-over-cost spread -10bp (price about 1.5% against opex inflation about 2.6%), digital investment -10bp, large-project dilution -5bp, GCG mix +24bp, synergies and bolt-on carry-over +1-3bp. 2028-30: self-help net +10-15bp a year, GCG synergies to 13% (+5bp a year to 2029), volume leverage +10bp a year, less price-over-cost and digital about -8bp a year. EBITDA margin = adjusted EBITA margin + D&A (excluding PPA) as a share of revenue (2.0-2.1%). D&A rises with GCG (about €20m), the 2026 bolt-ons, automated logistics centres (10 to 20) and leased hubs.

Below EBITDA. Net financial items include lease interest (about €74m in 2026 rising to about €89m by 2030); interest on borrowings rises by about €32m in 2027 on the €800m of GCG debt at about 4%; the €400m OCEANE costs about 3.5-4% in the P&L (accretion of the €347.2m debt component plus the 1% coupon). Tax: recurring rate 28.5% in 2026 with the French surcharge, 28.0% in 2027 (surcharge partly extended: the draft 2027 Finance Bill of 1 October 2026 proposes an extension), 27.0% in 2028, 26.5% from 2029 as US profit passes 50% of the group. Minorities about €2m a year (not an input). Share count: basic shares outstanding about 294.1m at end-2025 (293.2m on FactSet), 311,385,744 issued from 1 October 2026 after the placing of 14,388,490 shares at €34.75, of which about 3m are held in treasury; the "other" change is free-share deliveries from treasury (about 0.5-0.8m a year). The OCEANE (conversion price €50.314, about 2.68% of capital, due May 2031) is not assumed to convert.

Cash flow and balance sheet. Capital spending about 0.8-0.9% of revenue (FY2025 €146m; H1 2026 €70.2m), raised slightly for logistics automation and GCG's assembly sites. Working capital (FactSet definition: receivables plus inventories less payables) 14.8% of LTM revenue at end-2025 and 16.7% at June 2026 (seasonal plus copper); 15.3% at end-2026, easing to 14.9% by 2029 as copper stops rising. Note for the model: about €150m of the FY2027 working-capital increase (and about €60m in FY2026) is acquired with GCG and the bolt-ons and is paid for within the acquisition price; if the model deducts the full change in working capital as cash, FY2027 free cash flow will be understated by about €150m. Lease payments are the principal repayments only (lease interest is in net financial items), so EBITDA less lease principal less lease interest equals EBITDAaL (about €1,420m in 2026, €1,620m in 2027). Acquisitions: FY2026 = H1 actual €394m + about €30m of further bolt-ons + GCG €1,200m ($1.4bn enterprise value); FY2027 €50m (earn-outs and small deals); bolt-ons resume from 2028 as leverage falls below 2x. Buybacks less new shares: FY2026 = €500m placing (gross) less €15.2m of buybacks in H1 2026; buybacks paused through 2027 and resuming at €100m, €150m and €200m in 2028-30 (the pre-GCG programme was €50-150m a year). Dividends: €1.20 for FY2026 (unchanged since FY2022; balance-sheet priority), then about 47% payout. Resulting net financial debt (company definition, excluding leases): about €3,570m at end-2026 (about 2.3x EBITDAaL pro forma for GCG), about €3,255m at end-2027 (about 2.0x), about €3,060m at end-2028 (about 1.75x).

Not in the base case. Any recovery of the €124m antitrust fine on appeal (upside); loss of the €48m judicial cash guarantee (downside); the non-recurring copper effect (gain at rising copper, loss at falling copper); GCG transaction, integration and restructuring costs (about €45m in 2026 and 2027, €30m a year after) are excluded from EBITA but included in my free-cash-flow cross-check.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  1. Revenue. The company guides on same-day organic growth (constant scope, currency and working days); the model and consensus use reported revenue. FY2026 consensus revenue (€20,843m, median of 10 brokers) is about €400m above my €20,428m; the dispersion (Jefferies €20,501m, UBS €20,451m and one restricted broker €20,271m against €20.8-21.0bn for the rest) suggests most brokers consolidate part of GCG in Q4 2026. FY2027 consensus (€22,755m) is +9.2% on that higher base; my FY2027 is +9.2% on mine.
  2. Operating profit. Company "current adjusted EBITA" excludes the non-recurring copper effect, PPA amortisation and other income and expenses. Consensus EBIT (€1,314m, 6.30% in 2026) is on this basis. FactSet's history "EBIT (adjusted)" is reported EBITA including the copper effect less PPA amortisation (H1 2026: €614.8m + €20.7m - about €23m = €612m). FactSet's operating EBITDA history includes the copper effect; my EBITDA margins exclude it.
  3. EBITDA. Consensus EBITDA margins (8.3%, 8.6%, 8.9%) are on an IFRS 16 basis before lease costs; the FY2025 brokers'-basis actual (€1,501m, 7.7%) is inconsistent with the forecast years (consensus EBITDA less EBIT implies D&A of about €420-470m, against about €336m in the 2025 actual), so read the trajectory, not the level. EBITDAaL (company, for leverage and cash conversion) = EBITDA less lease payments (about €290m in 2026).
  4. EPS. Company "recurring net income per share" (€2.30 in FY2025) is, on my reconciliation, before PPA amortisation at a recurring tax rate of about 28.5%. FactSet's brokers'-basis actual for FY2025 (€2.19) reconciles only if PPA amortisation (about €47m) is deducted, so it appears to be after PPA. Jefferies states its adjusted EPS excludes PPA and that nine contributors do the same. Consequently consensus FY2026 EPS of €2.53 is about +10% on the company's recurring €2.30, not the +15.7% FactSet shows against €2.19. My cross-check EPS below is on the recurring (before-PPA) basis, basic shares; deduct about €0.12 (2026) and €0.20 (2027 onwards) for the after-PPA basis, and about 2.5% for IFRS diluted EPS (OCEANE if-converted).
  5. Net debt. Company net financial debt excludes lease liabilities (€3,321.9m at June 2026; 2.4x EBITDAaL on the Senior Credit Agreement definition); FactSet's includes about €1.5bn of leases (€4,856m; 2.98x EBITDA).
  6. Free cash flow. Company FCF "before interest and tax" is measured after lease payments and against EBITDAaL. FactSet's history "free cash flow" line deducts dividends (operating cash flow less capex less dividends paid, in every year 2019-2025), so it is not comparable with anything. Consensus FCF (€536m, €742m, €934m) and my cross-check are after interest, tax, capex and lease principal, before acquisitions, dividends and buybacks.
  7. Tax. Consensus FY2026 implies about 30% on pre-PPA profit; consensus FY2027 implies about 26%, i.e., the French surcharge lapsing. My inputs assume a partial extension in 2027.

Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 30 October 2026, 08:30 to 08:51 UK, Fable, richie login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Rexel (RXL-FR): Third and fourth sets of questions

Written 2026-10-29 by the Investment Analyst answering the third set (the predictability of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months) and the fourth set (how far our assessment is reflected in guidance, sell-side estimates and the market narrative), for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo for Richard.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Read this first: what the Q3 2026 print changed, the corrections I make to the earlier sets, and the forecast of record after my changes

1. The one unknown the second set flagged is now known, and it is stronger than either earlier set assumed. The Q3 2026 sales release and call of 22 October 2026 (AlphaSense own-1 report, citing the press release, presentation and call transcript) gave: group same-day sales +11.4% in Q3 (the highest quarterly rate since 2022; consensus was +6.6%), split volume +6.3%, cable price +2.9%, non-cable price +2.2%; reported sales €5,452.9m, +14.6%, with currency +1.2% (the dollar turned from headwind to tailwind), calendar +0.6% and net scope +1.2% (acquisitions +2.1%, Finland disposal -0.9%). Nine months: same-day +7.3% (volume +3.4%, cable +2.3%, non-cable +1.6%). By region: North America +17.4% same-day (US +18.2%, Canada +13.4%), primarily volume, with data centres now about 13% of US sales growing at triple digits and US industrial automation about +20%; Europe +4.5% (France +3.6% against a 5.3% consensus, Germany +6.4%, Benelux +7.5%, energy-transition categories +13%); Asia-Pacific +15.8%. The North American backlog exceeded $3bn at end-September, 3.4 months of sales (2.7 months at December 2025) and 8.3 months of project activity, with data centres about 25% of it, "no signs of pre-buying" and no project delayed or cancelled. FY2026 guidance was raised a second time: same-day growth "about 7%" (from about 5%), current adjusted EBITA margin "at least 6.4%" (from at least 6.2%), FCF conversion above 65%, with the $11,000/t copper assumption unchanged and the copper inventory effect excluded. Management declined to guide 2027 (first outlook on 10 February 2027) but said: tougher data-centre comparisons in Q4 and 2027; most of the backlog converts in 2027; European data-centre projects start to contribute but "will not move the needle"; subsidy-driven European electrification demand will fluctuate; supplier price rises of about 3% are passed through with a lag on large projects; large projects carry lower gross margins than the proximity business. GCG remains on track to close by year-end 2026.

What the guidance arithmetic says. "About 7%" for the year, with nine months at +7.3%, implies Q4 same-day growth of only about +6% (Q4 2025 base about €4.88bn). Given Q3 at +11.4%, a backlog that rose from 2.7 to 3.4 months, copper above $14,000/t against a year-ago base near $10,000/t and the sandbagging-precision record both earlier sets documented, I expect Q4 at about +7.5% (cable price contribution fading to about +2 points, volume about +4 points) and FY2026 same-day growth at about +7.25%, a small beat of the raised guidance. "At least 6.4%" for the margin implies H2 at least 6.63% against H1's 6.15%; I expect H2 at about 6.83% and FY2026 at 6.50% (H2 2025 was about 6.2%, so +60bp year on year on about +9.4% same-day growth, a drop-through of about 6-7bp per point, slightly above H1's 5.5bp because the volume leg is larger).

2. The inputs I change (recorded in inputs-changed.md). The second set's H2 2026 same-day assumption of +8.0% (Q3 +8.5%, Q4 +7.5%) is replaced by about +9.4% (Q3 +11.4% actual, Q4 +7.5%), and its H2 currency assumption of -1.2% by about +1.0% (Q3 actual +1.2%). FY2026 reported revenue growth therefore rises from +5.2% to +7.1% (about €20,785m against €20,424m), and the FY2026 EBITDA margin from 8.37% to 8.48% (adjusted EBITA margin 6.50%, from 6.36%). For 2027 I raise same-day organic growth from +3.4% to +4.5% (H1 about +5.5% on an easy H1 2026 comparison and a 3.4-month backlog; H2 about +3.5% as copper and data-centre comparisons bite), set currency at nil (the dollar is now a tailwind and the dollar share of sales passes 50% with GCG), and keep the GCG and bolt-on scope at about +6.0 points on the higher base; reported growth therefore rises from +9.2% to +10.5% (about €22,970m). The 2027 adjusted EBITA margin rises from 6.68% to 6.80% (EBITDA margin 8.75%): the organic bridge adds about +18bp (volume leverage +20bp, action plans and the UK and Germany +20bp, price-over-cost -7bp with non-cable price now running at about +1.7% against opex inflation of about 2.6%, digital investment -10bp, large-project dilution -5bp) and GCG's mix adds about +22bp on the larger base. 2028 rises from 6.95% to 7.00% (the above-7% target reached in 2028, inside the company's 2027-2029 window); 2029 and 2030 to 7.15% and 7.25% (EBITDA margins unchanged at 9.07-9.15% because depreciation falls as a share of the higher revenue). The 2027 tax rate rises from 28.0% to 29.0%: on 13 October 2026 the French Finance Committee adopted a one-year extension of the exceptional corporate surcharge into 2027 (KPMG, 13 October 2026), so a full extension is now the base case rather than a partial one; 2028 rises from 27.0% to 27.5% to carry a residual risk of a further extension after the April-May 2027 election. All other inputs stand.

*The company's FY2025 recurring EPS of €2.30 is after PPA amortisation and before the surcharge (see correction (a) below), so it is not exactly the model's basis; the model's 2025 base on its own basis would be about €2.38.

4. Four corrections to the earlier sets.

(a) The EPS basis map was wrong in the second set, and the right map changes the reading of consensus. The second set inferred that Rexel's "recurring net income per share" (€2.30 in FY2025) is before PPA amortisation and that FactSet's brokers'-basis actual (€2.19) is after it. The AlphaSense own-2 report shows the company's reconciliations: FY2025 reported net income €591.4m, less the copper effect €5.5m, plus other income and expenses €56.2m, plus "tax impact and non-recurring tax effects" €36.4m, equals recurring net income €678.5m; H1 2026: €342.1m, -€20.7m, +€7.7m, +€17.6m = €346.6m. The tax add-back is far larger than the tax effect of the items adjusted (about €13m in 2025 and about -€3m in H1 2026), and the difference matches the French exceptional contribution (€23.5m in 2025; €14.4m in H1 2026). The report also states that recurring net income does not add back PPA amortisation. So: company recurring EPS (€2.30) is after PPA amortisation and before the French surcharge; the brokers' basis (€2.19) is after PPA amortisation and after the surcharge ((678.5 - 23.5)/295.1m shares = €2.22, within rounding of €2.19-2.20); and the Financial Analyst's model basis is before PPA amortisation and after the surcharge. Two consequences. First, consensus FY2026 EPS of €2.53 is on a like-for-like after-surcharge basis with €2.19, so the +15.7% FactSet shows is real growth, not a basis change (the second set's "+10%" was wrong). Second, consensus FY2027 EPS of €3.00 at a 27.0% tax rate (company-compiled consensus, 15 October 2026) assumes the surcharge lapses; it does not, in my base case. To move from the model's EPS to the brokers' basis, deduct about €0.11 in 2026 and about €0.20 from 2027 (legacy PPA about €47m plus GCG PPA about €40m a year, tax-effected, over about 309m shares), as the model summary already says.

(b) The covenant doubt is resolved, and the rating is not investment grade. The Senior Credit Agreement (€900m revolver to January 2031) requires net debt to EBITDA below 3.50x, tested on 30 June and 31 December, with permitted excursions to 3.75x on two dates and 3.90x on one; leases are excluded and on-balance-sheet securitisation (€1,354.5m drawn) is included; the ratio was 2.39x at June 2026 (own-5 report, citing the H1 2026 interim report). There is no covenant constraint in any plausible path. However, Rexel is rated BB+ (S&P) and Ba1 (Moody's), stable outlooks, that is, the top speculative grade, not investment grade as the first set and several broker notes imply; the revolver margin steps from 0.30% to 1.40% with ratings. The €500m placing was sized to hold those ratings.

(c) The expected FY2026 "revenue miss" headline will not happen. Both earlier sets expected FY2026 consensus revenue (€20,843m) to be €200-400m too high because some brokers consolidate GCG in Q4. The Q3 print shows reported growth of +14.6% with currency turning positive, so FY2026 revenue reaches about €20.8bn without GCG; consensus was about right for a different reason. The 10 February 2027 headline will be a beat on sales and EBITA, not a miss on sales.

(d) The earlier sets were too conservative on the current run-rate, and that bias should be named. The first set put H2 2026 organic growth at 6-8%; the second set at 8.0%; Q3 delivered 11.4%, with volume at 6.3% in every region. The direction of both sets' calls (a peak rate of change in H2 2026, deceleration through 2027) still holds, and the composition (price about 45% of growth, data centres about a quarter) is as they described, but the level of volume growth outside data centres was underestimated: US sales ex data centres grew at roughly +11% in Q3 (about +6% in volume), not "slightly negative" as in H1. I have raised 2027 organic growth, but kept it below Morgan Stanley (5.8%) and Bernstein (5.3%), because the H2 2027 comparison is now very demanding.

5. The gaps the model summary flags between the second set's cross-check and the model's figures. FY2027 free cash flow: the Analyst's €735m against the model's €630.5m (-€105m). The cause is mechanical, not an input error: the model applies the working-capital percentage (15.2%) to total FY2027 revenue, so GCG's roughly €150m of acquired working capital, which arrives with the consolidation and is paid for inside the FY2026 acquisition outflow of €1,624m, is deducted a second time as an FY2027 cash outflow; the organic absorption the second set modelled (about €115m) plus that double count reproduces the gap within rounding. I do not change the working-capital input, because lowering the FY2027 percentage would only move the artefact into FY2028, and raising the FY2026 percentage to include GCG's balance would move it into FY2026, where the comparison with consensus FCF (€536m) matters for the February 2027 narrative. The Financial Analyst should exclude the acquired balance from the FY2027 working-capital change; failing that, the Consumer should read the model's FY2027 free cash flow as about €150m too low. The same effect is about €60m in FY2026 from the 2026 bolt-ons. The smaller gaps (FY2029 and FY2030 FCF 2-3% apart; EPS within 1.5% in every year) come from the model's treatment of restructuring and integration cash (the Analyst deducts about €30-45m a year, the model does not) and from the conversion of the €500m placing into shares (14.39m at €34.75); neither shows an input is wrong.

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: Risks (the third set) · Group: Guidance (the fourth set) · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

Appendix The share-price co-movement table the Investment Analyst was given

Co-movement of RXL-FR with its dashboard peers (computed 2026-10-28 from the master dashboard's daily prices)

What this is: daily close-to-close returns over rolling windows of 126 trading days (about six months). Correlation: how closely the daily moves line up (1 = always together, 0 = unrelated). Beta: how far the stock moves for a 1% move in the basket. R-squared: the share of the stock's daily price variance the peer basket explains; the remainder is the stock's own (idiosyncratic) movement. Basket: the peers below, equal-weighted. Prices only; the multiple's own history is in the financials block.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for the memo

Research requests for the next set

Company: Rexel SA (RXL-FR), a France-listed professional distributor of low and ultra-low voltage electrical products and services, reporting three regions: Europe (about 48% of 2025 sales; France about 20% of the group), North America (about 46%, rising above 50% after the pending acquisition of GCG, a US specialty infrastructure and engineered-products platform) and Asia-Pacific (about 6%); end markets non-residential (50%), industrial (26%) and residential (24%).

Request: OWN-1

Question: What are the arguments, estimates and target prices of the brokers that rate Rexel SA (RXL-FR) Sell, Underweight or Hold (Barclays, Sell with a €30 target on FactSet as of 23 October 2026; UBS, Hold with a €35 target dated 28 July 2026; Oddo BHF, Hold with a €37 target dated 23 October 2026), how do their 2027 and 2028 assumptions on organic growth, adjusted EBITA margin, tax rate, share count, net financial expense, treatment of purchase-price amortisation, inclusion of GCG and valuation method differ from those of the Buy-rated houses (Jefferies, Morgan Stanley, Bernstein, Goldman Sachs, CIC, Oxcap and the unnamed restricted contributors), and how did every house covering Rexel change its estimates, rating and target price in the weeks after the Q3 2026 sales release of 22 October 2026 (same-day sales growth of 11.4% in the quarter; FY2026 guidance raised to about 7% same-day growth and an adjusted EBITA margin of at least 6.4%) and after the French National Assembly's votes on the 2027 Finance Bill's extension of the exceptional corporate-tax contribution? Name every covering house and analyst, say how many of them AlphaSense holds research for and how many are covered with their own documents, and credit a view to a house only when citing that house's own document.

Request: OWN-2

Question: What do investors say and do about Rexel SA (RXL-FR): the changes in its shareholder register since the placing of 14,388,490 new shares at €34.75 on 29 September 2026 (who took the placing, which institutions have increased or reduced positions, any statements or filings by Cevian Capital, which holds about 23% and has a board seat), short interest and securities-lending data, fund-flow and positioning data for French-listed mid-caps and for electrical and industrial distributors, buy-side and conference feedback reported by brokers, and retail, social-media and independent commentary (Twitter/X, Reddit, Substack, investor forums, podcasts, investor letters) over the twelve months to November 2026; and how does the investor narrative compare with sell-side commentary on each of the following: the French corporate-tax surcharge and the April-May 2027 presidential election, the GCG acquisition (enterprise value about $1.4bn, announced 25 September 2026) and its debt-and-equity financing, US data-centre exposure and the risk of a pause in hyperscaler capital spending, copper prices, the takeover interest (QXO's rejected proposal at €28-28.40 a share in September 2024; reported CVC interest in December 2025), the paused share buyback, and the €34.75 placing price as a technical level?

Request: OWN-3

Question: What has driven the changes in Rexel SA's (RXL-FR) valuation multiples (12- and 24-month-forward price-to-earnings, EV/EBITA and EV/EBITDA, in absolute terms and relative to WESCO International, to European capital goods and to French mid-caps) over the last ten years, phase by phase (the 2016-2018 restructuring and Cevian's arrival; the 2020-2022 inflation and record-margin period; the 2023-2024 deflation and the October 2024 guidance cut; the QXO proposal of September 2024; the 2025-2026 recovery, the GCG acquisition and the €500m placing of 29 September 2026); what multiple did the market pay in each phase of delivery (trough, turning up, peak delivery, stalling); what explains the current discount (Morgan Stanley, 26 October 2026: 8.1x 2027 EV/EBITA, a 38% discount to capital goods peers; Bernstein, 28 April 2026: an EV/EBITDA discount to WESCO of about 30% against a historical median of about 15%); what precedents exist for distributors re-rating as leverage fell after a large debt-and-equity-funded acquisition (WESCO after Anixter from 2020; Beijer Ref after Heritage from 2023; Ferguson after FloWorks in 2026), how long each re-rating took and what triggered it; and how did French-listed industrials with similar domestic exposure (SPIE, Elis, Legrand, Schneider Electric) re-rate or de-rate in past episodes when French sovereign spreads widened and then narrowed?
